P(\mathbf{q},t)
is close to the stationary distribution
P^{\rm ss}\propto\exp(-\Phi(\vb{q}))
(
41
), these notions of entropy and the second law (
72
) closely resemble those of dissipative EFTs for thermal fluids
Glorioso and Liu 2016
. For this reason—and others discussed below—we believe this definition of entropy to be a logical starting point for generic, stochastic EFTs. However, we caution that the definition of entropy we recover may differ from that typically associated with entropy production in active and nonequilibrium dynamics
Schnakenberg 1976
;
Tomé and de Oliveira 2012
. Additionally, we restrict the derivation below to Gaussian noise (either additive or multiplicative) for simplicity. We conclude this discussion by commenting on the extension of this notion of entropy for nonequilibrium EFTs beyond Gaussian noise.

We can work out the “correct” choice of the entropy
D[P]
for a probability distribution
P(\mathbf{q},t)
(
33
) in a pedagogical way. The most important property that
D[P]
must satisfy is that
D_{\rm max}=D[P^{\rm ss}]
—i.e., that the stationary distribution
P^{\rm ss}
(
41
)
uniquely
maximizes the entropy
D
. A natural guess for the entropy density
s(\mathbf{q},t)
for the probability distribution
P(\mathbf{q},t)
(
33
) is the standard one
s(\mathbf{q},t)=-P(\mathbf{q},t)\,\log P(\mathbf{q},t)
, such that the global entropy

D_{\rm Shannon}(t)\,=\,-\int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{q}\,P(\mathbf{q},t)\,\log P(\mathbf{q},t)\,,~~

(73)

is the
Shannon entropy
for
P(\mathbf{q},t)
. However, it is straightforward to see that this is not the correct choice. Importantly, the Shannon entropy (
73
) for the
uniform distribution

P^{\rm uni}={\cal V}^{-1}\int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{q}
(
43
) is
D_{\rm uni}=\log{\cal V}
, where
{\cal V}=\int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{q}
. Meanwhile, the Shannon entropy (
73
) for the stationary distribution
P^{\rm ss}={\cal Z}^{-1}\,\int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{q}\,\exp( - \Phi(\vb{q}) )
(
41
)—where
{\cal Z}=\int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{q}\,\exp( - \Phi(\vb{q}) )
is analogous to a partition function for
P^{\rm ss}
—is given by

\displaystyle D_{\rm Shannon}\left[P^{\rm ss}\right]\,

\displaystyle=-\int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{q}\frac{e^{-\Phi(\mathbf{q})}}{\cal Z}\,\log\frac{e^{-\Phi(\mathbf{q})}}{\cal Z}\,=\,\log{\cal Z}+\frac{1}{\cal Z}\int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{q}\,\Phi(\mathbf{q})\,e^{-\Phi(\mathbf{q})}\,\leq\,D_{\rm Shannon}\left[P^{\rm uni}\right]\,,~~

(74)

where the final relation follows from Gibbs’ inequality, which says that
-\int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{q}\,P^{\rm ss}(\mathbf{q})\log P^{\rm ss}(\mathbf{q})\leq-\int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{q}\,P^{\rm ss}(\mathbf{q})\log P^{\rm uni}=\log{\cal V}
. In other words, Gibbs’ inequality guarantees that the Shannon entropy (
73
) for the stationary distribution
P^{\rm ss}
(
41
) saturates
\log{\cal V}
(i.e., is maximal) if and only if
P^{\rm ss}
is uniform. Hence, the Shannon entropy (
73
) is only a valid definition of the entropy for stochastic EFTs in which the stationary distribution
P^{\rm ss}
(
41
) is uniform (
43
), which is not generally the case for the systems of interest (e.g., active matter).

For generic stationary distributions
P^{\rm ss}
(
41
), we require a functional of
D[P(\mathbf{q},t)]
that is maximal when
P(\mathbf{q},t)=P^{\rm ss}
. This suggests that the correct entropy
D[P]
should be of the form of a
relative entropy
of the true probability distribution
P(\mathbf{q},t)
and the stationary distribution
P^{\rm ss}
(
41
). A natural choice—which follows from Gibbs’ inequality—is the Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence (or relative entropy),
D_{\rm KL}\left[P^{\rm ss}(\mathbf{q})\middle\|P(\mathbf{q},t)\right]=\int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{q}\,P^{\rm ss}(\mathbf{q})\,\log\left[\,P^{\rm ss}(\mathbf{q})/P(\mathbf{q},t)\,\right]\leq 0
. However, this shows that the KL divergence is
minimized
when
P(\mathbf{q},t)=P^{\rm ss}
, suggesting that the correct notion of entropy in generic stochastic EFTs is given by
minus
the KL divergence,

D\left[P(\mathbf{q},t)\right]\,\equiv\,\int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{q}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\Phi(\mathbf{q})}\,\log\frac{P(\mathbf{q},t)}{\mathrm{e}^{-\Phi(\mathbf{q})}}\,,~~

(75)

where we have ignored the normalization of
P^{\rm ss}
and
P(\mathbf{q},t)
, since this merely amounts to an unimportant rescaling and overall constant shift of
D
(
75
). We now explore this quantity in more detail.

First, we prove that a second law of thermodynamics (
72
) holds for
D(t)
(
75
). Recall that, by Gibbs’ inequality,
D(t)
is maximal only when
P\propto\exp(-\Phi)
. For convenience, we write
P(\mathbf{q},t)
in the form

P(\mathbf{q},t)\,=\,\mathrm{e}^{-\Phi(\mathbf{q})-\Psi(\mathbf{q},t)}\,,~~

(76)

where
\Psi
is small when
P
is close to the stationary distribution
P^{\rm ss}
(
41
). We then find that

\displaystyle\derivative{D}{t}\,

\displaystyle=\,\int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{q}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\Phi}\,\derivative{\,}{t}\log P\,=\,\int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{q}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\Phi}\,\frac{1}{P}\,\derivative{P}{t}\,=\,\int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{q}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\Phi}\,\mathrm{e}^{\Phi+\Psi}\,\left(-W\,P\right)

\displaystyle=\,\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{q}\,\mathrm{e}^{\Psi}\,\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\,\mathcal{Q}_{ab}\,\left(\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{b}+\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{b}\right)\mathrm{e}^{-\Phi-\Psi}\,=\,-\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{q}\,\mathrm{e}^{\Psi}\,\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\,\mathcal{Q}_{ab}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\Phi-\Psi}\,\left(\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{b}\Psi\right)

\displaystyle=\,\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{q}\,\left(\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\Psi\right)\,\mathrm{e}^{\Psi}\,\mathcal{Q}_{ab}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\Phi-\Psi}\,\left(\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{b}\Psi\right)\,,~~

(77)

where, in the last line, we used integration by parts and the fact that
\mathrm{e}^{\Psi}\mathcal{Q}_{ab}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\Phi-\Psi}\left(\partial^{\,}_{b}\Psi\right)
vanishes at the boundary of phase space. For simple noise distributions—in which
\mathcal{Q}_{ab}
depends only on
\mathbf{q}
and not
{\bf\it\partial}
—we observe that

\derivative{D}{t}\,\to\,\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{d}\mathbf{q}\,(\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\psi)\,\mathcal{Q}_{ab}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\Phi}\,(\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{b}\psi)\,\geq\,0\,,~~

(78)

by the fact that
\mathcal{Q}_{ab}\sim Q_{ab}
(
57
) is positive semidefinite. Thus, if we define
D
(
75
) to be the entropy of the probability distribution
P(\mathbf{q},t)
for a system that relaxes to the stationary distribution
P^{\rm ss}(\mathbf{q})=\exp(-\Phi(\vb{q}))
(
41
), and for noise distribution in which
\mathcal{Q}
(
57
) contains no derivatives, we obtain a second law of thermodynamics, captured by Eq. (
78
). This fact is well understood in, e.g., the statistics literature.

For non-Gaussian noise, which contributes higher-derivative corrections to the Fokker-Planck generator
W
(
53
) via Eq. (
65
), it is no longer obvious that
D(t)
(
75
) must be a nondecreasing function of
t
. However,
D(t)
should increase due to the data-processing inequality
Cover and Thomas 2012
. This suggests that our generalized notion of a second law of thermodynamics provides nontrivial constraints on any effective theory of non-Gaussian stochastic dynamics, which cannot be deduced by simply writing down all invariant building blocks in a derivative expansion in
W
. It would be interesting to more systematically analyze these constraints, but we expect that they are related to the fact that in the presence of non-Gaussian noise, the moments of the noise distribution are not independent and must be compatible with the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. It would be interesting to explicitly check this by using the relations between microscopic noise and higher-derivative terms in the FPE, following Ref.
60
.

The formal notion of entropy that we have defined depends
nonlinearly
on the solution to the Fokker-Planck equation (
34
). Thus, it is not defined for
single
stochastic trajectories—in fact, it is not possible for such an entropy to be well defined. For example, in the overdamped oscillator discussed in Sec.
1
, such an entropy would have to be maximal when
x=0
, but a system initialized with
x=0
can reach a state with
x\neq 0
, meaning that such an entropy function would decrease on this trajectory. To define a suitable entropy function
S(q_{a})
on phase space, the best one can hope for is that this function is nondecreasing
on average
. For linear stochastic processes, such an entropy function always exists (see App.
B.1
for details). For nonlinear processes, we have not systematically found such a function, but we anticipate that combining the construction above with the arguments of Ref.
61
could imply the existence of such an entropy function in certain models (e.g., in hydrodynamic systems).

2.6
Symmetries and conservation laws

Having developed the basic EFT formalism for stochastic dynamics (
34
) in terms of the stationary distribution
\Phi
(
41
), we now discuss the role of symmetries and conservation laws. As we will see, even though symmetries may lead to nonunique stationary distributions (e.g., depending on the “symmetry sector” to which the conserved charge belongs), they are elegantly imposed within our framework, using an analogue of Noether’s theorem for stochastic dynamics in Eq. (
80
). Note that there are two subtly different ways by which symmetry can enter our EFT formalism, corresponding to symmetries of the dynamics versus symmetries of the stationary distribution
\Phi
(
41
). The former case admits two types of conserved quantities, corresponding to “strong” and “weak” symmetries: A
strong
symmetry holds for each individual, random (stochastic) trajectory, while a
weak
symmetry only holds upon averaging over trajectories. However, there also exist situations where a symmetry of the stationary distribution
P^{\rm ss}\propto\exp(\Phi)
is not a symmetry of the dynamics (even on average). We address each case in turn, and detail how such symmetries constrain the evolution operator
W
(
53
).

2.6.1
Strong and weak symmetries of the dynamics

We first consider the case of
strong
symmetries, in which a quantity
F(\mathbf{q})
is conserved (i.e.,
\derivative*{F}{t}=0
) on
all
trajectories, and derive the consequences of such a conservation law on
W
(
53
). Since
F
is constant in time by construction, multiplying and dividing
W
by (any function of) thereof should not change
W
. In particular,

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{-F}\,W\,\mathrm{e}^{F}\,=\,W\,,~~

(79)

must hold for any such
F
. Since
W
is generically a differential operator, we must account for the action of
W
on the left-hand side of Eq. (
79
) on the function
\exp(F)
to its right. We then find that, for
F
to be conserved,
W
must be invariant under the following shift of the differential operator
\partial^{\,}_{a}
,

\displaystyle W(\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a},q_{a})\,=\,W(\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}+[\partialderivative*{F}{q_a}],q_{a})\,,~~

(80)

where the quantity
[\partialderivative*{F}{q_a}]
in square brackets is a function—not a differential operator. Thus, the form of
W
(
53
) is further constrained by
each
conserved quantity
F
; this also extends to the case of vector conserved quantities, in which each component of
F
is conserved. Examples of strong symmetries appear in Secs.
3.5
and
6
.

We now consider the case of
weak
symmetries, in which case, instead of
\derivative*{F}{t}=0
, we instead have

\displaystyle\derivative{t}\expectationvalue{F(t)}\,=\,\derivative{t}\int\differential{\vb{q}}F(\mathbf{q})P(\mathbf{q},t)\,=\,0\,,~~

(81)

where
P(\mathbf{q},t)
is the probability distribution at time
t
, and
\expectationvalue{F}
denotes the average of
F
over random trajectories (since
F
is only a conserved quantity on average, and not along individual trajectories). Note that
F(\mathbf{q})
has no explicit time dependence (it depends on time only implicitly via its dependence on
\mathbf{q}
); as a result,

\displaystyle 0\,=\,\int\differential{\vb{q}}\,F(\mathbf{q})\,\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{t}P(\mathbf{q})\,=\,\int\differential{\vb{q}}\,F(\mathbf{q})\,[-W\,P(\mathbf{q})]\,=\,\int\differential{\vb q}\,P(\mathbf{q})[-W^{T}F(\mathbf{q})]\,,~~

(82)

and, since
W
(
53
) always has a derivative operator on the left (to preserve normalization in the FPE), we find

\displaystyle W^{T}F(\mathbf{q})\,=\,\frac{1}{2}\,(\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}-\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a})\mathcal{Q}_{ba}\,\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{b}F\,=\,0\,,~~

(83)

so that, in many cases, we may simply enforce the condition. Thus, both strong and weak symmetries in stochastic dynamics may be enforced via constraints on
W
(
53
).

2.6.2
Symmetries of the stationary distribution

Alternatively, there may also exist “symmetries” of the stationary distribution function
\Phi
(
41
) that do
not
correspond to quantities
F
that are conserved by the dynamics, even on average. Nonetheless, there remains a sense in which a particular invariance of
\Phi
corresponds to a genuine symmetry; moreover, these nondynamical symmetries are relevant to our EFT formalism and have implications for the time-evolution operator
W
(
53
).

In particular, such symmetries directly facilitate the construction of certain dissipationless terms in
W
. For instance, suppose that a given stationary distribution
\Phi[\mathbf{q}]
(
41
) is invariant under coordinate shifts

q_{a}\,\to\,q_{a}+\epsilon F_{a}(\mathbf{q})\,,~~

(84)

which implies that the generator
\mathbf{F}
and chemical potential
{\bf\it\mu}
are orthogonal, i.e.,

\mathbf{F}\cdot{\bf\it\mu}\,=\,F_{a}\,\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\,=\,\partialderivative{q_a}{\epsilon}\,\partialderivative{\Phi}{q_a}\,=\,\derivative{\Phi}{\epsilon}\,=\,0\,,~~

(85)

and we also take
F
to be divergenceless (i.e.,
\partial^{\,}_{a}F_{a}=0
). In that case, a dissipationless term

\displaystyle v_{a}\,\sim\,F_{a}\,,~~

(86)

as defined in Eq. (
67
) automatically satisfies the constraint of Eq. (
70
) on all dissipationless contributions to the Fokker-Planck force
\mathbf{f}
(
60
), and is therefore allowed. More generally, dissipationless terms
v^{\,}_{a}
are always allowed to move the system along
level sets
(
84
) of the stationary distribution function
\Phi
(
41
).

A particularly important application of this idea arises when the distribution
\Phi
is invariant under some group
G
. This provides for the inclusion of nontrivial (and quite exotic) dissipationless terms, even though there may be no corresponding dynamically conserved quantity. We treat this more general case in Sec.
5.1
, where we also discuss the resulting possibility of spontaneous breaking of generalized time-reversal symmetry gT.

We note that symmetries of the distribution function
\Phi
(
41
) also offer a means by which to generate additional terms in
W
(
53
), starting from some “minimal” choice of
W
compatible with
\Phi
. To see this, note that a continuous symmetry of
\Phi
generated via Eq. (
84
) acts on a generic probability distribution
P
(
33
) via

\displaystyle P\,\to\,P^{\prime}\,=\,\mathrm{e}^{\epsilon F_{a}\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}}P\,,~~

(87)

where invariance of
\Phi
(
41
) under the transformation of Eq. (
84
) generated by
\mathbf{F}
requires that
\Phi^{\prime}=\Phi
(which recovers from taking
P=P^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{\rm ss}
and
P^{\prime}=P_{\rm ss}^{\prime}
). Applying
\exp(\epsilon F_a \pd*{a})
to both sides of
\partial^{\,}_{t}P=-WP
(
36
) leads to

\displaystyle\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{t}P\,=\,-WP\implies\mathrm{e}^{-\epsilon F_{a}\partial^{\,}_{a}}\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{t}P^{\prime}\,=\,-W\mathrm{e}^{-\epsilon F_{a}\partial^{\,}_{a}}P^{\prime}~~\implies~~\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{t}P^{\prime}\,=\,-\mathrm{e}^{+\epsilon F_{a}\partial^{\,}_{a}}W\mathrm{e}^{-\epsilon F_{a}\partial^{\,}_{a}}P^{\prime}\,\equiv\,-W^{\prime}P^{\prime}\,,~~

(88)

and, since
P_{\rm ss}^{\vphantom{\prime}}=P_{\rm ss}^{\prime}
(
41
), we have found that
W^{\prime}\equiv\mathrm{e}^{\epsilon F_{a}\partial^{\,}_{a}}W\mathrm{e}^{-\epsilon F_{a}\partial^{\,}_{a}}
is a valid Fokker-Planck generator with the
same
stationary distribution
P_{\rm ss}=\exp(\Phi)
(
41
) as
W
! In particular, for infinitesimal transformations, we find

\displaystyle W^{\prime}\,=\,W+\epsilon\left[F_{a}\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a},W\right]\,,~~

(89)

where
[A,B]=AB-BA
is the commutator. The shift captured by Eq. (
89
) shows how additional terms (consistent with the same stationary distribution) may be added to a given Fokker-Planck generator
W
.

2.7
Connection to the Martin-Siggia-Rose path integral

We now connect the foregoing operator formalism to the path-integral description of stochastic Langevin dynamics (
31
) and FPEs (
34
) due to Martin, Siggia, and Rose (MSR)
Martin et al. 1973
. The latter affords a Lagrangian description of dissipative systems, so that familiar path-integral methods can be used to study active and dissipative dynamics not only of point-particle systems, but continuum systems (i.e., field theories). As with the Fokker-Planck operator formalism developed thus far, the mathematical implementation and physical role of the time-reversal operation T are deeply important to the MSR description of the stochastic EFT. The MSR implementation of T appears—at least superficially—quite similar to that of the Kubo-Martin-Schwinger (KMS) invariance of systems both in thermal equilibrium
Araki 1978
;
Martin and Schwinger 1959
and beyond
Guo et al. 2022
;
Qi et al. 2023
. However, we note that there are several subtleties; accordingly, we view the corresponding
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
operation as the path-integral implementation of T.

We also note that T is often cumbersome to implement in the MSR framework—at least in full generality—due to subtleties related to the regularization (i.e., Itô versus Stratonovich) of SDEs such as Eq. (
31
). We relegate the appertaining technical details to App.
C
; other subtleties related to the regularization are discussed in Ref.
53
. In most of this work, we instead formulate EFTs in terms of the FPE operator
W
(
53
), in which T is straightforward. However, we note that a direct connection between the FPE and MSR implementations of T exists in certain simple limits. Accordingly—and owing to the utility of the MSR formalism in describing continuum models, and its relevance to recent developments concerning active and dissipative systems—we now review the MSR formalism, the implementation of T therein, and its relation to the operator-based Fokker-Planck approach discussed previously.

2.7.1
Details of the MSR formalism

The MSR path integral reproduces the transition probability (
39
) according to

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\mathbf{q}_{i}(0)\,\to\,\mathbf{q}_{f}(t);\mathcal{H}]\,=\,\langle\mathbf{q}_{f}(t)|\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}\,W(\mathbf{q}(t^{\prime}))}|\mathbf{q}_{i}(0)\rangle\,=\,\int\limits_{\mathbf{q}(0)=\mathbf{q}_{i}}^{\mathbf{q}(t)=\mathbf{q}_{f}}\mathrm{D}\mathbf{q}\,\mathrm{D}{\bf\it\pi}~\exp\left[\mathrm{i}\,\int\limits^{t}_{0}\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}\,(\pi_{a}\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{t}q_{a}-\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{q},{\bf\it\pi}))\right]\,,~~

(90)

where the conjugate momenta
\pi_{a}\sim-\mathrm{i}\partial^{\,}_{a}
and Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}\sim-\mathrm{i}\,W
are defined in Eq. (
54
) for FPE dynamics. As a reminder,
\cal H
is generally
not
the microscopic Hamiltonian (which appears in Sec.
3
), and the coordinates
\mathbf{q}
include all phase-space variables describing the dynamical system—for
N
point particles in
d
spatial dimensions,
\mathbf{q}
includes all
dN
canonical positions
x_{a}

and
momenta
p_{b}
. The fields
\pi_{a}
are the conjugate momenta to
q_{a}
in the sense of the EFT formalism, and are often easier to work with than
\partial^{\,}_{a}
in the context of continuum dynamics.

Before explaining the details of the MSR formalism and its connection to the FPE operator language, we first introduce a slightly modified notation to incorporate
multiplicative
noise. A stochastic process with random noise source
\xi_{a}(t)
—where
\expectationvalue{\xi_a (t)}=0
and
\expectationvalue{\, \xi_a(t) \, \xi_b(t') \, }=\Delta\,\delta^{\,}_{a,b}\,\delta(t-t^{\prime})
—can be extended to account for multiplicative noise via
\eta_{a}(\mathbf{q},t)=M_{ab}(\mathbf{q})\,\xi_{b}(t)
. Essentially, the bare noise
\xi_{b}
is modulated by
M_{ab}(\mathbf{q})
, so that

Q_{ab}(\mathbf{q})\,=\,\int\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}\,\expectationvalue{\eta_a(\vb{q},t) \eta_b (\vb{q},t')}\,=\,M_{ac}(\mathbf{q})\,M_{bd}(\mathbf{q})\int\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}\,\expectationvalue{\xi_c (t) \xi_{d}(t')}\,=\,M_{ac}(\mathbf{q})\,M_{bc}(\mathbf{q})\,,~~

(91)

is the symmetric, positive-semidefinite noise matrix (
35
). In the case
Q_{ab}(\mathbf{q})=\Delta\,\delta^{\,}_{a,b}
, the multiplicative noise reduces to Gaussian additive noise, described by the noise matrix (
35
) with variance
\Delta
.

Now, consider a stochastic process captured by a Langevin SDE (
31
) and subject to multiplicative noise
\eta_{a}
with noise matrix
Q
(
91
). The corresponding MSR path integral (with boundary conditions suppressed) is given by

\displaystyle Z[{\bf\it\xi}]\,=\,\int\mathrm{D}\mathbf{q}\,\mathcal{J}[\mathbf{q}]~\delta\left(\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{t}q_{a}-f_{a}(\mathbf{q})-M_{ab}(\mathbf{q})\,\xi_{b}(t)\right)\,=\,1\,,~~

(92)

where
\mathcal{J}[\mathbf{q}]
is the Jacobian of the operators inside the delta function (see App.
C
for technical details), and
{\bf\it\xi}
appears in Eq. (
92
) because
Z
is not yet averaged over noise. Note that Eq. (
92
) is equal to one because the integral is indefinite, and realizes the transition amplitude of Eq. (
90
) when appropriate boundary conditions are imposed. For convenience, we consider the Itô regularization, for which
\mathcal{J}[\mathbf{q}]=1
. The noise-averaged partition function (
92
) is

\displaystyle Z\,

\displaystyle=\,\int\mathrm{D}{\bf\it\xi}~Z[{\bf\it\xi}]\,\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{d}t\,\xi_{a}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\,\int\mathrm{D}{\bf\it\xi}\,\mathrm{D}\mathbf{q}\,\mathrm{D}{\bf\it\pi}~\exp\left(\mathrm{i}\int\mathrm{d}t\,\pi_{a}(\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{t}q_{a}-f_{a}(\mathbf{q})-M_{ab}(\mathbf{q})\,\xi_{b})\right)~\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{d}t\,\xi_{a}^{2}\right)\,,~~

(93)

and integrating out the noise (i.e., evaluating the integral over
{\bf\it\xi}
) leads to

\displaystyle Z\,

\displaystyle=\,\int\mathrm{D}\mathbf{q}\,\mathrm{D}{\bf\it\pi}~\exp\left(\mathrm{i}\int\mathrm{d}t\,(\pi_{a}\,\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{t}q_{a}-\pi_{a}f_{a}(\mathbf{q})+\mathrm{i}\frac{1}{2}\pi_{a}Q_{ab}\pi_{b})\right)

\displaystyle=\,\int\mathrm{D}\mathbf{q}\,\mathrm{D}{\bf\it\pi}~\exp\left(\mathrm{i}\int\mathrm{d}t\,L(\mathbf{q},{\bf\it\pi})\right)\equiv\int\mathrm{D}\mathbf{q}\,\mathrm{D}{\bf\it\pi}~\exp\left(\mathrm{i}\int\mathrm{d}t\,(\pi_{a}\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{t}q_{a}-\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{q},{\bf\it\pi}))\right)\,,~~

(94)

which implicitly defines the MSR
Lagrangian

L(\mathbf{q},{\bf\it\pi})\,=\,\pi_{a}\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{t}q_{a}-\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{q},{\bf\it\pi})\,=\,\pi_{a}\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{t}q_{a}-\pi_{a}f_{a}(\mathbf{q})+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\pi_{a}\,Q_{ab}(\mathbf{q})\,\pi_{b}\,,~~

(95)

where we have written both Eqs. (
94
) and (
95
) such that replacing
\pi_{a}\to-\mathrm{i}\,\partial^{\,}_{a}
(
54
) recovers
W=\mathrm{i}\,\mathcal{H}
(
53
).

2.7.2
Time-reversal symmetry

In developing the operator-based Wilsonian EFT for stochastic dynamics, we have emphasized the simple nature of the “time-reversal” transformation T acting on the Fokker-Planck generator
W
(
46
). There is also a natural definition of T in the MSR formulation of the same stochastic effective theories. Importantly, the latter implementation of T is (
i
) generally more convenient in the context of continuum systems described by field theories and (
ii
) closely connected to the KMS transformation
Araki 1978
;
Martin and Schwinger 1959
, around which the thermal EFTs of, e.g., hydrodynamic systems are organized
Glorioso and Liu 2018
;
Guo et al. 2022
;
Qi et al. 2023
;
Mullins et al. 2023
. We first present the path-integral implementation of T (
97
) in a simple (yet physically relevant) limit in which it is directly related to its FPE-operator analogue (
46
). We then justify in the path-integral framework that the corresponding transformation (
97
) is the correct MSR implementation of T. Finally, we comment on the connection between these two implementations of T beyond the simple limit above.

Consider a T-invariant stochastic model (i.e., with
{\cal W}=\widetilde{W}\implies{\cal H}=\widetilde{\cal H}
) subject to a Gaussian noise source with constant variance matrix
Q_{ab}
(
35
). Ignoring for the moment the dissipationless term
v_{a}
(
67
) in
\mathbf{f}
(
60
), the MSR Lagrangian for such a stochastic system is given by

L\,=\,\pi_{a}\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{t}q_{a}+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\pi_{a}\,\Delta_{ab}\,\left(\pi_{b}-\mathrm{i}\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{b}\right)\,,~~

(96)

which follows from Eqs. (
35
), (
60
), and (
95
), where the elements
Q_{ab}=\Delta_{ab}
(
35
) are independent of both
\mathbf{q}
and
{\bf\it\partial}
. We consider the inclusion of dissipationless terms—along with multiplicative and higher-derivative noise—momentarily.

The corresponding
MSR implementation of T
in the limit of Gaussian noise and T-even
W
(
53
) is given by

\displaystyle t\,\to-t~,~~q_{a}\to\pm q_{a}~,~~\text{and}~~\pi_{a}\to\mp\left(\pi_{a}-\mathrm{i}\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\right)\,,~~

(97)

where
\pm
denotes whether
q_{a}
is even (
+
) or odd (
-
) under T (i.e.,
x\to x
and
p\to-p
), and we find that
\mu^{\,}_{a}\to\pm\mu^{\,}_{a}
under T (
97
). We note that the transformation in Eq. (
97
) is mathematically equivalent to the implementation of KMS in the path-integral (EFT) description of thermal dissipative systems
Araki 1978
;
Martin and Schwinger 1959
;
Glorioso and Liu 2018
;
Qi et al. 2023
;
Guo et al. 2022
.

By applying the transformation T (
97
) to
\mathbb{P}[\mathbf{q}_{i}(0)\to\mathbf{q}_{f}(t)]
(
90
), we can compare the probability to go from configuration
\mathbf{q}_{i}
to
\mathbf{q}_{f}
in time
t
to the probability to go from
\mathbf{q}_{f}
to
\mathbf{q}_{i}
in time
t
under the time-reversed process (
46
),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}\,[\mathbf{q}_{i}(0)\to\mathbf{q}_{f}(t);\mathcal{H}]\,

\displaystyle=\,\int_{\mathbf{q}(0)=\mathbf{q}_{f}}^{\mathbf{q}(t)=\mathbf{q}_{i}}\mathrm{D}\mathbf{q}\,\mathrm{D}{\bf\it\pi}~\exp\left(\mathrm{i}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}\,\left[(\pi_{a}-\mathrm{i}\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a})\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{t}q_{a}-\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}(\mathbf{q},{\bf\it\pi})\right]\right)\,,~~

(98)

where
\widetilde{\cal H}=-\mathrm{i}\widetilde{W}
(
46
) is the time-reversed MSR Hamiltonian. Using the standard chain rule, we note that
\mu^{\,}_{a}\partial^{\,}_{t}q^{\,}_{a}=(\partialderivative*{\Phi}{q_a})\,\partial^{\,}_{t}q_{a}=\derivative*{\Phi}{t}
, so that the probability amplitude above becomes

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}\,[\mathbf{q}_{i}(0)\to\mathbf{q}_{f}(t);\mathcal{H}]\,

\displaystyle=\,\mathrm{e}^{\Phi(\mathbf{q}_{i})-\Phi(\mathbf{q}_{f})}\,\int_{\mathbf{q}(0)=\mathbf{q}_{f}}^{\mathbf{q}(t)=\mathbf{q}_{i}}\mathrm{D}\mathbf{q}\,\mathrm{D}{\bf\it\pi}~\exp\left(\mathrm{i}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}\,\left[\pi_{a}\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{t}q_{a}-\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}(q,\pi)\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\,\mathrm{e}^{\Phi(\mathbf{q}_{i})-\Phi(\mathbf{q}_{f})}~\mathbb{P}[\mathbf{q}_{f}(0)\to\mathbf{q}_{i}(t);\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}]\,,~~

(99)

which is the probability to transition from
\mathbf{q}_{f}
to
\mathbf{q}_{i}
in time
t
under
\widetilde{\cal H}
(
46
)—the time-reversed partner to
{\cal H}=-\mathrm{i}W
—in analogy to Eq. (
50
). Note that we have restricted to models in which
{\cal H}
is invariant under T, and thus obeys
detailed balance
(
17
). When
Q_{ab}
(
35
) corresponds to Gaussian noise (i.e., contains no derivatives
\partial^{\,}_{a}
), we find that

\displaystyle{\cal H}\,=\,-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\pi_{a}\,Q_{ab}\,\left(\pi_{a}-\mathrm{i}\,\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\right)\,

\displaystyle\to\,\widetilde{\cal H}\,=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\left(\pi_{a}-\mathrm{i}\,\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\right)\,Q_{ab}\,\pi_{a}\,=\,\,{\cal H},~~

(100)

meaning that
{\cal H}=\widetilde{\cal H}
, as required. Note that the transformation T (
97
)—and the connection to its FPE counterpart (
46
)—are complicated in the presence of non-Gaussian noise, where the derivatives
\partial^{\,}_{a}
in the expansion for
{\cal Q}_{ab}
(
65
) spoil the derivations above. For such noise sources, the connection between the two implementations of T is more elusive. However for Gaussian noise, invariance under the transformation of Eq. (
97
) implies that

W_{\rm MSR-even}\,=-\frac{1}{2}\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\left(\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{b}+\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{b}\right)Q_{ab}\,=\,\mathrm{i}\,{\cal H}_{\rm MSR-even}\,=\,\mathrm{i}\,\widetilde{\cal H}\,=\,\widetilde{W}\,,~~

(101)

in the FPE operator language, since the Fokker-Planck generator
W
(
53
) is straightforwardly related to the MSR Hamiltonian
\cal{H}
(
95
) via Eq. (
54
) when written in the Itô formalism (with all derivatives to the left).

In the presence of
multiplicative
noise, Eq. (
101
) fails, as
Q_{ab}
depends on
\mathbf{q}
. Moreover, even in the case of dissipation toward equilibrium, the connection to thermal KMS
Glorioso and Liu 2018
is complicated in the presence of multiplicative noise. In App.
C
, we show that Eq. (
97
) does
not
preserve the Itô regularization
4
4

4

A similar issue is also known to arise in standard quantum mechanical path integrals for particles for which
H\neq p^{2}/2m+V(q)

Kleinert 2009
.
; thus, the correct implementation of T involves supplementing Eq. (
97
) with an appropriate change in the regularization itself
5
5

5

In previous work
Gao et al. 2020
, the KMS transformation was implemented using a regularization stated in the
frequency
domain, in which case it is not straightforward to cast the dynamics in terms of a master equation.
. In App.
C
, in the case of multiplicative Gaussian noise, we explain how to implement the path integral (
90
) with a suitable regularization such that T (
97
) is manifestly a symmetry. We relegate a study of more general, non-Gaussian noise terms to future work.

However, we also note that, from a field-theoretic
renormalization-group
perspective, multiplicative noise is typically
irrelevant
. Thus, any potential discrepancy between the MSR implementation of T (
97
) and both the FPE analogue (
46
) and the KMS transformation for thermal systems
Araki 1978
;
Martin and Schwinger 1959
may ultimately be unimportant to the universal properties and classification of active and dissipative systems.

We now consider more general models, which may include
dissipationless
contributions
v_{a}
(
67
) to the Fokker-Planck force
\mathbf{f}
(
60
). In this case, we adjust the (T-even) MSR Lagrangian (
96
) as

L\,=\,L_{\rm MSR-even}+\pi_{a}\,v_{a}\,=\,\pi_{a}\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{t}q_{a}+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\pi_{a}\,Q_{ab}(\pi_{b}-\mathrm{i}\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{b})+\pi_{a}v_{a}\,,~~

(102)

where
v_{a}
(
67
) is generically a function of
\mathbf{q}
. Suppose that
v_{a}\to\widetilde{v}_{a}
under T, then for each label
a
, the
\pi_{a}v_{a}
term in the MSR Lagrangian (
102
) transforms as

\pi_{a}v_{a}\,\to\,\mp(\pi_{a}-\mathrm{i}\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a})\widetilde{v}_{a}\,,~~

(103)

and if
\widetilde{v}_{a}=v_{a}
, the
V
term in Eq. (
57
)—corresponding to
\pi_{a}v_{a}
in
L
(
102
)—flips sign under T, as expected. On the other hand, if
\widetilde{v}_{a}=-v_{a}
, the
\pi_{a}v_{a}
in
L
(
102
) is invariant under T (such dissipationless terms obey detailed balance, and are compatible with Hamiltonian dynamics). We now observe an important complication: The extra
\mathrm{i}\mu^{\,}_{a}v_{a}
term in Eq. (
103
) that is “spawned” when we apply T (
97
) to
L
(
102
). For the Lagrangian
L
(
102
) to transform under T into another valid MSR Lagrangian in the Itô regularization, we must have
\mu^{\,}_{a}v_{a}=0
since all terms must have at least one power of
\pi_{a}
. However, this is
not
the constraint on T-odd terms derived in Eq. (
70
) unless
\partial^{\,}_{b}V_{ab}=0
. This is guaranteed so long as
V_{ab}
is constant, but may not hold in general. The origin of this complication is the subtlety surrounding regularizations noted below Eq. (
101
). The takeaway is that there are extra constraints on the dissipationless terms
\pi_{a}v_{a}
in the MSR Lagrangian due to stationarity: Dynamics compatible with a stationary distribution
\Phi
(
41
) have a time-reversed evolution operator
\widetilde{W}
(
46
) that is also a valid FPE generator; however, if
\mu^{\,}_{a}v_{a}\neq 0
, then the time-reversed MSR Lagrangian does not generate valid Fokker-Planck dynamics, as all terms in the MSR Lagrangian (
95
) must contain at least one power of
\pi_{a}
.

The subtleties discussed above concerning multiplicative noise and dissipationless contributions arise when
\partial_{b}Q_{ab}
or
\partial_{b}V_{ab}
are nonzero. However, for continuum field theories, such as hydrodynamics, we show explicitly in App.
D
that it is possible to choose a spatial discretization of
Q_{ab}
and
V_{ab}
such that their divergences vanish. This discretization, which can be thought of as a regularization of the model, leads to an MSR realization of time reversal (
97
) that is
exactly
equivalent to the Fokker-Planck realization of T (
46
). The crucial assumption is that the expansion in spatial gradients is treated perturbatively. This observation further suggests that precision tests
Delacretaz and Mishra 2023
of nonlinear fluctuating hydrodynamics would
not
detect any discrepancy between reversibility (
46
) and KMS invariance
Araki 1978
;
Martin and Schwinger 1959
; meaning that such discrepancies can only arise in theories where higher-gradients are not perturbative.

2.7.3
Time-reversal symmetry without integrating out noise

The MSR framework also makes transparent how a noisy SDE (
31
) is
microscopically
T invariant, and thus obeys detailed balance. For simplicity, consider additive Gaussian noise with
Q_{ab}=\Delta\,\delta^{\,}_{a,b}
(
35
), so that T (
97
) acts as

t\,\to-t~,~~q_{a}\,\to\pm q_{a}~,~~\pi_{a}\,\to\mp\left(\pi_{a}-\mathrm{i}\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\right)~,~~\xi_{a}\,\to\mp\left(\xi_{a}-\Delta\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\right)\,,~~

(104)

where
\pm
refers to whether
q_{a}
is T even (
+
) or T odd (
-
). It is straightforward to verify that the MSR Lagrangian
L
(
93
) prior to integrating out the noise
\xi_{a}
is invariant under the transformation T (
104
).

In other words, Eq. (
104
) is the transformation in the path-integral EFT corresponding to T (i.e., reversibility). Considering the damped harmonic oscillator described by Eq. (
4
) of Sec.
1
, from our perspective, it makes sense to say that
\dot{x}=-\gamma x+\xi
, with
\gamma=\Delta\mu/2x
as in Eq. (
6
), is time-reversal symmetric (or T even). Under time reversal T, the noise variable
\xi
shifts so as to undo precisely the naïve sign change to the dissipative term. This does not happen by chance, but is a direct consequence of the existence of a stationary state.

3
Dissipative generalizations of Hamiltonian dynamics

Before we consider the application of the effective field theory (EFT) framework of Sec.
2
to nonequilibrium
active
matter, we first consider EFTs for dissipative thermal systems. The formalism of Sec.
2
provides a simple theoretical description of Hamiltonian systems in thermal equilibrium, and a natural prescription for adding dissipation to any such model. Such systems evolve toward a particular stationary distribution, corresponding to

\Phi\,=\,\beta\,H\,,~~

(105)

meaning that stationary distribution
\Phi
is simply the Boltzmann distribution for the underlying Hamiltonian
H
at inverse temperature
\beta
. Such a distribution naturally describes thermal equilibrium of a system with its environment, where the Hamiltonian
H
is also responsible for time evolution. We now describe several simplifying aspects of Hamiltonian dynamics, along with a natural procedure for coupling Hamiltonian systems to dissipative baths. While the treatment of equilibrium systems herein largely reproduces well-known results, it does so in a more systematic manner using a simple algorithm compared to existing literature, and helps to build intuition for the EFT formalism in anticipation of its later application to active matter in the rest of the paper.

We first review of Hamiltonian mechanics in Sec.
3.1
, using the EFT formalism of Sec.
2
. In particular, given a stationary state
\Phi=\beta H
(
105
), we relate the Hamiltonian
H
to a Fokker-Planck generator
W
compatible with
\Phi
; we note that this does not require the use of the canonical phase-space variables, and can incorporate symmetries of the dynamics and stationary distribution
\Phi
. In Sec.
3.2
, we prescribe how to add dissipation to generic Hamiltonian systems (including those with particular symmetries) in equilibrium with a thermal bath; this is remarkably straightforward in the EFT framework of Sec.
2
. Finally, in the remaining subsections, we survey a variety dissipative generalizations of paradigmatic Hamiltonian theories, including an example featuring nontrivial Poisson brackets (in Sec.
3.5
).

3.1
Hamiltonian dynamics and Poisson brackets

Here we briefly review the important features of Hamiltonian dynamics in the absence of dissipation (i.e., noise), and explain the application of the effective theory formalism developed in Sec.
2
to this setting.

3.1.1
Hamiltonian mechanics with canonical phase space variables

Numerous important aspects of Hamiltonian mechanics are captured by the
Poisson bracket

\{\cdot,\cdot\}
, which is defined for arbitrary functions of the phase-space variables. We frequently refer to the simple example of
N
particles in
d
spatial dimensions, described by
Nd
positions
x_{a}
and
Nd
momenta
p_{a}
, which satisfy the canonical Poisson brackets

\displaystyle\quantity{x_a,x_b}\,

\displaystyle=\,\quantity{p_a,p_b}\,=\,0,

(106a)

\displaystyle\quantity{x_a,p_b}\,

\displaystyle=-\quantity{p_b,x_a}\,=\,\delta^{\,}_{a,b}\,,~~

(106b)

where the label
a
combines the particle and spatial-axis indices (with a total of
Nd
values). The Poisson bracket
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
is defined for
all
classical dynamical systems, including those with noncanonical phase-space variables; it also extends to generic functions of the phase-space variables via the chain rule of calculus.

As a reminder, in the EFT formalism of Sec.
2
, the coordinate vector
\mathbf{q}
contains
all
phase-space variables. In the example above,
\mathbf{q}
is a length-
2Nd
vector of all positions
x_{a}

and
momenta
p_{b}
, such that Hamilton’s equations may be written simply as
\partial^{\,}_{t}q_{a}=\quantity{q_a,H}
, with
H
the Hamiltonian. For dissipationless Hamiltonian dynamics, we have

W\,=\,T\,\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\quantity{ q_a,q_b }\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{b}\,,~~

(107)

where
T\equiv\beta^{-1}
is the temperature (with
k_{B}=1
). Because the stationary distribution
\Phi
(
105
) is proportional to the Hamiltonian
H
, the “chemical potentials”
\mu^{\,}_{b}
in Eq. (
107
) are the canonical ones—the Legendre transforms (or thermodynamic conjugates) of the corresponding coordinate
q_{b}
. For example, for a single nonrelativistic particle in one dimension subject to the force
F(x)=V^{\prime}(x)
(i.e.,
H=p^{2}/2m+V(x)
), we have

\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\,=\,\partialderivative{[\beta H]}{q_a}~~\implies~~\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{x}\,=\,\frac{1}{T}\partialderivative{V}{x}\,=-\beta F(x)~,~~\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{p}\,=\,\frac{p}{mT}\,=\,\beta\dot{x}\,,~~

(108)

and thus, for dissipationless Hamiltonian mechanics, knowledge of the Poisson bracket (
106
) and the Hamiltonian
H
, directly implies the form of the Fokker-Planck generator
W
(
111
) of time evolution.

It is also straightforward to recover the corresponding MSR Lagrangian
L(\mathbf{q},\dot{\mathbf{q}},{\bf\it\pi})
(
95
),

\displaystyle L\,=\,\pi_{a}\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{t}q_{a}-\pi_{a}\quantity{q_a, H}\,,~~

(109)

where
\pi_{a}
is conjugate to
q_{a}
in the sense of Sec.
2.7.1
, the Hamiltonian
H
both generates time evolution via
\partial^{\,}_{t}q_{a}=\quantity{q_a,H}
and determines the stationary state
\Phi=\beta H
(
105
), and Eqs. (
107
) and (
109
) lead to the same equations of motion.

It is conventional to identify the Poisson bracket (
106
) with the inverse of the
symplectic form

\omega
,

\omega\,=\,\frac{1}{2}\omega^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{ab}\mathrm{d}q_{a}\wedge\mathrm{d}q_{b}~~~\implies~~~\omega^{-1}_{ab}\,\equiv\,\omega^{ab}\,=\,\quantity{q_a,q_b}\,,~~

(110)

where the
inverse
of the symplectic form is identified by
raised
indices. We can rewrite Eq. (
107
) as

\displaystyle W\,=\,T\,\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\,\omega^{ab}_{\,}\,\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{b}\,,~~

(111)

in terms of the inverse symplectic form
\omega^{-1}
, which is more convenient than Eq. (
107
) when generalizing to systems in which the phase-space variables satisfy noncanonical Poisson brackets (as in Sec.
3.5
).

3.1.2
Time-reversal symmetry

It is natural to ask how the symplectic form
\omega
(
110
) and the Fokker-Planck generator
W
(
111
) behave under time reversal T (
46
). Microscopically, time reversal transforms the phase-space coordinates as, e.g.,
(x,p)\to(x,-p)
; accordingly, the symplectic form
\omega
(
110
) transforms as
\omega\to-\omega
(and likewise for its inverse). Implementing T (
46
) in the operator language, this implies that
W
(
107
) is manifestly time-reversal symmetric for Hamiltonian dynamics (i.e., it obeys detailed balance); taking a simple example with
N=d=1
so that
\mathbf{q}=(x,p)
, we find that

\displaystyle\widetilde{W}\,

\displaystyle=\,S\,\left(\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{x}\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{p}-\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{p}\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{x}\right)\,S^{-1}\,=\,\mathrm{e}^{-\beta H}\,\left((-\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{p})(-\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{x})-(\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{x})(\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{p})\right)\mathrm{e}^{\beta H}

\displaystyle=\,\left(\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{x}\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{p}-[\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{x}\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{p}]+\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{p}[-\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{x}]-\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{p}\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{x}+[\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{p}\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{x}]+\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{x}[-\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{p}]\right)

\displaystyle=\,\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{x}\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{p}-\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{p}\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{x}\,=\,W\,,~~

(112)

where we used the chain rule and the fact that, e.g.,
\mu^{\,}_{x}=\beta\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{x}H
. Importantly, T symmetry is expected for Hamiltonian dynamics that give rise to the thermal equilibrium distribution
\Phi
(
105
). We also observe that
\omega^{ab}\mu^{\,}_{b}
is simply a special case of the term
[\partial^{\,}_{b}V_{ab}]-V_{ab}\mu^{\,}_{b}
in
v_{a}
(
67
), where the first term vanishes for constant
\omega
(
110
). We also stress that the inverse symplectic form
\omega^{ab}
is T even: Considering the case of
N
particles in
d
dimensions with canonical coordinates, the nonzero components are given by
\omega^{ab}=\quantity{x_a,p_b}=\delta^{\,}_{a,b}
; under T, we have

\omega^{ab}\,\to\,S\,(\quantity{x_a,-p_b})^{T}\,S^{-1}\,=\,S\,\quantity{x_a,p_b}\,S^{-1}\,=S\,\delta^{\,}_{a,b}\,S^{-1}\,=\,\delta^{\,}_{a,b}\,=\,\omega^{ab}\,,~~

(113)

and the generalization to additional degrees of freedom (
N\geq 1
) and spatial dimension
d\geq 1
is straightforward.

3.1.3
Symmetries and Noether’s theorem

We now consider how conservation laws of Hamiltonian systems manifest in the EFT formalism developed in Sec.
2
. In particular, we translate the discussion of Sec.
2.6
for stochastic EFTs into the more conventional treatment of conservation laws in Hamiltonian mechanics. According to Eqs. (
107
) and (
109
), the Fokker-Planck generator (
37
) is

\displaystyle W\,=\,\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\quantity{q_a, H}\,,~~

(114)

and consequently, for a
strong
symmetry with conserved quantity
F
, the symmetry condition in Eq. (
80
) leads to

\displaystyle W\,\to\,W+\partialderivative{F}{q_a}\quantity{q_a, H}\,=\,W+\quantity{F, H}\,,~~

(115)

from which we infer that, for the phase-space function
F
to be conserved, the condition in Eq. (
80
) is equivalent to the familiar requirement that
F
must have a vanishing Poisson bracket with the Hamiltonian
H
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\quantity{F, H}\,=\,0\,.~~

(116)

Additionally, in the absence of noise, strong and weak symmetries are
equivalent
. Since
\mathcal{Q}_{ab}=\omega^{-1}_{ab}
for dissipationless Hamiltonian mechanics, the analogy to Eq. (
83
) for a “weak” symmetry
F
is

\displaystyle 0\,=\,\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\omega^{ab}(\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{b}+\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{b})F\,=\,(\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{b}F)\omega^{ab}\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{b}\,=\,\beta\{F,H\}~~~\implies~~~\{F,H\}=0\,,~~

(117)

which is precisely the same as the condition in Eq. (
116
) for a strong symmetry of dissipationless Hamiltonian dynamics. In such systems, the statement that
F
is a conserved quantity is equivalent to the condition
\{F,H\}=0
.

3.1.4
Noncanonical symplectic forms

The discussion above illustrates the utility of the EFT formalism we develop in Sec.
2
for Hamiltonian systems where both time evolution and the stationary distribution
\Phi
(
105
) are generated by the Hamiltonian
H
. In fact, the EFT description of systems with ordinary, canonical phase-space variables is especially simple. However, the EFT construction also holds for
nontrivial
Poisson brackets
\quantity{q_a,q_b}
—or, equivalently, a nontrivial (inverse) symplectic form
\omega^{ab}_{\,}
(
110
)—which are not of the form, e.g.,
\quantity{x_i,p_j}=\delta^{\,}_{i,j}
. Such nontrivial Poisson brackets are realized by, e.g., systems in which the dynamics are most directly captured by angular-momentum variables
\mathbf{L}
, the components of which satisfy
\quantity{L_i,L_j}=\epsilon_{ijk}\,L_{k}
. The implementation of the EFT formalism for such nontrivial Poisson brackets requires extra care; moreover, since the Poisson bracket is antisymmetric (i.e.,
\quantity{q_a,q_b}=-\quantity{q_b,q_a}
, one must be careful regarding the ordering of variables in Eq. (
109
). Additionally, when the symplectic form
\omega
is a nontrivial function of the phase-space coordinates, its Pfaffian
\sqrt{\omega}\equiv\sqrt{\det\omega}
(borrowing the convention of general relativity) is the prefactor of the volume form on phase space
6
6

6

The volume of a region
R
of phase space is
\int_{R}\omega^{\wedge Nd}=\int_{R}\sqrt{\omega}\,\mathrm{d}q_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathrm{d}q_{2Nd}
.
. In such scenarios, the stationary distribution (
105
) is modified according to

\Phi\to\beta\,H-\log\sqrt{\omega}\,,~~

(118)

and the “chemical potential” (
52
) changes to
\mu^{\,}_{a}=\beta\partial^{\,}_{a}H-\partial^{\,}_{a}\log\sqrt{\omega}
. However, we find that the
\log\sqrt{\omega}
contribution to
\Phi
—which affects the chemical potential
\mu^{\,}_{a}=\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\Phi
—is precisely cancelled by another term, so that the Fokker-Planck generator remains in the form of Eq. (
111
) where
\mu^{\,}_{a}=\beta\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}H
. To see this, note that

\displaystyle W\,

\displaystyle=\,T\,\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\,\omega^{ab}\,(\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{b}+\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{b})\,=\,T\,\left(\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{b}\omega^{ab}-\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\left[\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{b}\,\omega^{ab}\right]\right)+T\,\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\omega^{ab}\,\left[\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{b}\,\Phi\right]

\displaystyle=\,\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\,\omega^{ab}\,\left[\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{b}\,H\right]-T\,\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\left(\left[\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{b}\,\omega^{ab}\right]+\omega^{ab}\left[\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{b}\log\sqrt{\omega}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\,\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\,\omega^{ab}\,\left[\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{b}\,H\right]-T\,\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\omega}}\left[\sqrt{\omega}\,\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{b}\omega^{ab}+\omega^{ab}\,\frac{1}{2\,\sqrt{\omega}}\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{b}\det\omega\right]

\displaystyle=\,\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\,\omega^{ab}\,\left[\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{b}\,H\right]-T\,\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\omega}}\left[\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{b}\sqrt{\omega}\,\omega^{ab}\right]\,=\,\,\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\,\omega^{ab}\,\left[\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{b}\,H\right]\,,~~

(119)

where we used the fact that
\partial^{\,}_{b}\left(\sqrt{\omega}\,\omega^{ab}\right)=0
because the symplectic form
\omega
is closed. Note that Eq. (
119
) precisely reproduces Eq. (
111
) if the contribution of the
\omega
term in Eq. (
118
) to
\mu^{\,}_{b}=\partial^{\,}_{b}\Phi
is ignored. Hence, we generically allow
\omega^{ab}
to be nontrivial while taking
\mu^{\,}_{b}=\beta\,[\partial^{\,}_{b}H]
. Thus, our EFT framework is compatible with nontrivial symplectic forms
\omega
, even when they depend nontrivially on the coordinates
\mathbf{q}
. Moreover, the T-odd contribution
v_{a}=-\omega^{ab}\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{b}H
to the force
f_{a}
(
60
) satisfies the constraint of Eq. (
70
), as required.

We make one final remark for readers familiar with the path-integral formulation of Sec.
2.7
. Consider a T-invariant MSR Lagrangian (
101
) describing Hamiltonian dynamics; neglecting dissipation, one would write
L=\pi_{a}(\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{t}q_{a}-\omega^{ab}\partial^{\,}_{b}H)
. However, the MSR implementation of T (
97
) is
not
the same as the T operation in Eq. (
46
) in the operator language, because
\mu^{\,}_{a}\neq\beta\partial^{\,}_{a}H
,
even in Hamiltonian mechanics without dissipation
when
\omega
(
110
) is not constant! The correct T-invariant path integral is far more complicated, and discussed in App.
C
.

3.2
Dissipation towards a thermal state

Having explained how noise-free Hamiltonian mechanics is captured by the EFT formalism of Sec.
2
,
we now demonstrate how to add dissipation (i.e., noise sources) to the dynamics in a way that preserves both the thermal stationary state
\Phi=\beta H
(
105
) toward which the system relaxes and any symmetries. While dissipative relaxation to thermal equilibrium is well understood, we find that such dynamics are especially transparent in the EFT formalism of Sec.
2
using Fokker-Planck operators. A key benefit of understanding dissipative Hamiltonian dynamics through the EFT lens is that this builds intuition for the application to
active
systems in, e.g., Sec.
4
.

A key observation of Sec.
3.1
is that the operator
W
(
107
) corresponding to noiseless Hamiltonian dynamics is antisymmetric, yet T even; at the same time, Sec.
2
establishes that the symmetric part of
W
(
53
) corresponds to
dissipationless
dynamics, and is generally T even. In this sense, the EFT formalism prescribes that adding
dissipative
effects on top of the dynamics generated by Hamiltonian
H
—where the stationary distribution is given by
\Phi=\beta H
(
105
)—merely requires adding terms
W^{\prime}
to
W
(
107
) corresponding to
noise
, which generally preserve both T invariance and the stationary state
\Phi
. The allowed additions to
W
(
107
) are of the form

\displaystyle W^{\prime}\,=\,-\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}Q_{ab}\quantity(\pd{b} + \chempo{b})\,,~~

(120)

where
Q_{ab}(\mathbf{q})
(
57
) is a symmetric matrix that may, in general, depend on the variables
\mathbf{q}
, and must be positive definite for thermodynamic stability, and respects any residual symmetries (conservation laws) as needed.

The simplest such choice of
Q
—and the most common in the literature—is given by

Q_{ab}\,=\,\Delta\,\delta^{\,}_{a,b}\,,~~

(121)

for some constant
\Delta>0
, which corresponds to additive Gaussian noise in Eq. (
35
). In this case, the Langevin equation (
31
) may be interpreted in the Itô formalism as

\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{t}q_{a}\,=\,\quantity{ q_a, H}-\gamma\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}H+\xi_{a}(t)\,,~~

(122)

where
\gamma
is defined in terms of the noise-variance matrix
Q
(
121
) via

\expectationvalue{\xi_a(t) \xi_b(t')}\,=\,Q_{ab}\delta\left(t-t^{\prime}\right)\,=\,2\gamma T\delta^{\,}_{a,b}\delta\left(t-t^{\prime}\right)\,,~~

(123)

with
T=1/\beta
and
\gamma=\beta\Delta/2
. The relation between the noise strength
\Delta
in Eq. (
123
) and the damping term in
\gamma
in Eq. (
122
) is the
classical fluctuation-dissipation theorem at finite temperature
T
. This celebrated result recovers immediately and transparently upon applying the EFT framework of Sec.
2
to Hamiltonian dynamics with dissipation.

The foregoing results also extend to systems with
multiplicative
noise (
91
). We caution that such noise sources require care when dealing with the ordering of operators in, e.g., the Fokker-Planck equation (
34
). Obtaining a fluctuation-dissipation theorem (
123
)—even for thermal stationary states
\Phi=\beta H
(
105
)—requires that one first write down the dissipative correction
W^{\prime}
(
120
) in the form of Fokker-Planck generator
W
(
53
), and then, if desired, rewrites that same generator
W^{\prime}
in the Itô (or other preferred) formulation of stochastic calculus (see also Sec.
2.7.2
).

3.3
Classical harmonic oscillator

We now consider several pedagogical examples of classical dynamical systems in the presence of dissipation, using the EFT formalism of Sec.
2
. In particular, we showcase how that formalism streamlines the derivation of several well-known models of dissipative dynamics (an apparently novel example appears in Sec.
6
).

We first apply the EFT formalism described above to one of the simplest classical systems imaginable—the harmonic oscillator in
d
spatial dimensions with frequency
\Omega=\sqrt{\kappa/m}
. This system is described by the canonical phase-space coordinates
\mathbf{q}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{d},p_{1},\dots,p_{d})
, and we couple this system to a general thermal bath, which induces dissipation. For convenience, we define the differential operators
\partial^{\,}_{a}\equiv\partialderivative*{x_a}
and
D_{a}\equiv\partialderivative*{p_a}
, and let
a\in\{1,2,\dots,d\}
.

3.3.1
Overdamped limit

We first consider the overdamped limit, which corresponds to the model discussed in Sec.
1.4
of the introduction. In this limit, the kinetic energy of the oscillator is negligible, so the stationary distribution (
105
) can be written

\Phi(\mathbf{q})\,\to\,\beta V({\bf\it x})\,=\,\frac{1}{2}\beta m\Omega^{2}x^{2}_{a}\,,~~

(124)

where summation over
a
is implicit, and for clarity, we assume an isotropic potential. Restricting to Gaussian white noise (
121
), the generator
W
(
107
) corresponding to T-even relaxation to
\Phi
(
124
) is given by

W\,=\,-T\gamma^{\prime}\,\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\left(\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}+\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\right)\,,~~

(125)

which can be straightforwardly recast as an SDE (
31
) in the Itô prescription,

\displaystyle\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{t}x_{a}\,=\,-\gamma^{\prime}m\Omega^{2}x_{a}+\xi_{a}(t)\,,~~

(126)

where the noise term
\xi_{a}(t)
obeys Eq. (
121
) and the FDT (
123
), and
\gamma^{\prime}=\beta\Delta/2
with
\Delta
the noise strength.
The result is the natural generalization of the overdamped harmonic oscillator previewed in Sec.
1
.

3.3.2
Finite-mass limit

We next consider the limit of one or more
d
-dimensional oscillators in which the kinetic energy is no longer negligible (the overdamped limit can be viewed as taking
m\to\infty
). The corresponding stationary distribution
\Phi
(
105
) is

\Phi\,=\,\beta\left[\frac{p^{2}_{a}}{2m}+\frac{1}{2}m\Omega^{2}x^{2}_{a}\right]\,,~~

(127)

where summation over
a
is implied, the coordinates satisfy the Poisson brackets
\quantity{x_a,p_b}=\delta^{\,}_{a,b}
, and we define

\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\,\equiv\,\partialderivative{\Phi}{x^{\,}_a}\,=\,\beta m\Omega^{2}x_{a}~~~\text{and}~~~\nu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\,\equiv\,\partialderivative{\Phi}{p_a}\,=\,\beta p_{a}/m\,,~~

(128)

as the two flavors of chemical potentials. Assuming that only the force driving the oscillator is random (i.e., that noise only couples directly to the momentum
{\bf\it p}
),
the Fokker-Planck generator (
107
) is given by

W\,=\,T\left[\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\nu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}-D_{a}\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\right]-T\,\gamma\,D_{a}\left(D_{a}+\nu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\right)\,,~~

(129)

and we note that
W
(
129
) is T symmetric and of the form of Eq. (
53
).
The equations of motion for the positions and momenta recover straightforwardly from
W
(
129
); in the Itô SDE form, they are

\displaystyle\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{t}x_{a}\,=\,\frac{p_{a}}{m}~~~~\text{and}~~~~\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{t}p_{a}\,=\,-m\Omega^{2}x_{a}-\frac{\gamma}{m}p_{a}+\xi_{a}(t)\,,~~

(130)

where the noise
\xi_{a}
obeys Eq. (
123
), and we note that
\gamma
has different units than in the overdamped case in Sec.
3.3.1
.

3.4
Vortex dynamics in superfluid thin films

We now consider a nontrivial example corresponding to the dynamics of point vortices in superfluid thin films
Onsager 1949
;
Newton 2011
;
Pines et al.
. Such systems are described by Hamiltonian mechanics in which the dynamical variables are the positions
\mathbf{r}_{i}=(x_{i},y_{i})
of the vortices, which have quantized circulations
\Gamma_{i}=n_{i}\hbar/m
, and the coordinates
x_{i}
and
y_{i}
are
canonically conjugate

Reeves et al. 2022
;
Newton 2011
. The kinetic energy of the vortices is given in terms of the coordinates
\mathbf{r}_{i}
via

\displaystyle H\,=-\sum_{i<j}\frac{\rho^{\,}_{0}\Gamma_{i}\Gamma_{j}}{4\pi}\log(\abs{ \vb{r}_i-\vb{r}_j } / R)

(131)

where
\rho^{\,}_{0}
is the mass density of the 2D superfluid and
R
is an arbitrary constant with units of length. We note that the Hamiltonian (
131
) has the form of the Coulomb interaction in two spatial dimensions.

The equations of motion follow from the following Poisson bracket for the canonical coordinates
x_{i}
and
y_{i}
,

\displaystyle\{x_{i},y_{j}\}\,=\,\Gamma_{i}^{-1}\delta^{\,}_{i,j}\,,~~

(132)

and writing
\mathbf{q}=(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{N},y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{N})
, the canonical dissipationless equations of motion are

\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{t}q_{a}\,=\,\omega^{ab}\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{b}\,\equiv\,u_{a}\,,~~

(133)

where
\omega
is the inverse symplectic form (
110
) coupling
x
and
y
coordinates and
u_{a}
corresponds to the
x
or
y
component of the superfluid velocity field at vortex
i
’s location. This leads to the well known Hamiltonian equations of motion

\Gamma_{i}\dot{x}_{i}\,=\,\partialderivative{H}{y_i}~~~~{\rm and}~~~~\Gamma_{i}\dot{y}_{i}\,=\,-\partialderivative{H}{x_i}\,,~~

(134)

where there is no sum over
i
. We now introduce dissipation, so that the noisy equations take the form

\displaystyle\dot{q}_{a}\,=\,u_{a}+G_{a}+\xi_{a}(t)\,,~~

(135)

where
G_{a}
is the dissipative part of the force
f_{a}
(
60
), and we take
\xi_{a}(t)
to be Gaussian white noise with strength
\Delta
(
121
). Then the only dissipative term
G_{a}
we may include is of the form

\displaystyle G_{a}\,=-\frac{1}{2}Q_{ab}\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{b}\,=-\Delta\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\,=-\beta\Delta\Gamma_{a}\epsilon^{ab}u_{b}\,,~~

(136)

where there is no sum over the label
a
, and
\mu_{a}
and
u_{a}
are defined in (
133
). We next define the
mutual friction coefficient

\gamma=\beta\Delta
. Including the dissipative correction
G_{a}
(
136
), the equations of motion (
134
) for the coordinate
\mathbf{r}_{i}=(x_{i},y_{i})
becomes

\dot{\mathbf{r}}_{i}\,=\,\mathbf{u}_{i}-\gamma\,\hat{\mathbf{z}}\times\Gamma_{i}\,\mathbf{u}_{i}+\xi_{i}(t)\,,~~

(137)

where
\mathbf{u}_{i}
is the superfluid velocity at the point
\mathbf{r}_{i}
. The second term above corresponds to the
mutual friction
that is common to the literature on point-vortex models
Ambegaokar et al. 1980
.

It is illustrative to compare the derivation above to that of Ref.
71
, which argued that the equations of motion for a point vortex should be local in the superfluid velocity
\mathbf{u}_{i}
and reduce to
\dot{\mathbf{r}}_{i}=\mathbf{u}_{i}
in the absence of dissipation. The simplest such equation of motion is Eq. (
137
), where the dependence on
\Gamma^{\,}_{i}
in the dissipative term is required for the two terms in Eq. (
137
) to transform equivalently under parity. While the derivation in Ref.
71
is quicker than the one above, it has two potential sources of confusion. First, the correct choice of sign
\gamma>0
in Eq. (
137
) follows straightforwardly from positive semi-definiteness of
Q
(
35
), but it is not obvious starting from Eq. (
137
) why we must have
\gamma>0
. Second, in the presence of additive Gaussian noise, it is not clear from Eq. (
137
) alone how the resulting dynamics are consistent with a thermal distribution at finite temperature
T
with the Hamiltonian (
131
). However, both of these properties are built into the stochastic EFT formalism of Sec.
2
.

In principle, our framework could also apply to the more complicated mutual friction arising in the dynamics of three-dimensional vortex rings, using the field-theoretic generalization of our EFT framework described in Sec.
7
, or to constrained vortex dynamics
Qi and Lucas 2023
.

3.5
Landau-Lifshitz-Gilbert spin dynamics

Another interesting application of our formalism is to the classical Heisenberg spin chain
Heisenberg 1928
;
Girvin and Yang 2019
;
Altland and Simons 2010
. This model also provides a nontrivial application of the generalized Noether’s theorem (
80
) in Sec.
2.6
. For simplicity, consider a single classical spin
\mathbf{S}
interacting with a magnetic field
\mathbf{B}
in
d=3
spatial dimensions; the corresponding Hamiltonian is

\displaystyle H\,=\,\gamma\,S_{i}\,B_{i}\,,~~

(138)

where
\gamma=g\mu^{\,}_{B}/\hbar
is the gyromagnetic ratio of the spin, with
g
the Landé
g
-factor for an electron and
\mu^{\,}_{B}=e\hbar/2m_{e}
the Bohr magneton. Again, we take
\Phi=\beta H
(
105
), and note that each spin component
S_{i}
corresponds to the chemical potential
\mu^{\,}_{i}=\beta\partialderivative*{H}{S_i}=\beta\,\gamma B_{i}
(
52
). Additionally, the spin components satisfy the nontrivial Poisson brackets

\displaystyle\quantity{S_i,S_j}\,=\,\epsilon^{ijk}\,S_{k}\,,~~

(139)

so that dissipationless (Hamiltonian) evolution is generated by the Fokker-Planck operator (
107
),

W\,=-\gamma\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{i}\epsilon^{ijk}\,S_{j}\,B_{k}\,,~~

(140)

where, as a reminder,
W
is a differential operator, so
\partial^{\,}_{i}
continues to act to the right of
W
.

We now add dissipation to the dynamics generated by
W
(
140
) while preserving a notion of time-reversal invariance. Although
W
(
140
) itself is invariant under T, we only require that the
dissipative
terms
W^{\prime}
we add thereto preserve a
generalized

\mathbb{Z}^{\,}_{2}
symmetry gT corresponding to the
combination
of time reversal
\cal T
(or T) and parity
\cal P
, as one expects of dynamics in the presence of a magnetic field
\mathbf{B}
. We note that the gT transformation
\cal PT
is itself a symmetry of the original
W
(
140
). The simplest dissipative correction
W^{\prime}
that respects this choice of gT is

\displaystyle W^{\prime}\,=\,\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{i}Q_{ij}(\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{j}+\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{j})\,,~~

(141)

where
Q_{ij}
(
35
) is the (symmetric) noise matrix. However, we must further impose the conservation of the norm of the spin
M=\mathbf{S}\cdot\mathbf{S}=S_{i}S_{i}
. Following Eq. (
80
) of Sec.
2.6
, we find that
W
must be invariant under the transformation

\displaystyle\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{i}\,\to\,\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{i}+\partialderivative{M}{S_i}\,=\,\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{i}+2S_{i}\,,~~

(142)

which is
not
satisfied by a constant matrix
Q_{ij}
. However, this can be corrected by noticing that
\epsilon^{ijk}\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{j}S_{k}
is invariant under the shift
\partial^{\,}_{i}\to\partial^{\,}_{i}+S_{i}
. Hence, we should build the matrix
Q_{ij}
(
35
) out of these invariant objects. We then find that the simplest dissipative term that may be added to
W
(
140
) corresponds to

\displaystyle W^{\prime}\,=-\Delta\,\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{i}\epsilon^{ijk}\,S_{j}\epsilon^{klm}\,S_{l}(\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{m}+\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{m})\,,~~

(143)

for some constant
\Delta>0
such that
Q_{im}
is positive semidefinite. To gain some intuition, consider the contribution of the
\Delta
term in Eq. (
143
) to the equation of motion for the spin,

\displaystyle\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{t}S_{i}\,=\,\gamma\,\epsilon^{ijk}\,S_{j}\,B_{k}+\Delta\,\epsilon^{ijk}\,\epsilon^{klm}\,S_{j}S_{l}\,\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{m}+\eta_{i}(\mathbf{S},t)\,,~~

(144)

where
\eta_{i}
is a multiplicative noise source with variance matrix (
143
)

\displaystyle Q_{ij}(\mathbf{S})\,=\,\Delta\,\epsilon^{imk}\,S_{j}\,\epsilon^{klj}\,S_{l}\,,~~

(145)

which is somewhat complicated, but required for
\absolutevalue{\vb{S}}^{2}
to be conserved as a strong symmetry (i.e., for
every
realization of the noise). Finally, we define
\lambda=\beta\,\Delta\,\gamma
and recast Eq. (
144
) into the vector form

\displaystyle\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{t}\mathbf{S}\,=\,-\gamma\mathbf{S}\times\mathbf{B}-\lambda\,\mathbf{S}\times(\mathbf{S}\times\mathbf{B})+{\bf\it\xi}(\mathbf{S},t)\,,~~

(146)

known as the the
Landau-Lifshitz
equation, where the term with coefficient
\lambda
is the
Landau-Lifshitz-Gilbert
damping term
Landau and Lifshitz 1935
;
Gilbert 1955
. Hence, this celebrated nonlinear phenomenological model follows straightforwardly from the EFT “cookbook” principles outlined in Sec.
2
using only knowledge of the stationary state (
138
) and symmetries.

Again, it is instructive to compare our derivation with the usual approach starting from the phenomenological equation of motion (
146
). One would first note that
\partial^{\,}_{t}\mathbf{S}\cdot\mathbf{S}=0
is guaranteed if

\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{t}\mathbf{S}\,=\,\mathbf{S}\times\mathbf{g}\,

(147)

for some vector
\mathbf{g}
, which may include both determinstic and stochastic contributions. For the system to be in equilibrium when
\mathbf{S}
is parallel to
\mathbf{B}
, we must choose
\mathbf{g}
such that
\mathbf{S}\times\mathbf{g}
vanishes when
\mathbf{S}\propto\mathbf{B}
are parallel. Writing

\mathbf{g}\,=\,\gamma\mathbf{B}+\lambda\mathbf{S}\times\mathbf{B}+\cdots\,,~~

(148)

reproduces Eq. (
146
). The FDT (
123
) can then be recovered by linearizing Eq. (
146
) around a configuration where
\mathbf{S}\propto\mathbf{B}
are parallel. In our view, the advantage of the Wilsonian EFT framework for stochastic systems that we develop in Sec.
2
is that it can be applied more
algorithmically
. Using more traditional methods instead requires starting with a phenomenological equation of motion and then working through a sequence of physical conditions and assumptions to constrain Eq. (
146
). In the EFT formalism, one merely specifies a stationary distribution (
138
) and a set of symmetries, from which all allowed terms recover using the prescription of Sec.
2
.

4
From thermal to active matter

We now consider additional examples and applications of the EFT formalism presented in Sec.
2
, with an eye toward the description of
active
matter. In particular, we consider systems for which the dynamics does
not
result from Hamiltonian mechanics, in contrast to the examples of Sec.
3

4.1
An “odd” two-dimensional harmonic oscillator

The simple harmonic oscillator is a useful toy model of equilibrium (thermal) physics, and is thus a useful starting point for examining physics
near
equilibrium. We note that, in
d=1
, there is only one nontrivial choice of the dissipationless term
V
in Eq. (
57
); hence, we look for simple examples of non-Hamiltonian dynamics in a two-dimensional oscillator. While the standard approach in studying nonthermal systems is to start by writing down an equation of notion, we note that there is no guarantee of either a stationary state nor stable solutions. Instead, we use the EFT framework developed in Sec.
2
and start by specifying the stationary distribution
\Phi
(
41
), which guarantees stability, and derive constraints on the equations of motion compatible with
\Phi
.

As a reminder, a harmonic oscillator (in any number of spatial dimensions) that relaxes to the equilibrium stationary state
\Phi=\beta H
(
105
) with
H=(\mathbf{p}^{2}+m^{2}\Omega^{2}\mathbf{x}^{2})/2m
(
127
) is captured by the Fokker-Planck generator

W\,=\,T\left[\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\nu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}-D_{a}\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\right]-T\gamma D_{a}\left(D_{a}+\nu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\right)\,,~~

(
129
)

and there are a number of terms that one could add to the generator
W
(
129
) to make the system appear “active.”

Here, we consider adding terms such that the equation of motion for
\partial^{\,}_{t}x_{a}
(
130
) is a function not only of the momentum
p_{a}
, but the position
x_{a}
. We also add correlations in the noise sources for
x_{a}
and
p_{a}
in
Q
(
35
). We add to the thermal (dissipative) Fokker-Planck generator
W
(
129
) the following “active” terms,

\displaystyle W^{\prime}\,

\displaystyle=\,-T\,\lambda\,\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}(\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}+\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a})-T\,\kappa\,\epsilon^{ab}\,(2\,\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}D_{b}+\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\nu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{b}-D_{a}\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{b})-V\,\epsilon^{ab}\,\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{b}-V^{\prime}\epsilon^{ab}\,D_{a}\nu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{b}\,,~~

(149)

where the parameters
\gamma
,
\lambda
, and
\kappa
capture correlations of noise sources
{\bf\it\xi}
and
{\bf\it\zeta}
via

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{ \zeta_a(t) \zeta_b(t') }\,

\displaystyle=\,2\,T\,\gamma\,\delta^{\,}_{a,b}\,\delta\left(t-t^{\prime}\right)\,

(150a)

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{ \xi_a(t) \xi_b(t') }\,

\displaystyle=\,2\,T\,\lambda\,\delta^{\,}_{a,b}\,\delta\left(t-t^{\prime}\right)

(150b)

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{ \xi_a(t)\zeta_b(t') }\,

\displaystyle=\,2\,T\,\kappa\,\epsilon^{\,}_{a,b}\,\delta\left(t-t^{\prime}\right)\,,~~

(150c)

and since the total variance matrix
Q
(
35
) must be positive semidefinite, we must have

\displaystyle\lambda\,>\,0,~~\gamma\,>\,0,~~~{\rm and}~~~\gamma\lambda\,>\,\kappa^{2}\,,~~

(151)

and we note that the couplings
V
and
V^{\prime}
are not constrained. We also note that,
a priori
, starting from the equation of motion (
152
)
without
noise, the constraint of Eq. (
151
) is ambiguous.

The Fokker-Planck generator
W+W^{\prime}
gives rise to the following SDE (
31
) in the Itô prescription,

\displaystyle m\,\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{t}x_{a}\,

\displaystyle=\,p_{a}-\lambda\,m^{2}\,\Omega^{2}\,x_{a}-\beta\,m^{2}\,\Omega^{2}\,V\epsilon^{ab}\,x_{b}-\kappa\,\epsilon^{ab}\,p_{b}+\xi_{a}(t)

(152a)

\displaystyle\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{t}p_{a}\,

\displaystyle=\,-m\,\Omega^{2}\,x_{a}-\frac{\gamma}{m}\,p_{a}-\frac{\beta}{m}\,V^{\prime}\,\epsilon^{ab}\,p_{b}+\kappa\,m\,\Omega^{2}\,\epsilon^{ab}\,x_{b}+\zeta_{a}(t)\,,~~

(152b)

which describes a system with a
generalized
time-reversal symmetry gT corresponding to
\cal PT
, where the parity operation
\cal P
sends
\epsilon_{ab}\to-\epsilon_{ab}
. The generator
W^{\prime}
(
149
) includes T-odd terms (with coefficients
V
and
V^{\prime}
) that lead to
dissipationless
rotations in position and momentum space. Notice that it is possible to obtain both
V,V^{\prime}
terms in
thermal
systems in which a modified Poisson bracket due to the presence of a magnetic field and Berry curvature, which correspond to
V^{\prime}
and
V
, respectively
Xiao et al. 2010
.
On the other hand, the
dissipative
T-odd term proportional to
\kappa
in Eq. (
152b
) realizes the “odd” force that has recently been investigated in active systems
Brandenbourger et al. 2021
.

To map the model described above to that of
Brandenbourger et al. 2021
, we set
V=V^{\prime}=0
for simplicity, so that Eq. (
152
) takes the form of the following second-order differential equation for
x_{a}
,

\displaystyle\big(\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{t}+\frac{\gamma}{m}\big)^{2}x_{a}\,=-\big(1-\kappa^{2}\big)\,\Omega^{2}\,x_{a}-\big(\lambda\,m\,\Omega^{2}-\frac{\gamma}{m}\big)\big(\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{t}+\frac{\gamma}{m}\big)\,x_{a}+2\,\kappa\,\Omega^{2}\,\epsilon^{ab}\,x_{b}\,,~~

(153)

where the final term (proportional to
\kappa
) realizes the “odd” force, as in Ref.
78
. The normal modes are given by

\displaystyle\omega\,=\,-\mathrm{i}\frac{\lambda\,m^{2}\,\Omega^{2}+\gamma}{2\,m}\pm\frac{1}{2\,m}\,\sqrt{4(1-\kappa^{2})\,m^{2}\,\Omega^{2}-(\lambda\,m^{2}\,\Omega^{2}-\gamma)^{2}\pm\mathrm{i}\,8\,\kappa\,m^{2}\,\Omega^{2}}\,,~~

(154)

whose imaginary part is given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{Im}~\omega\,=\,-\frac{\lambda\,m^{2}\,\Omega^{2}+\gamma}{2\,m}+\frac{1}{2\,m}\,\mathrm{Re}~\,\sqrt{(\lambda m^{2}\Omega^{2}-\gamma)^{2}-4(1-\kappa^{2})\,m^{2}\,\Omega^{2}+\mathrm{i}\,8\,\kappa\,m^{2}\,\Omega^{2}}

(155)

and is a monotonically increasing function of
\kappa
; by the constraint of Eq. (
151
), we have that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Im}~\omega\,\leq\,-\frac{\lambda\,m^{2}\,\Omega^{2}+\gamma}{2\,m}+\frac{1}{2\,m}\,\mathrm{Re}~\,\sqrt{(\lambda\,m^{2}\,\Omega^{2}+\gamma)^{2}-4\,m^{2}\,\Omega^{2}+\mathrm{i}\,8\,\sqrt{\gamma\lambda}\,m^{2}\,\Omega^{2}}\,,~~

(156)

where the right-hand side is maximized when
\lambda\,m^{2}\,\Omega^{2}=\gamma
, leading to

\displaystyle\mathrm{Im}~\omega\,\leq\,-\lambda\,m\,\Omega^{2}+\mathrm{Re}~\,\sqrt{(\lambda m\,\Omega^{2}+\mathrm{i}\Omega)^{2}}\,=\,0\,,~~

(157)

so that the constraints of Eq. (
151
) imposed by positivity of the noise-variance matrix
Q
(
150
) imply
stability
of the normal modes (i.e.,
\mathrm{Im}~\omega\leq 0
). The dynamics described by Eq. (
153
) correspond to the decaying mode in Ref.
78
.

We next define the dimensionless parameter

\theta\,\equiv\,2\,m\,\kappa\,\Omega/(\lambda\,m^{2}\,\Omega^{2}+\gamma)\,,~~

(158)

which diagnoses whether Eq. (
153
) realizes decaying motion (
\absolutevalue{\theta}\leq 1
) or limit-cycle motion (
\absolutevalue{\theta}>1
) in Ref.
78
. When
\absolutevalue{\theta}\leq 1
, the dissipation dominates, so that the system relaxes to a stationary configuration; when
\absolutevalue{\theta}>1
, the combination of dissipation and activity lead to nonlinear dynamics, resulting in periodic, limit-cycle behavior. Crucially, we find that the constraint
\kappa^{2}\leq\gamma\,\lambda
(
151
) from positive semidefiniteness of
Q
(
150
) implies that

\displaystyle\theta^{2}\,\leq\,1-\left(\frac{\lambda\,m^{2}\,\Omega^{2}-\gamma}{\lambda\,m^{2}\,\Omega^{2}+\gamma}\right)^{2}\,\leq 1\,,~~

(159)

which further implies that
\absolutevalue{\theta}\leq 1
for the equation of motion (
153
) for valid sources of noise (
150
), which must obey Eq. (
151
). In other words, the EFT perspective naturally gives rise
only
to the decaying motion observed in Ref.
78
. Realizing the limit-cycle motion of Ref.
78
in an EFT requires identifying a stationary distribution
\Phi
other than that of Eq. (
127
); in particular, that
\Phi
must contain cubic (or higher) terms, so that Eqs. (
152
) are
nonlinear
.

4.2
Nonreciprocal Kuramoto model

For a more interesting realization of
active
matter, we now consider the
nonreciprocal Kuramoto model

Fruchart et al. 2021
, corresponding to classical spins on a lattice coupled via “nonreciprocal” interactions. This model has a rich phenomenology and phase structure, and even hosts a spinning chiral phase that spontaneously breaks a
generalized
time-reversal symmetry gT
Fruchart et al. 2021
. Using the EFT framework developed in Sec.
2
, we first review the equilibrium Kuramoto model of
synchronization

Acebrón et al. 2005
before incorporating nonreciprocal interactions to realize an
active
analogue
Fruchart et al. 2021
. Indeed, it is this section precisely which motivates the schematic summary of our approach in Fig.
1
.

4.2.1
The equilibrium (reciprocal) Kuramoto model

The phenomenon of
synchronization
appears in various areas of biology, chemistry, and even the social sciences, and is distinguished by the alignment of the periodic phase variables describing a large number of agents. A simple model that realizes this phenomenon is the noisy, equilibrium
Kuramoto model

Kuramoto 1975
;
Kuramoto 1984
;
Strogatz 2000
;
Acebrón et al. 2005
: The synchronizing agents correspond to stationary rotors described by an angle
\theta^{\,}_{a}
. We now consider the simple implementation of the Kuramoto model in which the angles
\{\theta^{\,}_{a}\}
are described by a nonlinear SDE (
31
) of the form

\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{t}\theta_{a}\,=\,\sum\limits_{a,b}J_{ab}\sin(\theta_a - \theta_b)+\xi_{a}(t)\,,~~

(160)

where
J
is a matrix of coupling strengths
J_{ab}
between distinct rotors
\theta_{a}
and
\theta_{b}
, and
{\bf\it\xi}(t)
is a noise source.

The
equilibrium
variant of the stochastic model in Eq. (
160
) has been studied extensively in the literature, and corresponds to a
symmetric
coupling matrix
J_{ab}=J_{ba}
. This model hosts two stationary phases, analogous to those of the classical XY model: A symmetry-breaking “synchronized” phase at weak noise
\Delta<\Delta_{c}\sim 1/N
, where
N
is the number of rotors (analogous to a low-temperature ferromagnet for
\beta<\beta_{c}
), and a “disordered” phase at strong noise
\Delta>\Delta_{c}
(analogous to a high-temperature paramagnet for
\beta>\beta_{c}
)
Strogatz 2000
. We now review the phenomenology of the equilibrium Kuramoto model from the perspective of the EFT formalism developed in Sec.
2
.

The starting point of the EFT is the stationary distribution
\Phi
; a natural guess is the XY-like “Hamiltonian,”

\displaystyle\Phi\,=-\frac{1}{2\,\Delta}\sum\limits_{a,b=1}^{N}J_{ab}\,\cos(\theta_a - \theta_b)\,,~~

(161)

where
J
is a symmetric matrix and
\Delta
captures the noise strength. This is the simplest form of
\Phi
consistent with rotational invariance, and is essentially a classical
2d
XY model . For convenience, we take the noise
{\bf\it\xi}
to be additive and Gaussian so that
Q_{ab}=\Delta\delta^{\,}_{a,b}
(
121
).
The chemical potential
\mu^{\,}_{a}
corresponding to
\theta^{\,}_{a}
is

\displaystyle\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\,=\,\frac{1}{\Delta}\sum\limits_{b=1}^{N}J_{ab}\,\sin(\theta_a - \theta_b)\,,~~

(162)

where the factor of
1/2
is cancelled by the sums over all
a,b
in Eq. (
161
). We then recover the Fokker-Planck generator
W
(
37
), which we choose to be invariant (i.e., even) under T (
46
), giving

\displaystyle W\,=-\frac{1}{2}\,\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\,{\cal Q}_{ab}\,\left(\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{b}+\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{b}\right)\,=-\frac{\Delta}{2}\,\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\left(\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}+\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}\right)\,,~~

(163)

so that the resulting equation of motion is (
160
)
Acebrón et al. 2005
. The model is “reciprocal” in that
J
is symmetric in
a\leftrightarrow b
.

From our EFT perspective, this variant of the Kuramoto model—with stationary state (
161
) and equation of motion (
160
)—is an
equilibrium
model that realizes nonthermal physics. The model is “equilibrium” in that it admits the stationary state
\Phi
(
161
