), and the corresponding Fokker-Planck generator
W
(
163
) is T even (i.e., it obeys detailed balance). However, the physics realized by this model—i.e., the dynamics and phase structure—are
not
captured by a stationary distribution
\Phi=\beta H
(
105
) at temperature
T=1/\beta
, where
H
is related to
W
. In other words, the system is not captured by Hamiltonian evolution as described in Sec.
3
, and the stationary state
\Phi
(
161
) does not correspond to a thermal distribution
\Phi=-\mathrm{i}\beta W
for the corresponding generator
W
(
163
).

Instead, assuming sufficiently long-ranged interactions, the equilibrium Kuramoto model (
163
) generically displays a nonthermal phase transition at some critical noise strength
\Delta_{c}
. To see this, we first change variables via

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}_{a}\,\equiv\,\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta_{a}\\
\sin\theta_{a}\end{pmatrix}~~~~\implies~~~~\Phi\,=-\frac{1}{2\,\Delta}\sum_{a,b=1}^{N}\,J_{ab}\,\mathbf{S}_{a}\cdot\mathbf{S}_{b}\,,~~

(164)

where
\mathbf{S}_{a}
is the two-component classical spin for the
a
th rotor. In this language, the
order parameter
that captures alignment of the rotors in the “low-temperature” phase can be written simply as

\mathbf{m}\,\equiv\,\frac{1}{N}\sum_{a=1}^{N}\,\expectationvalue{\vb{S}_a}\,,~~

(165)

where
\mathbf{m}
is analogous to the
magnetization
in an XY model, and its magnitude is
m=\absolutevalue{\vb{m}}
.

For a concrete example, consider the all-to-all Kuramoto model, where
J_{ab}=J/N
, connects all
N
rotors with the same strength. Using mean field theory—which is exact in this effectively infinite-dimensional system—we write
\mathbf{S}_{a}=\expectationvalue{\vb{S}_a}+\delta\mathbf{S}_{a}
, where
\expectationvalue{\vb{S}_a}
is the mean-field (equilibrium) value, and we discard all terms that are nonlinear in the fluctuations
\delta\mathbf{S}_{a}=\mathbf{S}_{a}-\expectationvalue{\vb{S}_a}
. The corresponding mean-field stationary state
\Phi
, at linear order in fluctuations, is

\displaystyle\Phi_{\rm MFT}\,=-\frac{J}{2N\Delta}\sum_{a,b}\,\expectationvalue{\vb{S}_a}\cdot\left(\expectationvalue{\vb{S}_b}+2\,\delta\mathbf{S}_{b}\right)\,,~~

(166)

and assuming a spatially uniform state with
\expectationvalue{\vb{S}_a}=m\,\hat{\mathbf{x}}=\mathbf{S}_{0}
for all rotors
a
, we seek a self-consistency condition for a nonzero mean-field magnetization
m
(
165
). The corresponding mean-field stationary state (
166
) is simply

\displaystyle\Phi_{\rm MFT}\,=-\frac{J}{2\Delta}\sum\limits_{a,b}\,m\,\left(m\,+2\left[\cos\theta_{a}-m\right]\right)\,=\,\frac{m^{2}\,J\,N}{2\Delta}-\frac{J\,m}{\Delta}\sum_{a}\,\cos\theta_{a}\,,~~

(167)

at lowest order in the fluctuations. The self-consistency condition follows from evaluating
m=\mathbf{m}_{x}=\sum_{a}\expectationvalue{\cos\theta_a}/N
in the uniform stationary state (
167
); defining
\lambda=mJ/\Delta
, we note that
m=\expectationvalue{\cos\theta_a}
for all rotors
a
, giving

\displaystyle m\,=\,\frac{\int\differential{\theta}\cos(\theta)\,\mathrm{e}^{\lambda\cos\theta}}{\int\differential{\theta}\mathrm{e}^{\lambda\cos\theta}}\,=\frac{I_{1}(\lambda)}{I_{0}(\lambda)}\,\approx\,\frac{J}{2\,\Delta}m-\frac{J^{3}}{16\,\Delta^{3}}m^{3}\,,~~

(168)

to cubic order in
m
, where
I_{n}(\lambda)
is a modified Bessel function of the first kind. This expression admits a solution

m\,=\,\frac{2\,\Delta}{J}\,\left(\frac{4\,\Delta}{J}-2\right)^{1/2}>0~~~~\mathrm{for}~~~~\Delta\,<\,\Delta_{c}\,=\,J/2\,,~~

(169)

so that a nontrivial order—in which all rotors are aligned, captured by
m>0
(
165
)—realizes for
\Delta<\Delta_{c}
.

Essentially, the critical noise strength
\Delta_{c}
the rotors align in a spontaneously chosen direction so long as
J>0
. Above
\Delta_{c}
, the system is disordered, with average angle
\expectationvalue{\theta_a}=0
. These features are built into
\Phi
(
161
), corresponds to the XY Hamiltonian with all-to-all interactions, which has precisely this ordering transition in equilibrium statistical mechanics. Since
\Phi\propto 1/\Delta
, the noise
\Delta
is analogous to the temperature
T
, and the low-noise order for
\Delta<\Delta_{c}
is analogous to a low-temperature XY ferromagnet, with
\exp(-\Phi)
maximized on the manifold in which all rotors are aligned. Even away from the all-to-all limit considered above, the thermodynamics of this Kuramoto model is the same as that of a classical XY model in thermal equilibrium, with
\Delta
analogous to
T
.

4.2.2
Nonreciprocity and generalized time-reversal symmetry

The equilibrium Kuramoto model described by Eq. (
160
) can also be generalized to the
nonreciprocal
case
Fruchart et al. 2021
, via

\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{t}\theta_{a}\,=\,\sum\limits_{b}K_{ab}\,\sin(\theta_a - \theta_b)+\xi_{a}(t)\,,~~

(170)

where
K_{ab}\neq K_{ba}
is nonsymmetric. Compared to the equilibrium version (
160
), the SDE (
170
) does
not
recover from a Hamiltonian stationary distribution
\Phi=\beta H
(
105
), and thus cannot be realized via Hamiltonian dynamics (see Sec.
3
). Compared to the equilibrium phase structure
Acebrón et al. 2005
, the nonreciprocal Kuramoto model
Fruchart et al. 2021
admits an additional,
chiral
phase in which all rotors
\theta_{a}
spin at a fixed rate about a spontaneously chosen axis.

From our EFT perspective, the difference between the nonreciprocal and equilibrium Kuramoto models relates to their respective
symmetries
. In particular, the nonreciprocal Kuramoto model (
170
) breaks detailed balance (i.e., is not T even) while respecting a
generalized
time-reversal symmetry gT: the product of the reversibility transformation T (
46
) and a
\mathbb{Z}^{\,}_{2}
“chirality” transformation
P
that swaps the sign of the interaction term in
W
or (
170
).

We now explain how to reproduce the phase transition to the chiral phase of Ref.
36
within our EFT formalism. We note that this formalism does not reproduce exactly the equations of motion of Ref.
36
. This is due to a lack of analytic control: The distribution
\Phi
(
41
) that leads to Eq. (
170
) is not known, and may be arbitrarily complicated. Instead, we start with a plausible guess for
\Phi
and derive the allowed T-odd terms and resulting equations of motion that reproduce the desired phase transition.

We first sketch a choice of
\Phi
that leads to nonreciprocal dynamics qualitatively similar to that of Eq. (
170
). This
\Phi
admits a phase with spontaneous symmetry breaking (SSB) of gT; in the SSB phase, the configuration that minimizes
\Phi
itself breaks gT (i.e.,
PT
), so that the dynamics of particular trajectories does not explore the entire low-energy manifold, and applying the gT operation to a particular trajectory
{\bf\it\theta}(t)
leads to a trajectory
{\bf\it\theta}^{\prime}(t)
that has negligible overlap with
{\bf\it\theta}(t)
. Fortunately, it is straightforward to concoct such a
\Phi
. Consider the
equilibrium
stationary distribution
\Phi
(
161
), but suppose that there are two, distinguishable species of rotors labeled
A
and
B
. We modify
\Phi
(
161
) by including a term that is quadratic in
\cos(\theta_a - \theta_b)
for
a\in A
and
b\in B
, leading to

\Phi\,=-\frac{J}{2\Delta N}\sum\limits_{i,j}\cos\left(\theta^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{i}-\theta^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{j}\right)+\frac{J^{\prime}}{2\Delta N}\sum\limits_{a\in A,b\in B}\cos^{2}\left(\theta^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{a}-\theta^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{b}\right)\,,~~

(171)

where
i,j
run over all rotors in both sets
A
and
B
, and
J^{\prime}/J
is the parameter of interest. The new term (with coefficient
J^{\prime}>0
) competes with the ordinary Kuramoto attraction (with coefficient
J>0
), and makes configurations with fixed angular separations between the
A
and
B
rotors
\theta_{a}
and
\theta_{b}
statistically favorable. We emphasize that the nonequilibirum distribution (
171
) is a natural modification of the equilibrium analogue (
161
)—the nonequilibrium version simply incorporates one higher harmonic of the rotor variables
\theta^{\,}_{i}
compared to Eq. (
161
).

For simplicity, we again consider the
mean-field
dynamics of systems that relax to the stationary distribution
\Phi
(
171
). At late times, we expect all rotors to align
within
their species (
A
or
B
), due to the first term in Eq. (
171
); the second term suggests that alignment between the two species is undesirable. Accordingly, we take
\expectationvalue{\theta_{a/b}}=\theta_{A/B}
, so that all rotors in
a\in A
(
b\in B
) have the average orientation
\theta_{A}
(
\theta_{B}
); we further define
\delta\theta\equiv\theta_{A}-\theta_{B}
as the average angular separation between
A
and
B
rotors. The mean-field stationary distribution (
171
) is then

\displaystyle\Phi_{\rm MFT}\,=-\frac{\alpha\,J\,N}{\Delta}\,\cos(\delta\theta)+\frac{\alpha\,\bar{J}^{\prime}\,N}{2\,\Delta}\,\cos^{2}(\delta\theta)\,,~~\,,~~

(172)

where we have ignored the constant term in
\Phi
as well as fluctuations from
\theta_{a/b}=\theta_{A/B}
and
\alpha=N_{A}N_{B}/N^{2}
. Because
\Phi
(
171
) is fully connected, and effectively infinite dimensional, mean-field theory correctly describes the coarse-grained physics, even when we neglect fluctuations, as in Eq. (
172
). At the mean-field level, the distinct phases of the rotors realized by
\Phi_{\rm MFT}
(
172
) are identified by its two minima: When
J\gg J^{\prime}
,
\Phi_{\rm MFT}
is minimized (and entropy and probability maximized) when
all
rotors are aligned, so that
\delta\theta=0
; in the opposing limit
J\ll J^{\prime}
,
\Phi_{\rm MFT}
has a
pair
of degenerate minima corresponding to
\delta\theta=\pm\,\arccos(J/J')
, and the system spontaneously chooses one of the two minima with fixed, average angular separation
\expectationvalue{\theta_a - \theta_b}=\pm\arccos(J/J')
between all
A
and
B
rotors.

We now consider the two phases of
\Phi_{\rm MFT}
(
172
). In particular, we consider whether the rotors are stationary in the SSB phase (with two degenerate minima), or if they spin in a manner that violates gT. Such dynamical details do not follow from
\Phi
alone, but depend on whether the resulting equations of motion are “nonreciprocal.” For convenience, we assume additive Gaussian noise with variance
\Delta
. First, consider the reciprocal case, which can be captured by purely dissipative dynamics, and whose Fokker-Planck generator
W
is invariant under
P
and T individually,

W\,=\,-\frac{\Delta}{2}\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{i}(\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{i}+\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{i})\,,~~

(173)

where the sum over
i\in A\cup B
is implicit. The chemical potentials are

\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{A}\,=-\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{B}\,=\,\frac{\alpha\,N}{4\,\Delta}\left(J\sin(\delta\theta)-J^{\prime}\,\cos(\delta\theta)\sin(\delta\theta)\right)\,,~~

(174)

and the corresponding mean-field equations of motion for the
A
and
B
rotors are, respectively,

\displaystyle\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{t}\theta_{A}\,

\displaystyle=-\frac{\alpha\,N}{8}\left(J-J^{\prime}\,\cos(\theta_A - \theta_B )\right)\,\sin(\theta_A - \theta_B )+\xi^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{A}(t)

(175a)

\displaystyle\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{t}\theta_{B}\,

\displaystyle=+\frac{\alpha\,N}{8}\left(J-J^{\prime}\,\cos(\theta_A - \theta_B )\right)\,\sin(\theta_A - \theta_B )+\xi^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{B}(t),~~

(175b)

where the only necessary contribution to the stochastic force
f_{A/B}
is
-\Delta\mu^{\,}_{A/B}/2
, corresponding to dissipative, T-even dynamics. Note that Eq. (
175
) is manifestly invariant under
P:\theta_{A}\leftrightarrow\theta_{B}
. At late times, the average over trajectories should reproduce the distirubtion
\Phi_{\rm MFT}
(
172
). In the symmetric (reciprocal) phase, we have
\theta_{A}=\theta_{B}
, so that the sine terms in Eq. (
175
) vanish, and
\expectationvalue{\pd*{t}\theta_{A/B}}=0
, meaning that the rotors are
static
. However, in the putative SSB phase with
\theta_{A}-\theta_{B}=\arccos(J/J')
, we would find that
\expectationvalue{\pd*{t}\theta_{A/B}}=0
, since the parenthetical terms in Eq. (
175
) vanish. In other words, this purely dissipative case (
175
) leads to all rotors aligning in some stationary (spinless) configuration. On average,
\theta_{A}
and
\theta_{B}
are static at late times, with
\theta_{A}-\theta_{B}
determined by the phase of
\Phi
(
172
).

Realizing a spinning phase at late times requires adding dissipationless terms to the Fokker-Planck generator
W
(
173
), and corresponds to the nonreciprocal phase in which
\Phi_{\rm MFT}
(
172
) has two degenerate minima with finite angular separation between all
A
and
B
rotors. The new dissipationless terms explicitly break the T invariance of
W
(
173
); however, the target nonreciprocal stationary state
P_{\rm ss}=\exp(- \Phi_{\rm MFT})
with spinning rotors also breaks T. Instead, both
P_{\rm ss}
and the corresponding generator
W
are invariant under a gT symmetry corresponding to the combination of T (
46
) and a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
operation
P\,:\,\theta_{A}\leftrightarrow\theta_{B}
that swaps the roles of the
A
and
B
species of rotors
7
7

7

The operation
P
is only meaningful in the nonreciprocal case in which the two species are distinguishable.
. The identification of a generalized T symmetry gT provides analytic control over the theory (i.e.,
W
, which is gT even), and the results of Sec.
2
apply straightforwardly. In particular, the results of Sec.
2.4
guarantee that
W
(
173
) is modified to

W\,=\,\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{i}v_{i}-\frac{\Delta}{2}\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{i}\,(\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{i}+\mu^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{i})\,,~~

(176)

where
i\in{A,B}
in the mean-field limit described by the pair of angles
\theta_{A}
and
\theta_{B}
. The new, dissipationless contribution
v_{i}
(
67
) to the Fokker-Planck force (
60
) satisfies Eq. (
70
), which can be fulfilled by taking

v_{A}\,=\,v_{B}\,=\,v(\delta\theta)\,,~~

(177)

for some as-yet undetermined function
v(\cdot)
, which may be constrained by symmetries. In particular, for
W
(
176
) to be symmetric under gT (i.e.,
P\,T
),
v
must be an
odd
function of
\delta\theta=\theta_{A}-\theta_{B}
. The resulting equations of motion are

\displaystyle\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{t}\theta_{A}\,

\displaystyle=\,v(\theta_{A}-\theta_{B})-\frac{\alpha\,N}{8}\left(J-J^{\prime}\,\cos(\theta_A - \theta_B )\right)\,\sin(\theta_A - \theta_B )+\xi^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{A}(t)

(178a)

\displaystyle\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{t}\theta_{B}\,

\displaystyle=\,v(\theta_{A}-\theta_{B})+\frac{\alpha\,N}{8}\left(J-J^{\prime}\,\cos(\theta_A - \theta_B )\right)\,\sin(\theta_A - \theta_B )+\xi^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{B}(t),~~

(178b)

where
v(\delta\theta)
need not vanish at the degenerate minima
\delta\theta=\pm\arccos(J/J')
, though it must vanish at
\delta\theta=0
. As before, all of the terms on the right-hand side of Eq. (
178
) vanish, on average,
except
the new
v
term. In the phase with
J\gg J^{\prime}
so that
\delta\theta=0
,
v=0
, and all rotors are static and aligned. However, in the phase with
J\ll J^{\prime}
, we find that all
A
rotors have orientation
\theta_{a}=\theta_{A}
, while all
B
rotors have orientation
\theta_{b}=\theta_{B}
, with fixed separation
\delta\theta=\theta_{A}-\theta_{B}=\pm\arccos(J/J')
. Crucially, all rotors
spin
at the constant rate
\Omega=\expectationvalue{\pd*{t}\theta_{A/B}}=\pm v(\arccos(J/J'))
, where the direction
\pm
(clockwise versus counterclockwise) is dictated by which of the two minima of
\Phi_{\rm MFT}
(
172
) is spontaneously chosen. While the stationary state
\Phi
(
172
), the generator
W
(
176
), and the equations of motion (
178
) respect gT (i.e., the combination of
P
and T), the fact that
\Omega_{A/B}=\expectationvalue{ \pd*{t}\theta_{A/B} }\neq 0
reflects spontaneous breaking of gT, where gT sends
\Omega_{A/B}\to-\Omega_{A/B}
. In other words, the two minima of
\Phi_{\rm MFT}
(
172
), corresponding to all rotors spinning clockwise or counterclockwise at rate
\Omega_{A/B}=\expectationvalue{\pd*{t}\theta_{A/B}}=\pm v(\theta_{A}-\theta_{B})
, are interchanged under gT.

It is instructive to compare the nonreciprocal case above the the reciprocal, equilibrium example of Sec.
4.2.1
. In both cases, the transformation
P
—a symmetry of
\Phi
that is not a symmetry of the equations of motion—is spontaneously broken. However, in the nonreciprocal case, the operation T—which is also a symmetry of
\Phi
in both cases—is not a symmetry of the
nonreciprocal
equations of motion, whereas the equilibrium variant inherently obeys detailed balance, and its equations of motion are T even. In the nonreciprocal case, the product of T and
P
(i.e., gT) is preserved by
\Phi
(
172
) and the equations of motion (
178
), but is spontaneously broken by solutions to the latter (corresponding to particular minima of
\Phi
). This spontaneous breaking of gT is due to the dissipationless term
v
(
177
) that appears in (
178
), and is indicated by a nonzero expectation value of the order parameter

\Omega\,=\,\expectationvalue{\pd{t}\theta_A + \pd{t} \theta_B)}\,=\,\pm 2\,v(\arccos(J/J'))\,,~~

(179)

which changes sign under T but is symmetric under
\theta_{A}\leftrightarrow\theta_{B}
(
P
), and hence breaks gT; the rightmost expression is evaluated in the stationary state
\Phi
(
172
) for
J\ll J^{\prime}
, where
\pm
corresponds to the two minima of
\Phi
. The nonreciprocal Kuramoto model
Fruchart et al. 2021
realizes the simplest example of spontaneous breaking of gT (
P
T), captured by the order parameter
\Omega\neq 0
(
179
); we consider other examples of spontaneous gT breaking in Sec.
5.1.4
.

We emphasize that the EFT presented above is
not
precisely the nonreciprocal Kuramoto model (
170
) described in Ref.
36
, as can be seen by comparing those equations of motion (
170
) to those that result from the effective theory (
178
). Rather, our perspective is that the stationary distribution
\Phi
that gives rise to the phenomenological Kuramoto equation of motion (
170
) is likely quite complicated, involving many terms, some of which may be nonlocal. Instead, we consider a simpler stationary state
\Phi
(
171
), at the cost of complicating the resulting equations of motion. However, those equations are simple in the mean-field limit (
178
). Still, the topology of the resulting phase diagram of
\Phi_{\rm MFT}
(
172
) agrees in part with that of the microscopic nonreciprocal Kuramoto model
Fruchart et al. 2021
, and we expect that the phase transition from a static to chiral (i.e., spinning) phase is the same in each model. We relegate calculation (or simulation) of any dynamical critical exponents of these models to future work.

4.3
Dynamics of wealth

As a final toy example of nonthermal dynamics, we apply the EFT formalism of Sec.
2
to a cartoon model from the social science describing the dynamics of
wealth
(or income)
Kalecki
;
Angle
;
Ispolatov et al.
;
Yakovenko and Rosser 2009
. Although real economic systems do not evolve toward a stationary distribution
\Phi
(
41
), due to the ever-changing nature of society, technology, and markets, this approximation is useful from the perspective of modeling short-time dynamics. Denoting by
x>0
the wealth of an individual, it is well established in the literature
Kalecki
;
Angle
;
Ispolatov et al.
;
Yakovenko and Rosser 2009
that the appropriate stationary distribution takes the form
\exp(-\Phi)\sim x^{-\alpha}
when
x\gg x^{\,}_{0}
is large, while
\exp(-\Phi)\sim x\,\exp(-x)
when
x\ll x^{\,}_{0}
is small.

A simple choice of
\Phi
that realizes these two limits is given by

\Phi\,=\,(1+\alpha)\,x^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{0}\,\log\left(1+\frac{x}{x^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{0}}\right)-\log x\,,~~

(180)

where
x^{\,}_{0}
is a characteristic wealth scale that separates the lower and upper ends of the in distribution, so that

\mu\,=\,\partialderivative{\Phi}{x}\,=\,\frac{(1+\alpha)\,x^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{0}}{x+x^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{0}}-\frac{1}{x}\,,~~

(181)

is the chemical potential corresponding to
\Phi
(
180
). We assume multiplicative Gaussian noise with

\expectationvalue{\eta(x,t) \eta(x,t')}\,=\,Q(x)\,\delta\left(t-t^{\prime}\right)\,,~~

(182)

in which case the Fokker-Planck generator
W
(
37
) takes the form

W\,=\,-\frac{1}{2}\,\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{x}\,Q(x)\left(\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{x}+\mu(x)\right)\,,~~

(183)

via Eqs. (
53
) and (
57
), since there are no dissipationless terms required.

We note that, at both small and large wealth
x
, the rate of income growth (or decay) should be proportional to
x
. Since
\mu(x)
(
181
) scales as
1/x
, this is achieved if the noise variance
Q(x)
(
182
) is of the form

Q(x)\,=\,Q^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{0}\,x^{2}\,~~

(184)

for some constant
Q^{\,}_{0}
, so that the term in
W
proportional to
\partial^{\,}_{x}
is linear in
x
.

From the perspective of our EFT formalism, we have essentially derived (or guessed) the equations of motion governing wealth based on plausible assumptions about (
i
) the late-time distribution of wealth and (
ii
) a rough idea of how wealth should grow or shrink on short time scales. From the Fokker-Planck generator
W
(
183
), it is straightforward to work out the stochastic equation of motion for
x
. Using the Itô regularization, we find

\frac{\dot{x}}{x}\,=\,-Q^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{0}\frac{[(1+\alpha)\,x^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{0}-3]\,x-3\,x^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{0}}{x+x^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{0}}+\sqrt{2\,Q^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{0}}\,\xi\,,~~

(185)

where
\xi
is an additive Gaussian noise source, such that
\eta\propto\sqrt{x}\,\xi
. Note that Eq. (
185
) agrees with the literature: When
x\gg x^{\,}_{0}
is large, we find a contribution corresponding to multiplicative noise
\dot{x}/x\sim\xi
, as well as a deterministic (and
approximately constant) contribution
\dot{x}/x\sim c
. This reproduces cartoon models of the “Mathieu effect,” which realize (approximate) power-law tails in a range of distribution functions that appear throughout the social sciences
Newman 2005
.

We comment that the standard approach in the literature—in the social and physical sciences—is to start by writing down an equation of motion, and then work out its properties, including the existence and form of a stationary distribution. We reiterate that the opposite approach—in which one starts by positing a stationary distribution
\Phi
(
41
) and works out all compatible dynamics—is just as valuable. The latter perspective, captured by our EFT formalism, unsurprisingly leads to similar models as have been identified in the literature. While this perspective may lead to more complicated (i.e., uglier) equations of motion, their derivation is more systematic, and the symmetries, late-time behavior, and possible phases are more readily understood. In many cases—including the dynamics of wealth considered above—these results can be recovered more expediently in the EFT approach.

5
Spontaneous breaking of generalized time-reversal symmetry

The nonreciprocal Kuramoto model in Sec.
4.2.2
provides a simple realization of “activity,” a crucial consequence of which is the
spontaneous breaking
of generalized time-reversal symmetry gT. Importantly, the breaking of gT is forbidden under thermal dynamics, and is thus a fingerprint of activity. We now systematically explore the mechanism(s) by which gT can be spontaneously broken from the perspective of the EFT framework of Sec.
2
. This construction also provides an elegant application of the results of Sec.
2.6
regarding the incorporation of symmetries.

5.1
General framework

We first develop the mathematical machinery to investigate how generalized time-reversal can be broken spontaneously. For clarity, we consider the example of overdamped dynamics of rigid bodies, and comment on more general results.

5.1.1
Example: rigid-body rotation

Here we consider the rotational dynamics of a rigid body—in which the constituent degrees of freedom rotate together—in
d=3
8
8

8

We consider EFTs for rigid-body dynamics in further detail in Sec.
6.2
. For convenience, we identify the fixed center of mass with the origin
\mathbf{x}_{\rm COM}=0
. At the microscopic level, the rigid body is comprised of an enormous number of atoms or molecules labeled
a=1,\dots,N
. The microscopic configuration (phase) space
M
is
a priori
extremely large, with
M=\mathbb{R}^{3N}
spanned by configurations
\mathbf{x}\in M
with
\mathbf{x}=\{x^{a}_{i}\}
, where
i=1,2,3
is a spatial index. Still, the configuration of a rigid body is highly constrained within
M=\mathbb{R}^{3N}
; at late times, the system relaxes to a configuration on
M
that minimizes the stationary distribution
\Phi
(
41
), as such configurations are the most probable. At sufficiently late times, the particles “condense” into a rigid body, at which point the only
guaranteed
degeneracies of
\Phi
are those related by global rotations of all particle positions,

\Phi\left(\{x^{a}_{i}\}\right)\,=\,\Phi\left(\{R^{\vphantom{\dagger}}_{ij}x^{a}_{j}\}\right)\,,~~

(186)

for some rotation matrix
R\in\mathsf{SO}\left(3\right)=G
, where all positions are defined with respect to the center of mass. We emphasize that
R
does not depend on
a
—meaning that all particles rotate together under
R\in G
9
9

9

The case in which
R
depends on
a
(or varies spatially) is treated in Ref.
88
.
. However, the configuration
\overline{\mathbf{x}}\in M
that minimizes
\Phi
does not have the symmetry
\mathbf{x}^{*}=R\,\mathbf{x}^{*}
for
R\in G
of
\Phi
itself; hence, such configurations spontaneously break isotropy (the rotational symmetry
G
). For example, one could imagine a
\Phi
whose minimum
\mathbf{x}^{*}
corresponds to the
N
particles realizing a cubic body; while
\Phi
remains invariant under all rotations in
G
via Eq. (
186
), the cubic body is only invariant under the
point group

H=\mathrm{S}_{4}
(the permutation group of four elements).

In the context of rigid bodies, it is standard practice to refer to a “space frame” and a “body frame.” The space frame is defined by a fixed coordinate axis in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
; different space-frame configurations correspond to different points in
M
. However, a particular configuration—such as the configuration
\mathbf{x}^{*}
that minimizes
\Phi
—also identifies a particular rigid body, in the sense of defining a particular, spontaneous breaking of the symmetry group
G
, where different configurations
\mathbf{x}
may realize distinct point groups
H\subset G
. Of course, rotations of a rigid body (under
R\in G
) do not change the
type
of rigid body (i.e., they do not deform the body), and hence correspond to the same body. This implies an
equivalence class
on
M
, corresponding to distinct rigid bodies, which are labeled by distinct body-frame configurations in
M/G
. Thus, points in the
coset space

M/G
correspond to distinct ways of breaking
G
spontaneously, and all points
\mathbf{x}\in M
related by rotations
R\in G
correspond to the same body-frame coordinate
\overline{\mathbf{x}}
.

More importantly, at the level of EFT, the coarse-grained dynamics of the rigid body are entirely captured by rotations
R\in G
. The space-frame configurations
\mathbf{x}\in M
corresponding to the same body-frame coordinate
\overline{\mathbf{x}}\in M/G
not only realize rotations of the same body, but also have the same late-time probability due to Eq. (
186
). Note that
\Phi
is well defined on
M/G
by Eq. (
186
). Now, while deformations of the rigid body are possible in principle, they map “low-energy” configurations of
\Phi
to higher-energy (i.e., less probable) configurations, which rapidly decay back to the low-energy manifold of
M
, corresponding to the body-frame coordinate
\overline{\mathbf{x}}^{*}
that minimizes
\Phi
. Hence, the coarse-grained dynamics are captured by rotations
R\in G
, so that the space-frame coordinates evolve in time via

x_{i}^{a}(t)\,=\,R^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{iI}(t)\,\overline{x}^{\,a}_{I}\,,~~

(187)

where
x^{a}_{i}(t)
is the time-dependent position of the
a
th particle in the
space
frame (an inertial reference frame), and
\overline{x}^{a}_{I}
is the position of the
a
th particle in a statistically favorable configuration in the
body
frame (at
t=0
, for reference). The space-frame axes correspond to a fixed Cartesian frame, while the body-frame axes are fixed relative the rigid body; we use lowercase indices
i,j
for the space frame and uppercase indices
I,J
for the body frame. We generally assume that
\Phi
has finitely many degenerate minima in
M/G
; for convenience, we often assume it has only one. The orthogonal matrix
R^{\,}_{iI}(t)
effects a change of basis from the body frame to the space frame, as is standard to the treatment of rigid bodies
José and Saletan 1998
. The matrix
R(t)
is dynamical degree of freedom for EFTs of rigid bodies.

We also stress that the notation of Eq. (
187
) is useful not only in the context of rigid-body dynamics, but for continuous symmetries more generally. A dynamical system with configuration space
M
and a continuous symmetry group
G
generally realizes different dynamics in the space frame
M
compared to the body frame
M/G
; moreover, from the EFT perspective, it is useful to distinguish these two. For convenience of presentation, we generally focus on the case of rigid bodies; we also use the terminology of “space” versus “body” frames in all contexts.

5.1.2
Left and right symmetry groups

Having argued that the matrix
R^{\,}_{iI}(t)
is the correct coarse-grained degree of freedom for rigid bodies, we now comment on the additional symmetries constraining any EFT for
R^{\,}_{iI}(t)
, and how they transform
R
. As a reminder, a symmetry of the EFT is equivalent to a symmetry of its Fokker-Planck generator
W
(see Sec.
2.6
).

We first observe that there is no “preferred” choice of coordinate axis for
\mathbb{R}^{3}
in the space frame, or equivalently, of the origin for
G=\mathsf{SO}\left(3\right)
. Essentially, even when
G
is spontaneously broken by a configuration
\overline{\mathbf{x}}\in M/G
, the same physics should recover for any rotation, captured by
\Lambda\in G
, which simply redefines the space-frame coordinates via

\displaystyle x^{a}_{i}\,\to\,y^{\alpha}_{i}\,

\displaystyle=\,\Lambda^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{ij}x^{a}_{j}\,,~~

(188)

where
\Lambda^{\,}_{ij}
is a space-frame rotation that acts on the left-hand side of Eq. (
187
). For the equality in Eq. (
187
) to hold in this rotated basis for
M
, we also transform the element
R\in G
according to

\displaystyle R^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{iI}(t)\,\to\,\Lambda^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{ij}\,R^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{jI}(t)\,,~~~~\Lambda\in G\,,~~

(189)

which modifies
R
(
187
) to reflect the rotated space-frame coordinates. Because the body-frame configurations
\overline{\mathbf{x}}
belong to
M/G
(they are fixed relative the body’s principal axes), all points in
M
related by rotations
\Lambda\in G
are equivalent, and correspond to the same
\overline{\mathbf{x}}
. Hence,
\Lambda
is not applied to the
I
index of
R
(
187
).

This naturally leads to the distinction between left and right invariance of the dynamical variables
R(t)
under (subgroups of)
G
. For a system with no symmetries whatsoever, the variables
R
would have no invariance properties (this also results if a symmetry
G
is
explicitly
broken). If a system is invariant under a continuous symmetry
G
that is respected not only by
\Phi
, but by the configurations that minimize
\Phi
(i.e., there is neither explicit nor spontaneous symmetry breaking), then the variables
R
would be invariant under
G
from the left and right. In that case,
R
is an element of
G
, and multiplying from the left or right by elements of
G
also results in elements of
G
. The interesting scenario corresponds to
spontaneous
breaking of
G
, as in the rigid-body example, in which case the space frame
M
retains the full symmetry group
G
, but the body frame
M/G
is symmetric only under a subgroup
H\subset G
(e.g.,
S_{4}\subset\mathsf{SO}\left(3\right)
in the case of the cube), corresponding to the part of
G
that remains after
G
is spontaneously broken. As a result,
R^{\,}_{iI}(t)
(
187
) is invariant under
G
from the left (the space-frame index
i
) but only invariant under
H\subset G
from the right (the body-frame index
I
). Essentially, the “point group”
H
includes the transformations of the body itself that leave it invariant. Thus, for spontaneously broken continuous symmetries
G\to H
, the dynamical variables are left invariant under
G
and right invariant under
H\subset G
.

We next consider how left and right invariance of
R
manifest in the resulting theory. Using the notation convention of Eq. (
187
), left
G
invariance of the Fokker-Planck generator
W(R^{\,}_{iI},\partial^{\,}_{iI})
is ensured by demanding that lowercase (space-frame) indices
i,j,\dots
of
R
and/or
\partial
are only contracted with invariant tensors of
G
. For example,
\delta^{\,}_{i,j}\in G
is an invariant tensor, and so a simple term that could appear in the rigid-body Fokker-Planck generator is

\displaystyle W=\partial_{iI}R_{iI}\Omega^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{JI}\,,~~

(190)

where
\Omega
is a matrix related to the components of the angular velocities
{\bf\it\omega}
in the body frame (see Sec.
6
) via

\displaystyle\Omega_{IJ}=-1/2\epsilon_{IJK}\omega_{K}=x_{I}\dot{x}_{J}-x_{J}\dot{x}_{I}.

(191)

Thus
\Omega
encodes the physical degrees of freedom. One can also check that
\Omega_{IJ}
must be antisymmetric if
R_{iI}R_{iJ}=\delta_{IJ})
.

Having established that we should enforce
G
on the left (lower case) indices, let us turn now to the right (upper-case) indices. We wish now to consider transformations where we rotate the body-frame axes with respect to the body itself. However, a general rigid body will not itself be symmetric under every transformation in
G
!
If
\Omega_{JI}\neq 0
, then the body itself must explicitly break
G
—this follows simply from the fact that a general antisymmetric matrix
\Omega_{IJ}
does not lead to covariance on the right-hand side of Eq. (
190
). Let
H\subset G
be the (possibly discrete) subgroup that represents the body-frame symmetry of the rigid object—for instance, a cylindrical body would have
H=\mathsf{SO}\left(2\right)
. Next, consider transforming the body-frame axes under
H
, so that
x_{I}\to V_{IJ}x_{J}
for
V\in H
. Then in Eq. (
187
), since the left-hand side is invariant under body-frame transformations, the matrix
R_{iI}
on the right-hand side must transform with
V^{-1}=V^{T}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle R_{iI}\,\to\,R_{iJ}\,V_{JI}\,~~~~V\in H\,,~~

(192)

and we further demand that this is a symmetry of the Fokker-Planck generator
W
(
53
), which we refer to as
right

H

invariance
.
In the rigid-body example, one can show that
\Omega_{JI}
itself respects right
H
invariance, which forms the same group
\mathsf{SO}\left(3\right)
if and only if the object is spherically symmetric! To see this, recall first that the SDEs (
31
) of interest—at least in the overdamped limit—are linear in
\dot{R}
. Covariance of these equations of motion then corresponds to covariance of Eq. (
190
), which in turn requires that

\displaystyle\Omega_{IJ}\,=\,R_{iI}\,\dot{R}_{iJ}\,\to\,R_{iK}\,R_{jL}\,\Omega_{KL}\,V_{KJ}\,V_{LJ}~~~\implies~~~~\left[\Omega,V\right]\,=\,0\,,~~

(193)

meaning that
V
and
\Omega
commute, and thus,
\Omega_{IJ}
respects right
H
invariance (
192
).

5.1.3
Dynamics along
G

We are now ready to construct an effective theory for the low
\Phi
dynamics in the case where the symmetry group
G
is spontaneously broken. Again, constructing an effective theory in our perspective amounts to building the symplest
W
operator that is consistent with the desired symmetries. We should first note that the stationary distribution
\Phi
is constant on
G
, since
G
is the manifold of degenerate minima of
\Phi
. That means the “chemical potentials” vanish:
\mu^{\,}_{iI}\equiv\partialderivative*{\Phi}{R_{iI}}=0
. We further demand that the Fokker-Planck generator
W
be compatible with the left
G
invariance described in Sec.
5.1.3
. Finally, if
R_{iI}(t)\in G
for all time
t
, the time derivative
\dot{R}_{iI}
must satisfy

\displaystyle\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{t}R_{iI}\,=\,R_{iJ}\,\Omega_{JI}\,,~~

(194)

for an antisymmetric matrix
\Omega_{IJ}=-\Omega_{JI}
that is an element of the Lie algebra of
G
. The object
\Omega_{IJ}
, which encodes the angular velocity in the body frame, previously appeared in Eq. (
190
). Finally, if the right symmetry group
H
is nontrivial, Eq. (
193
) must hold as well; this is satisfied by identifying the simplest
\Omega
that commutes with all
V\in H
(
193
). The vanishing of
\mu^{\,}_{iI}
(together with left and right symmetries) heavily constrains the dissipationless part of the Fokker-Planck operator
W
, giving

\displaystyle W\,=\,\partialderivative{R_{iI}}\,R_{iJ}\Omega_{JI}\equiv\partial_{iI}R_{iI}\Omega_{IJ}\,,~~

(195)

where, in the rightmost equality above, we have introduced the convenient shorthand
\partial^{\,}_{iI}\equiv\partialderivative*{R_{iI}}
.

However, in addition to noise-free dynamics, one can also consider adding a stochastic noise source
\xi_{iI}(t)
to the equations of motion for
R_{iI}
, such that the Langevin SDE (
31
) is

\displaystyle\dot{R}_{iI}\,=\,R_{iJ}\,\Omega_{JI}+\xi_{iI}(t)\,,~~

(196)

where, following Eqs. (
53
) and (
57
),
\xi_{iI}
should enter
W
(
195
) at quadratic order in the gradients
\partial^{\,}_{iI}
. However, one must be careful that the addition of
\xi_{iI}(t)
in Eq. (
196
) does not spoil the orthogonality of
R
, so that
R
remains confined to
G
. To maintain closure on
G
, the additive noise source
{\bf\it\xi}
must be replaced by a
multiplicative
noise source
\eta_{iI}(R,t)
of the form

\displaystyle\eta_{iI}(t)\,=\,R_{iJ}\,A_{IJ}(t)

(197)

where
A_{IJ}(t)
is a stochastic antisymmetric matrix, again in the Lie algebra of
G
. In the simple Gaussian case a
3\times 3

A_{IJ}
belongs to the Lie Algebra
\mathfrak{so}(3)
and may therefore be expanded in terms of its basis matrices
\epsilon^{\alpha}_{IJ}=\epsilon_{\alpha IJ}
where
\epsilon_{\alpha IJ}
is the completely antisymemtric tensor , giving

\displaystyle A_{IJ(t)}=\epsilon^{\alpha}_{IJ}\,\xi^{\alpha}(t),

(198)

where
\epsilon^{\alpha}
are the constant antisymmetric matrices that generate
G
and
\xi^{\alpha}
are stochastic variables with zero mean (
\expectationvalue{\eta^\alpha(t)}=0
) and variance

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\xi^{\alpha}(t) \xi^{\beta}(t^\prime}=Q^{\alpha\beta}\delta(t-t^{\prime})

(199)

for a constant matrix
Q^{\alpha\beta}
. Using our prescription (
53
) for converting a stochastic noise in to a Fokker-Planck generator, we find that the
W
describing noisy overdamped dynamics on
G
is

\displaystyle W=\partial_{iI}R_{iJ}\Omega_{JI}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{iI}\left(R_{iK}\epsilon^{\alpha}_{KI}Q^{\alpha\beta}R_{jL}\epsilon^{\beta}_{LJ}\right)\partial_{jJ}.

(200)

For completeness, we note that in the common simplifying case were the noise is isotropic and
Q^{\alpha\beta}=\Delta\delta^{\alpha\beta}
, one may check use properties of
\epsilon^{\alpha}_{IJ}
to show that
W
takes a simpler form

\displaystyle W=\partial_{iI}R_{iJ}\Omega_{JI}-\frac{1}{2}\Delta\,\partial_{iI}\left(\quantity(\delta_{ij} \delta_{IJ} - R_{iJ}R_{jI})\right)\partial_{jJ}.

(201)

In the following subsections, we will be mostly concerned with the deterministic part of
W
, but it is good to know that in principle we can include noise as well.

5.1.4
Spontaneously breaking generalized time-reversal symmetry

Now that we have an EFT for dynamics on a general group
G
, we have a good platform to consider the spontaneous breaking of reversibility symmetry.
Spontaneous symmetry breaking (SSB) happens in a classical dynamical system when the individual stochastic trajectories (almost surely) do not sample the whole phase space, even restricted to the appropriate symmetry sector (e.g. at a given energy or conserved charge).
Strictly speaking, in the infinite time limit, SSB cannot occur if there are a finite number of degrees of freedom, so long as
\Phi
is not infinite, or without some other fine tuning. However, since we may often use a finite-dimensional theory (as in the Kuramoto model) to describe the mean-field limit of a sytsem in the thermodynamic limit, we can still start to classify possible patterns of SSB without immediately worrying about whether SSB is stable to such fluctuations in the
t\rightarrow\infty
limit.

In our framework, SSB must be a feature captured by the minima of
\Phi
, which are not invariant under the symmetry group of
\Phi
as a whole. This is only possible if
\Phi
has more than one minimum in
M/G
, so we will now posit that this is indeed the case. For simplicity in the discussion below, we will consider the case where
\Phi
has exactly two degenerate minima
m_{\pm}\in M/G
, which transform into each other under a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry, which we will denote as
P
, which together with T forms the generalized time-reversal gT. If
\Phi
has a thermodynamically large barrier between
m_{\pm}
, then
P
will be spontaneously broken—we will observe the system to be in either
m_{+}
or
m_{-}
with no transition between the two, and the configuration space of the resulting dynamics is effectively the corresponding disconnected sector of phase space parameterized only by
R
.

In this setting, let us now consider a Fokker-Planck generator
W
which is gT-invariant. This can be achieved by choosing
\Omega
, defined in (
194
), to obey

\Omega(m_{\pm})=\pm\Omega_{0};

(202)

namely,
\Omega_{JI}
has opposite sign at the two minima
m_{\pm}
. Since
\Omega_{JI}
flips sign under time-reversal, but also flips sign under
P
,
W
(and thus the dynamical system) explicitly breaks both P and T, while being PT-symmetric.

We emphasize that the
dynamics
of the system explicitly break T, while the
thermodynamics
of the system spontaneously break P. Of course, since
W
has exact PT-symmetry, and the symmetry breaking pattern of
\Phi
is, by definition, insensitive to time-reversal symmetry, we can consider
\Phi
to be PT-invariant as well. Then, PT will be spontaneously broken as the system picks either
m_{+}
or
m_{-}
as the minimum on
M/G
. In this way, our framework systematically describes a mechanism for spontaneously breaking a
generalized
time-reversal symmetry. We emphasize it is important that gT, and not just T, be broken spontaneously; after all, the spontaneous breaking of P in the “thermodynamic”
\Phi
was crucial to the mechanism.

A concrete example of this is found in the Kuramoto model of Section
4.2
. Here, we saw that the two minima of
\Phi
corresponded to
\mathrm{\Delta}\theta=\pm\theta_{0}
in the spontaneous generalized time-reversal breaking phase; thus
\{\pm\theta_{0}\}
is the set of minima of
\Phi
on
M/G
. The residual symmetry group
G=\mathrm{U}(1)
has a single generator corresponding to constant shifts in
\theta_{A}+\theta_{B}
. There is a symmetry group P of
\Phi
, which corresponds to switching the species A and B of nonreciprocal rotors. In the phase where PT-symmetry is spontaneously broken, applying the PT transformation to a typical
trajectory
of the system will flip the signs of
\dot{\theta}_{A}+\dot{\theta}_{B}
or
\Delta\theta
: we can think of either of these two quantities as order parameters for the SSB phase.
10
10

10

It would be interesting to connect these ideas to recent generalizations of the Landau paradigm described in
McGreevy 2022
to generalized symmetries.

5.2
Generalizations of the nonreciprocal Kuramoto model

The group-theoretic construction outlined above gives a natural and straightforward way to find model systems with interesting dissipationless dynamics and spontaneous generalized time-reversal breaking.
One simply picks a
\Phi
which has some discrete minima, and chooses an
\Omega
compatible with the desired residual symmetries which also switches sign depending on the degenerate minimum into which the system condenses. In this section, we will describe several examples of more complicated systems than the nonreciprocal Kuramoto model, especially models where
G
is a non-Abelian group. This section is
not
an exhaustive account by any means but is instead meant to provide illustrative examples. The methods of this section readily generalize to a large class of gT-breaking phase transitions, some of which are also described in Section
6.2
.

5.2.1
Kuramoto model with
N
species

To start, we consider a Kuramoto-like model with
N

different
interacting two-dimensional spins. Intuitively, this model is just the mean-field limit of a Kuramoto model with
N
different species. The problem is defined on the manifold
M=\mathrm{S}^{1}\times\mathrm{S}^{1}\times\cdots\mathrm{S}^{1}=\mathrm{T}^{N}
, since each copy of
\mathrm{S}^{1}
denotes the angular coordinate of a single (species of) rotor.

For this problem, we take our continuous left symmetry to be
G=\mathrm{SO}(2)=\mathrm{U}(1)
. We assume that
\Phi
additionally has a discrete symmetry group
\mathrm{S}_{N}
corresponding to permutations of the
N
rotors. To break gT spontaneously, however, we must further break
\mathrm{S}_{N}
to a subgroup.
\mathrm{\mathrm{}}{S}_{N}
has one natural
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
subgroup: the sign of a permutation. Hence we will consider a theory that is only invariant under time-reversal together with reversing the sign of a permutation. We may write
\mathrm{S}_{N}=\mathrm{A}_{N}\rtimes\mathrm{P}
, where
\mathrm{P}
(for ‘parity’) is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and is the sign of a permutation, while
\mathrm{A}_{N}
is the
alternating group
(of even permutations of
N
objects). This model has a generalized time-reversal symmetry PT, and we will study models that spontaneously break PT while preserving
\mathrm{A}_{N}
.

Although many of the statements made in this section do not depend on the explicit form of
\Phi
, it helps to have a simple example in mind; let us take a repulsive Kuramoto model, assuming that
N>2
:

\displaystyle\Phi=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{I\neq J}\cos(\theta_I-\theta_J)+\cdots.

(203)

The static equilibrium corresponds to the
N
spins’ being equally spaced around a circle as in Fig.
2
, and this conclusion is agnostic to the actual form of the repulsive interactions.
11
11

11

This can be shown analytically by writing
\Phi=\frac{1}{4}|\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta_{1}}+\cdots+\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta_{N}}|^{2}-\frac{N}{4}
. When the
\theta
s are equally spaced on a ring, the first term (which is nonnegative) is exactly zero, thus implying it is a global minimum. Without
\cdots
in (
203
) there are many other global minima, which can be lifted by adding further terms such as
|\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}2\theta_{1}}+\cdots+\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}2\theta_{N}}|^{2}
, etc.
We may then parameterize the orientation of the configuration using a rotation matrix
R_{iI}\in\mathrm{SO}(2)
.

Let us now turn to examining the T-breaking phases that could occur in such models. To do so, with reference to (
196
) and (
202
), we must find an antisymmetric matrix
\Omega_{IJ}
that is a body-frame (
\mathrm{A}_{N}
) invariant. Such a matrix must be proportional to
\epsilon_{IJ}
, the only antisymmetric
2\times 2
matrix, which is a body-group invariant. We may therefore write

\displaystyle\Omega_{IJ}=\omega({\bf\it\theta})\,\epsilon_{IJ}.

(204)

1

Figure 2:
Left: An
even
permuation (read counterclockwise, starting from the top for sake of argument) of the spins on a circle rotates counterclockwise. Right: An
odd
permutation of spins rotates clockwise. Generalized time-reversal is spontaneously broken; the direction of motion depends on the sign of the permutation of the minimum into which the system condenses.

All that remains is to choose an
\omega
that is invariant under even-signed permutations of the spins, and flips sign under odd ones. For odd
N
,
12
12

12

If
N
is even, then there will be particles separated by angle
\pi
(equivalent to
-\pi
), meaning that
v(\pi)=0
and thus
\omega=0
, and a more complicated choice is needed.
one option is to choose

\displaystyle\omega({\bf\it\theta})=\prod_{I<J}v(\theta_{I}-\theta_{J}),

(205)

for a fixed odd function
v
, e.g.,
v(\theta)=\sin(\theta)
. This object changes sign under any single swap of two rotors, meaning in particular it is invariant under even permutations and differs by a sign for odd permutations.

In this case, the direction of motion depend on the sign of the permutation into which we condense—for example, even (odd) permutations could lead to anticlockwise (clockwise) motion, see Fig.
2
. The operation of time-reversal effectively takes us from a permutation read anticlockwise to the same one read
clockwise
.
Hence, in the spinning phases, P and T are separately spontaneously broken, but PT is conserved.

5.2.2
Motion on a two-dimensional sphere

One natural generalization of this nonreciprocal
N
-species Kuramoto model (in the mean-field limit) is to consider
N
particles moving on the
M
-dimensional sphere
\mathrm{S}^{M}
. We will illustrate a few such cases with
M=2
. As a minimal example, let us take
M=N=2
, with:

\displaystyle\Omega_{IJ}=\epsilon_{IJK}\epsilon_{KLM}\,n^{(1)}_{L}n^{(2)}_{M}.

(206)

This term leads to nontrivial dynamics, for example, in a situation where
\Phi
enforces the rotors to be separated by a fixed angle and the two species of particles
\hat{\mathbf{n}}^{(1)},\hat{\mathbf{n}}^{(2)}
have nonreciprocal interactions. This case may be visualized according to Fig.
5b
. Here, the particles spin around an “equator” as in the nonreciprocal Kuramoto model of Section
4.2
(with
M=1
). An alternative model, with the same gT symmetry, corresponds to

\Omega_{IJ}=\epsilon_{IJK}\left(n^{(1)}_{K}-n^{(2)}_{K}\right);

(207)

this case is depicted in Fig.
3a
.

(a)

(b)

(c)

Figure 3:
Examples of Spontaneous
T
-breaking on
\mathrm{S}^{2}
. (a) Two particles form a line and rotate together about an axis through the center of the sphere and parallel to the line joining the two particles. In terms of equations, this is the situation of (
207
). (b) Two particles lie on a circle and rotate around it. This is the situation of (
206
). (c) Three particles form a triangle and rotate about an axis perpendicular to the triangle. This is the situation of (
5.2.2
). It is important to note that more exotic
T-
breaking could arise by demanding even less symmetry in the body frame.

For something slightly more nontrivial, consider the case
N=3
and
M=2
, with:

\displaystyle\omega_{IJ}

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{IJK}\,\epsilon_{KLM}\,\quantity(n^{(1)}_L -n^{(2)}_L)\quantity(n^{(2)}_M-n^{(3)}_M)=\epsilon_{IJK}\,\epsilon_{KLM}\,\quantity(n^{(1)}_Ln^{(2)}_M + n^{(2)}_Ln^{(3)}_M + n^{(3)}_Ln^{(1)}_M)

\displaystyle\propto\sum_{\sigma\in\mathrm{S}_{3}}\mathrm{sign}(\sigma)\,\epsilon_{IJK}\,\epsilon_{KLM}\,n^{(\sigma(1))}_{L}n^{(\sigma(2))}_{M}.

(208)

In this situation, the particles may form a triangle and rotate about an axis perpendicular to aligned with the triangle’s normal vector. Note that this normal vector is sensitive to the “chirality” of the triangle on the sphere. The motion in this case may be visualized according to Fig.
3c
.

If we try to go to
N=4
and
M=2
, the situation becomes more complicated; this case, a systematic analysis (which relies on group theory) is discussed in Appendix
E
.

6
Active and dissipative rigid-body dynamics

Here we explore an interesting example that displays nontrivial dissipative and active phenomena: the dynamics of a free rigid body about a fixed rotation point. We will begin by casting ordinary rigid-body mechanics in a convenient Hamiltonian formalism that closely parallels the approach of Sec.
5.1
. Next, following the framework of this paper, we will modify the Hamiltonian dynamics by deriving the appropriate active (time-reversal breaking) and dissipative corrections that may appear in the presence of microscopic activity or noise. Our constraints on the allowed active terms could be relevant for studying active systems like bacteria which can sometimes be approximated as rigid bodies
Berg 1993
. Our minimal models of rotating bodies with angular-momentum conserving noise could provide toy models of (simple aspects of) galaxy formation
Binney and Tremaine 2011
;
Gunn and Tinsley 1972
, tidal friction
Hut 1980
, or other dissipative phenomena involving rotating objects in astrophysics, geophysics, and otherwise. Another possible application is to the simpler modeling of rotating molecules interacting with atomic gases
Raston et al. 2013
.

6.1
Hamiltonian formalism of rigid-body dynamics

Let us begin by studying ordinary rigid-body dynamics (in our universe’s three spatial dimensions) in a Hamiltonian formalism. To do so, we must first specify a set of coordinates for the rigid body, their conjugate momenta, and the form of the Poisson bracket. With respect to fixed ‘space frame’ axes, the configuration of such a rigid body may be specified by a rotation matrix
R_{iI}(t)\in\mathrm{SO}(3)
from the body frame to the space frame, as described in Section
5.1.2
.
Of course, being elements of
\mathrm{SO}(3)
, these matrices must be orthogonal:

\displaystyle R_{iI}R_{jI}=\delta_{ij},\ R_{iI}R_{iJ}=\delta_{IJ}.

(209)

We would now like to write down ordinary Hamiltonian mechanics for the problem that has configuration space SO(3)
Arnold and Khesin 1998
. In Hamiltonian mechanics, this corresponds to dynamics on
\mathrm{T}^{*}\mathrm{SO}(3)
, the cotangent bundle of the Lagrangian configuration space.
To do Hamiltonian mechanics, we also need a symplectic form
\omega
, related to the Poisson brackets. The standard choice is to find an exact symplectic
2
-form
\omega
:

\displaystyle\omega=\differential{\alpha}

(210)

for a 1-form
\alpha
. Hamiltonian mechanics comes from a particular choice of
\alpha
commonly called the
Liouville form
or
canonical 1-form

José and Saletan 1998
:

\displaystyle\alpha=\frac{1}{2}P_{iI}\differential{R_{iI}}.

(211)

The odd-looking factor of
1/2
will become clear later—essentially, it avoids double counting the dynamical variables. With this choice,

\displaystyle\omega=\differential{\alpha}=\frac{1}{2}\differential{P_{iI}}\wedge\differential{R_{iI}}.

(212)

This approach, however, poses two main complications. First, the
R_{iI}
variables are subject to the constraint (
209
), requiring the introduction of a Lagrange multiplier. We would like to find clever coordinates where this can be neglected because the dynamics inherently respects the orthogonality constraint to begin with. Secondly, the momenta
P_{iI}
have one body frame and one space frame index, so it is difficult to interpret these objects as physical momenta or angular momenta. But there is a trick that will allow us to obtain nontrivial Hamiltonian evolution for
\dot{P}
in this situation while elegantly imposing the constraint. Instead of
P_{iI}
, it helps to consider different variables
P_{IJ}
(two body-frame indices) defined through:

\displaystyle P_{iI}=R_{iJ}P_{JI}.

(213)

This is simply a change of variables, but the body-frame object
P_{IJ}
will end up encoding the angular momentum in the body frame coordinates, and this is the degree of freedom in which the standard Euler equations of rigid-body motion are expressed. But
R
and
P_{IJ}
are no longer canonical, so we must calculate their Poisson brackets. Making the substitution to the
P_{IJ}
coordinates, the Liouville form
\alpha
becomes

\displaystyle\alpha=\frac{1}{2}R_{iJ}P_{JI}\differential{R_{iI}}.

(214)

Now is a good time to see how to efficiently enforce the orthogonality constraint (
209
). Applying
\differential
to the constraint, we find:

\displaystyle R_{iJ}\differential{R_{iI}}=-R_{iI}\differential{R_{iJ}}.

(215)

If this constraint held, observe that only the
antisymmetric
part of the matrix
P_{IJ}
contributes to
\alpha
—the symmetric part vanishes due to contraction with the antisymmetric matrix
R_{iJ}\differential{R_{iI}}
. Since the symmetric part of
P_{IJ}
does not appear in
\alpha
, it will also be absent from
\omega
, meaning it will have trivial dynamics. It is natural, then, to work with only the antisymmetric part of
P_{IJ}
from the start—we obtain the same
\omega
as if we had begun with the full unconstrained
P
. Moreover, we now have 3 momentum coordinates (the number of independent antisymmetric
3\times 3
matrices) that can be “conjugate” to the 3 degrees of freedom in the orthogonal matrix
R_{iI}
. For clarity, let us define

\displaystyle L_{IJ}=\frac{1}{2}(P_{IJ}-P_{JI})

(216)

as the momentum variables for this problem. The key point is: we can take
a priori
the antisymmetric matrices
L_{IJ}
to be our degrees of freedom, and this choice automatically imposes the constraint on
R_{iI}
, (
209
).
The
R_{iI}
variables are now formally
unconstrained
—so long as we choose initial conditions where
R_{iI}(0)
is orthogonal, it will stay that way for all times if we can simply enforce a
linear constraint
that
L_{IJ}=-L_{JI}
, which is not difficult and can be done at the outset.
13
13

13

Abstractly, one can think about this coordinate choice as follows. The cotangent bundle can be decomposed into position coordinates on the group SO(3), and Lie algebra
\mathfrak{so}(3)
-valued momentum coordinates
L_{A}
, given in (
219
).

\displaystyle\alpha=\frac{1}{2}R_{iJ}\,L_{JI}\,\differential{R_{iI}}.

(217)

Having implemented the constraint and thus established that antisymmetric matrices
L_{IJ}
are good momentum variables for the rigid-body problem, we must now calculate the symplectic form
\omega
. Carrying out the derivative of the Liouville form, we find

\displaystyle\omega=\frac{1}{2}(L_{JI}\differential{R_{iJ}}\wedge\differential{R_{iI}}+R_{iJ}\differential{L_{JI}}\wedge\differential{R_{iI}}).

(218)

The unusual coupling between
R
and
R
in this form is responsible for the nontrivial Hamiltonian dynamics in this problem. To explore it, let us first convert to the physical variables
L_{A}
defined by

\displaystyle L_{IJ}=\epsilon_{IJA}L_{A}.

(219)

With this definition, we find the symplectic form

\displaystyle\omega=\frac{1}{2}(\epsilon_{JIK}L_{K}\differential{R_{iJ}}\wedge\differential{R_{iI}}+\epsilon_{JIK}R_{iJ}\differential{L_K}\wedge\differential{R_{iI}}).

(220)

It corresponds to a matrix according to:

\displaystyle\omega=\frac{1}{2}\omega_{\alpha\beta}\,\differential\xi_{\alpha}\wedge\differential\xi_{\beta},

(221)

Our goal now is to use
\omega
to find the
Poisson brackets
between the dynamical variables. The matrix elements of the
inverse
of
\omega_{\alpha\beta}
matrix yield the Poisson bracket according to

\displaystyle\{f,g\}=\partialderivative{f}{\xi_\alpha}\,\omega^{-1}_{\alpha\beta}\partialderivative{g}{\xi_\beta}.

(222)

To calculate
\omega^{-1}
, however, we must be careful to restrict
R_{iI}
to the three physical degrees of freedom. To do so, it is convenient to momentarily consider a local patch of
\mathrm{SO}(3)
parameterized by the Lie algebra-valued variables
\phi_{A}
defined by:

\displaystyle R_{iI}=R^{0}_{iA}(\delta_{AI}+\epsilon_{AIB}\phi_{B}),

(223)

where
R^{0}
is a constant element of
\mathrm{SO}(3)
. A short calculation verifies that
\omega
is given as

\displaystyle\omega=-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{IJK}L_{K}\differential{\phi_I}\wedge\differential{\phi_J}+\differential{L_K}\wedge\differential{\phi_K}.

(224)

Here, we have expanded each term to lowest nontrivial order in
\phi
, which will ultimately lead to Poisson Brackets accurate to
O(\phi^{0})
. We may now write the matrix
\omega
, in
3\times 3
blocks (with ordering
(\phi,L)
, as:

\displaystyle\omega_{IJ}=\begin{pmatrix}-\epsilon_{IJK}L_{K}&-\delta_{IJ}\\
\delta_{IJ}&0\end{pmatrix}

(225)

Its inverse is readily found to be:

\displaystyle\omega^{IJ}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\delta_{IJ}\\
-\delta_{IJ}&-\epsilon_{IJK}L_{K}\end{pmatrix}

(226)

This result allows us to read off Poisson brackets between
\phi,L
, which in turn allow us to calculate the Poisson brackets between
R_{iI},L_{K}
as:

\displaystyle\{R_{iI},L_{K}\}

\displaystyle=R^{0}_{iJ}\epsilon_{JIB}\quantity{\phi_B, L_K}=R^{0}_{iJ}\epsilon_{JIK}\rightarrow R_{iJ}\epsilon_{JIK}

(227a)

\displaystyle\{R_{iI},R_{jJ}\}

\displaystyle=0,

(227b)

\displaystyle\{L_{I},L_{J}\}

\displaystyle=-\epsilon_{IJK}L_{K}.

(227c)

For (
227a
), because our Poisson brackets are accurate only to order
\phi
, it is appropriate to replace
R^{0}_{iJ}=R_{iJ}
. Additionally, the standard Poisson brackets for angular momentum (
227c
) are read immediately from (
226
), albeit with a relative sign, which is a consequence of
L_{I}
being attached to the body frame rather than the space frame.

Now that the coordinates, momenta, and Poisson brackets are known, the next step is to actually specify a Hamiltonian for the problem. Left SO(3)-invariance, together with the orthogonality constraint, implies that
H
can only depend on
L_{A}
. The natural minimal choice is:

\displaystyle H=\frac{1}{2}L_{I}I^{-1}_{IJ}L_{J},

(228)

where
I^{-1}
is a symmetric constant matrix compatible with the symmetries of the rigid body. Defining
{\bf\it\omega}=I^{-1}\mathbf{L}
and
\Omega_{IJ}=\epsilon_{IJK}\omega_{K}
, we may then compute Hamilton’s equations (
\dot{f}=\{f,H\}
) as

\displaystyle\dot{R_{iI}}

\displaystyle=R_{iL}\epsilon_{LIK}(I^{-1}\mathbf{L})_{K}=R_{iL}\Omega_{LI},

(229a)

\displaystyle\dot{\mathbf{L}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{L}\times{\bf\it\omega}=-{\bf\it\omega}\times(I{\bf\it\omega}).

(229b)

The first line verifies that the object
I^{-1}\mathbf{L}
corresponds to the angular velocity in the rotating reference frame, and the second line is called
Euler’s equations
, giving the evolution of the angular momentum in the rotating frame
José and Saletan 1998
. Evolution of
R(t)
preserves the orthogonality of
R
automatically, as it should, because
\Omega
is antisymmetric.

Finally, before turning to derive active and dissipative corrections to Hamiltonian mechanics in this context, it helps to put our results into the language of Sec.
2
. To that end, we define the “chemical potentials” corresponding to
R_{iI}
and
L_{I}
as

\displaystyle\mu_{iI}

\displaystyle=\partialderivative{H}{R_{iI}}=0,

(230a)

\displaystyle\nu_{I}

\displaystyle=\partialderivative{H}{L_I}=\omega_{I}\ \ (\nu_{IJ}=\epsilon_{IJK}\nu_{K}).

(230b)

For brevity, let us also introduce a shorthand notation for derivatives with respect to
R_{iI}
and
L_{a}
:

\displaystyle\partial_{iI}

\displaystyle\equiv\partialderivative{R_{iI}},

(231a)

\displaystyle D_{K}

\displaystyle\equiv\partialderivative{L_K}.

(231b)

With these definitions, the Fokker-Planck operator for dissipationless Hamiltonian rigid-body dynamics may be written as

\displaystyle W=\partial_{iJ}R_{iK}\epsilon_{KJA}\nu_{A}+D_{A}\epsilon_{ABC}L_{B}\nu_{C}.

(232)

To be explicit, let us show directly that this
W
is invariant under T. For the first term, notice that
(\partial_{iJ}R_{iK})\nu_{KJ}=3\delta_{JK}{\nu_{KJ}}=0
, due to
\nu_{JK}=-\nu_{KJ}
. Then under time-reversal, using that momenta
L_{I}
are odd:

\displaystyle\partial_{iJ}R_{iK}\nu_{KJ}\rightarrow R_{iK}(-\nu_{KJ})(-\partial_{iJ})=\partial_{iJ}R_{iK}\nu_{KJ}.

(233)

Similarly, for the second term
(D_{A}\epsilon_{ABC}L_{B}\nu_{C}=0)
, meaning that

\displaystyle D_{A}\epsilon_{ABC}L_{B}\nu_{C}\rightarrow\epsilon_{ABC}L_{B}\nu_{C}(D_{A}+\nu_{A})=D_{A}\epsilon_{ABC}L_{B}\nu_{C}-\epsilon_{AAC}\nu_{C}-\epsilon_{ABC}L_{B}\frac{\partial^{2}H}{\partial L_{A}\partial L_{C}}=D_{A}\epsilon_{ABC}L_{B}\nu_{C}.

(234)

Accordingly, we find that (
232
) is time-reversal symmetric. With the Hamiltonian mechanics derived in this subsection as a starting point, we are now ready to study modifications to
L
(and therefore
W
) corresponding to activity or dissipation.

6.2
Dissipative rigid-body dynamics

Having cast rigid-body dynamics in a convenient Hamiltonian formalism, we may now apply the methods of this work to explore the natural dissipative terms that can complement the Hamiltonian dynamics of rigid bodies. We will be especially interested in dissipative terms that violate energy conservation (associated with the macroscopic rotation of the collective body) while
preserving
angular momentum conservation. This is certainly the situation for some systems of physical interest including stars and galaxies
Binney and Tremaine 2011
, although neither may strictly be modelled as a rigid body.

In order to impose angular momentum conservation, we will have to again use the Noether theorem of Sec.
2.6
, so the first step is to write down the conserved quantity (angular momentum) interms of our variables
R_{iI}
and
L_{A}
. To obtain the angular momentum in the space frame in terms of the body frame objects
L_{A}
, we must pass to the space frame:

\displaystyle L_{a}=R_{aA}L_{A}

(235)

We would like all three components
L_{a}
to be conserved.
According to the Noether theorem (
80
), conserving
L_{a}
corresponds to demanding
W
is invariant under the following shifts:

\displaystyle\partial_{iJ}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\partial_{iJ}+\partialderivative{L_a}{R_{iJ}}=\partial_{iJ}+\delta_{ai}L_{J},

(236a)

\displaystyle D_{B}

\displaystyle\rightarrow D_{B}+\partialderivative{L_a}{L_B}=D_{B}+R_{aB}.

(236b)

The question addressed in this section is: what form of
dissipative
terms are allowed, if the above shift symmetries are to be demanded?

Before tackling the question of dissipative terms, it is instructive to check that the ordinary Hamiltonian mechanics we have derived for the rigid body conserves angular momentum. This amounts to checking that (
232
) is invariant under the shift symmetries (
236
). The change in the
W
from (
232
) under (
236
) is then

\displaystyle\delta W

\displaystyle=\delta_{ai}L_{I}R_{iJ}\epsilon_{IJB}\nu_{B}+R_{aA}\epsilon_{AJK}L_{J}\nu_{K}=0.

(237)

As a result, we conclude that the Hamiltonian evolution of a rigid body conserves angular momentum in the space frame.

To find a dissipative term that conserves angular momentum, we should start by looking for an motif involving
\partial
and
D
that is invariant under (
236
). It is natural to simply look for such a term that is a linear combination of
\partial
and
D
. Following the same logic as above, one may show that the following object is invariant:

\displaystyle M_{A}=\partial_{iJ}R_{iK}\epsilon_{AKJ}+D_{I}\epsilon_{AIJ}L_{J}.

(238)

where
\epsilon_{AKJ}=\varepsilon^{A}_{KJ}
, for fixed
A
, are the generators of SO(3) in the adjoint representation. We may combine these motifs into a dissipative term entering the Lagrangian, giving a general dissipative term at lowest order in the fields
R
and
L
:

\displaystyle W^{\prime}=-(\partial_{iJ}R_{iK}\varepsilon^{\alpha}_{KJ}+D_{A}\varepsilon^{\alpha}_{A}BL_{B})Q^{\alpha\beta}(R_{iK}\varepsilon^{\beta}_{KJ}\partial_{iJ}+\varepsilon^{\beta}_{AB}L_{B}(D_{A}+\nu_{A})).

(239)

Here, the matrix
Q^{\alpha\beta}
is positive semidefinite, and related to the microscopic noise variance in a way that we will make explicit shortly. The symmetric
Q
matrix must be chosen to be invariant under the body group—the ‘right group invariance’ of Sec.
5
. For the purposes of this work: we will focus mostly on the case of isotropic noise,
Q^{\alpha\beta}=\Gamma\delta^{\alpha\beta}
—in this simple case, the dissipative part of the Lagrangian becomes:

\displaystyle W^{\prime}=-\Gamma(\partial_{iJ}R_{iK}\epsilon_{AKJ}+D_{I}\varepsilon^{\alpha}_{IB}L_{B})(\partial_{iJ}R_{iK}\epsilon_{AKJ}+(D_{I}+\nu_{I})\varepsilon^{\alpha}_{IB}L_{B}).

(240)

Let us now explore the consequences of
W^{\prime}
for the equations of motion. The part of
W^{\prime}
featuring only one derivative is:

\displaystyle W^{\prime}\sim-\Gamma(\partial_{iJ}R_{iK}\epsilon_{AKJ}+D_{I}\epsilon_{AIB}L_{B})(\nu_{I}\epsilon_{AIB}L_{B})

(241)

The dissipative part of the Lagrangian modifies both the equations of motion for
R_{iJ}
and
L_{A}
. Beginning with the modification of
\dot{R}
(including the contribution both of
W
and
W^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle\dot{R}_{iI}=R_{iJ}\epsilon_{JIL}(\nu_{L}+\Gamma(L\times{\bf\it\nu})_{L}).

(242)

Importantly, the dissipative term modifies the relationship between angular velocity
{\bf\it\omega}
and angular momentum
\mathbf{L}
: we find

\displaystyle{\bf\it\omega}={\bf\it\nu}+\Gamma\mathbf{L}\times{\bf\it\nu}.

(243)

Secondly, for
\dot{\mathbf{L}}
, we find a modification of Euler’s equation:

\displaystyle\dot{\mathbf{L}}=\mathbf{L}\times{\bf\it\nu}+\Gamma\,\mathbf{L}\times(\mathbf{L}\times{\bf\it\nu})=\mathbf{L}\times{\bf\it\omega}.

(244)

Already, we can see a striking similarity to the Landau-Lifshitz-Gilbert damping term that arises in the Heisenberg spin chain (cf. (
146
)). This similarity is nontrivial: in the Heisenberg spin chain, we demanded only that the total angular momentum
S^{2}
is conserved, while in the rigid-body case we conserve each component of
\mathbf{L}
separately. The reason for the similarity is that the length of total angular momentum
\mathbf{L}\cdot\mathbf{L}
is the same in both the space and body frame; however, it was not trivial that we can always choose dynamics for
R_{iI}
that preserves the remaining two components of angular momentum! Further, we have seen that the damping must break the simple relation between angular velocity and angular momentum, in order to conserve angular momentum in the presence of noise. This is a subtle result that manifestly follows from our “cookbook” protocol for developing theories of dissipative dynamics based on symmetry.

Figure 4:
Simulation of the dynamics of a rigid body under (
244
). For the simulation, we use dimensionless units and work in the principal axis basis, where
I
is dimensionless with
I_{xx}=1,I_{yy}=2,I_{zz}=3
. We choose
\Gamma=0.5
. The initial conditions are chosen such that the body rotates about the unstable
\hat{\mathbf{y}}
axis. As
t\rightarrow\infty
, the body’s angular velocity
{\bf\it\omega}
(calculated according to (
243
)) aligns with the body frame
\hat{\mathbf{z}}
axis, that is, the axis with largest moment of inertia.

It is also worth considering the situation of a rigid body whose angular momentum is dissipating in addition to its energy. This would be the case, for example, for rotons in contact with a bath that carries angular momentum
de Groot and Mazur 1962
. In this case where the equation of motion for
\mathbf{L}
is subject to isotropic noise, we simply add the usual quadratic disspative term to
W
:

\displaystyle W^{\prime}=-\Gamma D_{I}(D_{I}+\nu_{I})

(245)

for
\Gamma>0
. This piece adds a damping term to Euler’s equations:

\displaystyle\dot{\mathbf{L}}=\mathbf{L}\times{\bf\it\omega}-\Gamma{\bf\it\omega}.

(246)

A naive relaxation term
\dot{\mathbf{L}}\sim-\Gamma\mathbf{L}
does not satisfy time-reversibility.

6.3
Active rigid-body dynamics

In this final subsection, we will explore the T-odd terms that may appear for rigid-body motion. What other dissipationless terms may be added in the absence of time-reversal symmetry?

Let us first try to guess modifications of Euler’s equations that violate angular momentum conservation. In the limit of weak noise, a general dissipationless term that is odd under time-reversal symmetry is:

\displaystyle W_{\mathrm{T-breaking}}=\partial_{iJ}R_{iK}A_{KJ}+D_{I}B_{IJ}(D_{J}+\nu_{J})

(247)

for an antisymmetric matrices
A
and
B
that may depend on even powers of the coordinates
L
(due to left-SO(3) symmetry). These matrices
A
and
B
, having two body-frame indices, must also be an invariant tensor of the body group. In the case where there exists a constant, antisymmetric matrix that is a body group invariant, we could then include a terms with constant
A
or
B
, corresponding to driven rotation and precession about the axes chosen by
A
and
B
. In cases where such a constant antisymmetric invariant tensor does not exist, creating a body group-invariant object requires
A
and
B
to have dependence on the coordinates. For instance, choosing
A=\nu
,
B=L
corresponds to Hamiltonian mechanics, which is dissipationless but time-reversal invariant. To find a
T
-odd dissipationless term, one should choose
A
and
B
to be
T
-even.

We may, however, also be interested in active systems that still conserve angular momentum. In that case, Noether’s theorem constrains the form of
A
and
B
further. If we wish to conserve all three components of
L_{i}
(lab frame angular momentum), then (
236
) demands
A=c(\mathbf{L})\nu
and
B=c(\mathbf{L})L
. In particular, the choice
c(\mathbf{L})=1
corresponds to plain Hamiltonian mechanics. In the absence of a
T
-odd scalar
c(\mathbf{L})
(a time-reversal pseudoscalar), angular-momentum conserving active terms are not possible.

If angular momentum is
not
conserved however,
A
and
B
may be any antisymmetric matrices compatible with body frame symmetries. Let us write down a simple example where
B=0
(maintaining the form of Euler’s equation) and:

\displaystyle A_{IJ}=\lambda\epsilon_{IJK}\epsilon_{KLM}L_{N}\nu_{M}

(248)

This modifies the relationship between angular velocity
{\bf\it\omega}
and angular momentum
\mathbf{L}
:

\displaystyle{\bf\it\omega}={\bf\it\nu}+\lambda({\bf\it\nu}\times\mathbf{L}).

(249)

The equation of motion for
\mathbf{L}
becomes

\displaystyle\dot{\mathbf{L}}=\mathbf{L}\times{\bf\it\omega}-\lambda({\bf\it\nu}\times\mathbf{L}).

(250)

Of course, in situations with even less symmetry, other active terms are possible. If the body has a preferred axis, say
\hat{\mathbf{z}}
, then one may choose
A_{IJ}=\epsilon_{IJK}\hat{z}_{K}
, corresponding to driven rotation about this axis. Choosing
B_{IJ}=\epsilon_{IJK}\hat{z}_{K}
instead corresponds to driven precession about the
\hat{\mathbf{z}}
axis.

7
Field theory

We now discuss genuine field theories using the formalism developed above. Both to make contact with recent literature
Crossley et al. 2017
;
Glorioso et al. 2017
, and also to keep the discussion a little cleaner in theories where the number of fields can proliferate quickly, in this section we will use the notation
\pi_{a}=-\mathrm{i}\partial_{a}
, and
H=-\mathrm{i}W
. Since we will work almost exclusively in linear response and in the limit of weak noise, it is simple to convert between the path integral approach described in Sec.
2.7
, and the operator formalism used in the rest of the paper. However, we always present formulas for
\mathcal{L}
in such a way that they can be easily converted into operator expressions if desired.

The focus of this section will be on active matter whose dynamics break a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry (usually spatial parity in two dimensions) and time-reversal symmetry (but not their combination). Microscopic origins of such systems can include includes spinning particles, granular gases, and Brownian rollers
Liebchen and Levis 2022
;
Banerjee et al. 2017
;
Han et al. 2021
. Although these microscopic models make particle-based simulations possible, a field theory can elucidate the aspects of the physics which are universal and model-independent. Of course, our Wilsonian approach to stochastic dynamics is naturally designed for such effective theories.

As we will see, our EFT approach allows a more streamlined derivation of a number of recent results in the literature on active solids and fluids, and in some cases also highlights some subtle or overlooked phenomena.

7.1
Odd diffusitivity

To warm-up to the study of hydrodynamics, let us begin by considering a minimal model. Firstly, we remind the reader that hydrodynamics is the effective theory that describes how a thermalizing system reaches equilibrium

(
)

. The degrees of freedom within hydrodynamics are associated with the densities of conserved quantities, and/or the Goldstone bosons associated with any spontaneously broken continuous symmetries. We will see examples of each in this paper, but we will leave the development of an exhaustive EFT framework for active fluids to another paper.

Now, we turn to our minimal model. Suppose that we have a single conserved scalar field
n(\mathbf{x},t)
—e.g., the number density of conserved particles—defined on a
d
-dimensional region coupled to a thermal bath of temperature
T_{0}=\beta_{0}^{-1}
. We consider expanding the fluid around static equilibrium
n(\mathbf{x},t)=n_{0}
: perturbations are denoted with
\delta n\equiv n-n_{0}
. By the central limit theorem (and Wilsonian philosophy),
\Phi
can be taken to be quadratic, at leading order:

\displaystyle\Phi=\beta_{0}\int\differential[d]{x}\frac{1}{2\chi}(\delta n)^{2}.

(251)

The constant
\chi
is known as the charge/number susceptibility. We will now derive the minimal Lagrangian in terms of
\delta n(\mathbf{x})
and its conjugate noise field
\pi(\mathbf{x})
. For sake of illustration, let us briefly deal first with the thermal case in which
T
-symmetry is present, and we will demand parity
P
as well. Additionally, since the total charge
\delta Q=\int\differential[3]{x}\delta n
is conserved, the Noether theorem demands that the Lagrangian is invariant under shifting
\pi\to\pi+1
. Then the simplest Lagrangian is

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=\pi\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{t}\delta n+\mathrm{i}\beta_{0}^{-1}\chi D_{ij}\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{i}\pi\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{j}\,(\pi-\mathrm{i}\mu)

(252)

where
D_{ij}
(positive semidefinite) are the diffusion constants, with prefactor
\beta^{-1}\chi
chosen for convenience. The chemical potential

\displaystyle\mu(x)=\functionalderivative{\Phi}{(\delta n(x))}=\frac{\beta_{0}\delta n}{\chi}.

(253)

In the Itô scheme, (
252
) describes, through varying w.r.t the noise field
\pi
, a fluctuating diffusion equation

\displaystyle\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{t}\delta n=D_{ij}\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\delta n+\xi,

(254)

where the white noise
\xi
satisfies

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\xi(\vb x, t)\xi(\vb x^\prime, t^\prime)}=2\beta_{0}^{-1}\chi D_{ij}\,\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\delta(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\,\delta(t-t^{\prime}).

(255)

This is how the usual Fick’s law arises from fluctuating hydrodynamics with derivative white noise. For normal isotropic achiral systems,
D_{ij}=D\delta_{ij}
is the most general form possible, with
D\geq 0
required for stability.

For chiral systems, one can instead consider
D_{ij}=D^{\mathrm{odd}}\epsilon_{ij}
.
D^{\mathrm{odd}}
is unsurprisingly called the odd diffusivity
Hargus et al. 2021
. Note that
D^{\mathrm{odd}}
does not contribute to the equation of motion: this can be seen either by noting that
D^{\mathrm{odd}}
leads to a total derivative term in
\Phi
, or by evaluating the equation of motion to be
\partial_{t}n=D^{\mathrm{odd}}\epsilon_{ij}\partial_{i}\partial_{j}n=0
.

Nevertheless, it is instructive to see how such a term can arise in the action, because, e.g., at the nonlinear level odd diffusivity can contribute to the equations of motion.
14
14

14

For example, imagine there are two species of charge
n^{a},a=1,2
satisfying diffusion equation
\partial_{t}\delta n^{a}=\partial_{i}(D_{ij}(n^{b})\partial_{j}\delta n^{a})
. Taking
D_{ij}(n^{b})=D^{\mathrm{odd}}\epsilon_{ij}n^{b}n^{b}
as T-odd, we have
\partial_{t}\delta n^{a}=D^{\mathrm{odd}}\epsilon^{ij}n^{b}\partial_{i}\delta n^{b}\partial_{j}\delta n^{a}
.
For the sake of simplicity, let us assume an isotropic system and work in
d=2
so that there exists a rank-2 isotropic antisymmetric tensor. Assume the stationary distribution (
251
).

Then way may write down the effective Lagrangian that satisfies
gT
symmetry with
g=P
. The minimal effective Lagrangian is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\delta n,\pi)=\pi\partial_{t}\delta n-\beta_{0}^{-1}(\partial_{i}\pi)\chi D^{\mathrm{odd}}\epsilon_{ij}\partial_{j}\mathrm{i}(\pi-\mathrm{i}\mu)+\mathrm{i}\beta_{0}^{-1}\chi D\partial_{i}\pi\partial_{i}(\pi-\mathrm{i}\mu).

(256)

The antisymmetric of
D^{\mathrm{anti}}_{ij}=D^{\mathrm{odd}}\epsilon_{ij}
allows this coefficient to enter at linear order in
\pi
, meaning it is not related to noise—the statistics of the noise are still (
255
). Upon variation of
\pi
, we find (
254
) with
D
being the dissipative diffusion constant;
D^{\mathrm{odd}}
is now a T-odd force in the effective field theory. Although
D^{\mathrm{odd}}
