does not enter the equation of motion, it may nonetheless affect
\delta n
when the boundary conditions depend only on the flux
J_{i}\equiv D_{ij}\partial^{\,}_{j}\delta n
.

7.2
Odd elasticity

We now consider an example of an active continuous medium: the two-dimensional odd elastic solid
Fruchart et al. 2022
. Following much of the literature
Scheibner et al. 2020
, we will focus on an overdamped elastic solid, such that the only degrees of freedom are the displacement fields of the solid
u_{i}(x)
. These are Goldstone bosons of a spontaneously broken translation symmetry
\mathrm{U}(1)^{2}
, and for simplicity, following
Scheibner et al. 2020
, we assume that these are the only hydrodynamic degrees of freedom.

As usual in this framework, the first thing to do is to postulate the form of
\Phi
. For the odd elastic solid, there are two plausible choices, and we will explore them both in turn.

7.2.1
Gyroscopic potential

The first plausible choice for
\Phi
is

\Phi=\int\mathrm{d}^{2}x\,\left[c_{1}(\partial_{i}u_{j})^{2}+c_{2}(\partial_{n}u_{n})^{2}\right]

(257)

The reason this choice is natural is that as Goldstone bosons, we may expect that
u_{i}\rightarrow u_{i}+a_{i}
should be a shift symmetry for any constant vector
a_{i}
. This seemingly mundane choice will have physical consequences which we will soon discuss. Notice that
\mu
now has derivatives: treating
c_{1,2}
as constants, we find that

\mu_{i}(x)=\frac{\delta\Phi}{\delta u_{i}(x)}=-c_{1}\partial_{j}\partial_{j}u_{i}-c_{2}\partial_{i}\partial_{j}u_{j}

(258)

already contains two derivatives.

Next we turn to the Lagrangian
\mathcal{L}
. Assuming PT-symmetry, and studying
overdamped
Goldstone dynamics where
u_{i}
(but not momenta) are the only degrees of freedom, we find

\mathcal{L}(u,\pi)=\pi_{i}\partial_{t}u_{i}+\mathrm{i}\pi_{i}\left(Q_{1}\delta_{ij}+Q_{2}\epsilon_{ij}\right)\left(\pi_{j}-\mathrm{i}\mu_{j}\right).

(259)

Q_{1}
corresponds to T-even effects, while
Q_{2}
corresponds to T-odd effects. The equations of motion are then:

\partial_{t}u_{i}=-\left(Q_{1}\delta_{ij}+Q_{2}\epsilon_{ij}\right)\mu_{j}

(260)

To understand what these mean, it is helpful to consider plane waves of the form
u_{i}\sim\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(kx-\omega t)}
:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u_{i}

\displaystyle=\left(Q_{1}\delta_{ij}+Q_{2}\epsilon_{ij}\right)\left(c_{1}k^{2}u_{j}+c_{2}k_{j}k_{i}u_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(Q_{1}c_{1}k^{2}\delta_{ij}u_{j}+Q_{2}c_{1}k^{2}\epsilon_{ij}u_{j}+Q_{1}c_{2}\delta_{ij}k_{j}k_{i}u_{i}+Q_{2}c_{2}\epsilon_{ij}k_{j}k_{l}u_{l}\right).

(261)

Because we choose
k_{i}
to lie in the
x
-direction, we find (neglecting noise contributions)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u_{x}

\displaystyle=-k^{2}\left(Q_{1}(c_{1}+c_{2})u_{x}+Q_{2}c_{1}u_{y}\right)=-k^{2}\left((B+\mu)u_{x}+K^{\circ}u_{y}\right)

(262a)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u_{y}

\displaystyle=-k^{2}\left(Q_{1}c_{1}u_{y}-Q_{2}(c_{1}+c_{2})u_{x}\right)=-k^{2}\left(\mu u_{y}-(A+K^{\circ})u_{x}\right).

(262b)

where we have introduced the bulk
B
and shear
\mu
elastic moduli, along with their odd counterparts
A
and
K^{\circ}
respectively
Scheibner et al. 2020
.
Observe that the following equality holds:

\frac{A}{K^{\circ}}=\frac{B}{\mu}.

(263)

An example of a system that obeys this “gyroscopic condition” is found in
Nash et al. 2015
.
15
15

15

Mechanistically, this new force law is just a rotation of the passive force one would expect of Hookean springs. Since each spring has the same odd spring constant, this system rotates each force by the same angle. In other words, the net force is just a rotation of the passive force, which is exactly what a gyroscope does.
Consider a triangular lattice consisting of springs that have an additional odd spring constant: they obey the Hookean spring force law, but with a chiral transverse force proportional to the odd spring constant:

F_{i}=\left(k\delta_{ij}+k^{\circ}\epsilon_{ij}\right)\delta r_{j}.

(264)

Here
\delta r_{i}
is the displacement of each spring relative to its rest displacement. The force-displacement matrix for this system is not symmetric which implies the linear response is nonconservative and thus time-reversal symmetry is broken. The coefficient
k^{\circ}
multiplies the parity-odd
\epsilon_{ij}
, but the dynamics is overdamped and thus this dissipationless force also is odd under time-reversal; hence the system is overall PT-symmetric and it must obey (
263
).

(a)

(b)

Figure 5:
(a)
Triangular lattice made of odd Hookean springs which obey the transverse force law. The pink arrows show that the pointwise force is just a passive rotation of the passive force one would expect of Hookean springs. Notice that this rotation of the effective force is what a gyroscope does.
(b)
A plaquette of the honeycomb lattice with next-nearest-neighbor bonds. Angular momentum is conserved since the tension along each bond is cancelled by its corresponding color.

To show that working with a microscopic system directly can lead to the explicit breaking of additional symmetries, let us add a next-nearest-neighbor odd spring constant with distinct parity-even and parity-odd coefficients (
k^{\,}_{2}
and
k_{2}^{\circ}
, respectively). Naïvely, an identical argument to the above goes through, and the system should still be PT-symmetric. However, an explicit microscopic calculation
Scheibner et al. 2020
shows that theories with nearest- and next-nearest-neighbor odd springs does not obey (
263
). What happens is that the stationary distribution, captured by
\Phi
,
explicitly breaks parity
:

\Phi=\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{d}^{2}x\left[c^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{1}(\nabla\times u)^{2}+(c_{1}+c_{2})(\nabla\cdot u)^{2}+c^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{3}(\nabla\cdot u)(\nabla\times u)\right]\,,~~

(265)

where the P-odd coefficient
c_{3}
depends on the details of the microscopic spring constants. Repeating the calculation above, we find that

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=Q_{1}c_{2}+2Q_{2}c_{3},

(266a)

\displaystyle\mu

\displaystyle=Q_{1}c_{1}-Q_{2}c_{3},

(266b)

\displaystyle K^{\circ}

\displaystyle=Q_{1}c_{3}+Q_{2}c_{1},

(266c)

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=Q_{2}c_{2}-2Q_{1}c_{3}.

(266d)

Thus the ratio (
263
) is broken and we get four distinct elastic coefficients, due to the explicit breaking of parity. Observe that it is possible for
\mu<0
or
B<0
if
Q_{2}
is large (which is not incompatible with the stability of the theory); however,
B+2\mu\geq 0
since
Q_{1},c_{1},c_{2}\geq 0
are all required by stability of the fixed point.

Interestingly, we can explicitly break parity, meaning that
c_{3}\neq 0
, but restore time-reversal symmetry, meaning that
Q_{2}=0
. We still in this case find that the two new odd elastic moduli do not vanish, implying that time-reversal symmetry breaking was not essential to see odd elastic coefficients
Lier et al. 2022
. In this case, we do see that
B,\mu>0
directly, while
A=-2K^{\circ}
.

Lastly, let us return to a point remarked at the beginning of this subsection: the choice that
\Phi
had a shift symmetry has led to direct physical consequences. Notice that the Lagrangian (
259
), or more formally the generator of the Fokker-Planck equation, has no derivatives acting on
\pi
. This means that when we write down the noisy equations of motion:
\partial_{t}u_{i}=\cdots+\xi_{i}
, the variance of the noise will be

\langle\xi_{i}(x,t)\xi_{j}(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})\rangle\sim\delta(x-x^{\prime})\delta(t-t^{\prime}).

(267)

In other words, this noise will
translate
the solid in space. This theory therefore corresponds to an odd solid that is pinned to some substrate, and therefore can slide around over time. In most experiments, this is indeed likely to be the case.

7.2.2
Translationally pinned potential

However, it is also reasonable to postulate—at least theoretically—the existence of an intrinsically odd solid, which itself forms a closed system, and where the stochastic dynamics has only arisen due to integrating out short wavelength fluctuations of the solid itself. Along the lines of the above discussion, let us now turn to discuss the case where the solid itself is taken to be an isolated system, that cannot slide itself spontaneously in the plane. Then, the quantities

\displaystyle X_{i}

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}^{2}x\,u_{x},

(268a)

\displaystyle Y_{i}

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}^{2}x\,u_{y}\,,

(268b)

are conserved. Noether’s theorem then implies that the Lagrangian must be invariant under
\pi_{x,y}\rightarrow\pi_{x,y}+1
, and therefore (
259
) must be modified to

\mathcal{L}=\pi_{i}\partial_{t}u_{i}+\mathrm{i}\partial_{i}\pi_{j}Q_{ijkl}\partial_{k}\left(\pi_{l}-\mathrm{i}\mu_{l}\right).

(269)

Due to the addition of two extra derivatives on
\pi_{i}
, if we take the same choice of
\Phi
as before, evidently the elastic equations of motion will be fourth order. On the other hand, since
X_{i}
and
Y_{i}
are conserved, it need not be the case that
\Phi
depend on
u_{i}
only via derivatives: we are
not
just as likely to find the solid in all configurations translated by a constant. So now, let us instead postulate

\Phi=\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{d}^{2}x\;u_{i}u_{i}

(270)

For simplicity we have chosen units so there is no constant prefactor in
\Phi
above. In this case
\mu_{i}=u_{i}
,
and the equation of motion is

\partial_{t}u_{i}=Q_{ijkl}\partial_{j}\partial_{k}u_{l}+\xi_{i},

(271)

where

\langle\xi_{j}(x,t)\xi_{l}(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})\rangle=-2Q_{ijkl}\partial_{k}\partial_{l}\delta^{(2}(x-x^{\prime})\delta(t-t^{\prime}).

(272)

Now we want to know what components are allowed in the fourth rank tensor
Q_{ijkl}
. We consider the basis of
2\times 2
matrices,
\{\omega^{i}\}
such that

\omega^{0}=\delta_{ij},\quad\omega^{1}=\epsilon_{ij},\quad\omega^{2}=\sigma_{ij}^{z},\quad\omega^{3}=\sigma_{ij}^{x}

(273)

In this basis, we express the fourth rank tensor
Q_{ijkl}
as a
4\times 4
matrix;

Q^{\alpha\beta}=\left(\omega^{\alpha}\right)_{ij}Q_{ijkl}\left(\omega^{\beta}\right)_{kl}

(274)

First, we assume that the solid is isotropic for simplicity, as before. This means that
\omega^{2,3}
lie in the same real-valued representation of the rotation group distinct from
\omega^{0,1}
. In order to be invariant under rotations,

Q^{\alpha\beta}=\begin{pmatrix}Q^{00}&Q^{01}&0&0\\
Q^{10}&Q^{11}&0&0\\
0&0&Q^{22}&Q^{23}\\
0&0&-Q^{23}&Q^{22}\end{pmatrix}

(275)

Table
2
describes how the components of
Q^{\alpha\beta}
transform under parity, inversion, and time-reversal symmetry.

Component

Parity

Inversion

Time

\sigma_{z}\otimes\sigma_{x}-\sigma_{x}\otimes\sigma_{z}

Table 2:
Tensor transformations for
Q_{ijkl}
under parity, inversion, and time-reversal.

While we will not consider all possible combinations exhaustively, let us note that if we wish to have PT-symmetry only, the only further constraint is that
Q^{10}=-Q^{01}
.

As above, we can calculate the elastic moduli. Assuming PT-symmetry, we find:

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=Q^{00}+Q^{22},

(276a)

\displaystyle\mu

\displaystyle=Q^{11}+Q^{22},

(276b)

\displaystyle K^{\circ}

\displaystyle=Q^{01}+\frac{1}{2}Q^{23},

(276c)

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=0.

(276d)

Note that because
\Phi
is explicitly parity invariant,
A
and
K^{\circ}
are now necessarily T-odd coefficients.

A system with
A=0
and
K^{\circ},B,\mu\neq 0
arises in an active solid on a two-dimensional honeycomb lattice. At vertices of the lattice, we place hinges between the edges, which are active components that deform according to the deformation of its nearest clockwise neighbor
Fruchart et al. 2022
. We further assume that
B
and
\mu
are nonzero so that the solid resists changes in volume and shape (this can be realized by adding next-nearest-neighbor (NNN) Hookean springs). Each NNN bond responds to the strain of its counterclockwise neighbor in the unit cell. Reversing time would imply that each NNN bond responds to the tension of its clockwise neighbor which is tantamount to a parity (P) transformation. We therefore expect this system to have PT symmetry. Our effective theory shows that angular momentum must be conserved so long as
Q^{11}=0
, which is always the case when (as in this microscopic model) the system is invariant under a global rotation
(u_{i}=\epsilon_{ij}x_{j}
). In this microscopic model we can indeed confirm that angular momentum is conserved, and therefore
A=0
: all forces from Hookean springs are radial so momentum is conserved pair by pair, while the active hinges exert equal and opposite torques on the two bonds they connect.

7.3
Chiral fluid

Now, let us turn to the most complex example of fluid we will consider in this paper: a two-dimensional chiral fluid with both density and momentum conservation. There are three possible “odd” transport coefficients we should expect: odd diffusivity
Hargus et al. 2021
(which we have already discussed), odd viscosity
Banerjee et al. 2017
, and odd stress
Han et al. 2021
. While the former two have a close analogy with dissipationless Hall transport in equilibrium systems, the last one appears to us unique to active phases: in particular, it violates the thermal KMS condition in the presence of a generic background geometry
Huang and Lucas 2022
.

7.3.1
Odd viscosity

Let us begin by discussing odd viscosity. For simplicity, we’ll momentarily neglect fluctuations of density, and assume that the most likely fluid configuration has zero velocity. Then for small fluctuations around equilibrium, the stationary distribution is given by the nonrelativistic kinetic energy (with background mass density / momentum susceptibility
Hartnoll et al. 2018

\rho
):

\displaystyle\Phi=\beta_{0}\int\differential[d]{x}\frac{\rho}{2}u_{i}u_{i}.

(277)

As usual, we introduce ‘chemical potentials’ as

\displaystyle\mu_{i}=\functionalderivative{\Phi}{u_i}=\rho u_{i}.

(278)

In the usual nonactive case, the effective Lagrangian should satisfy
T
and
P
and also be consistent with conservation of momentum,
\mathbf{P}=\int\differential[d]{x}\rho\mathbf{u}
. The minimal Lagrangian therefore features two gradients of
\mathbf{u}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(u,\pi)=\pi_{i}\partial_{t}u_{i}+\mathrm{i}\beta_{0}^{-1}\rho^{-2}\eta_{ijkl}\partial_{i}\pi_{j}\partial_{k}(\pi_{l}-\mathrm{i}\mu_{l}).

(279)

The constant object
\eta_{ijkl}
is known as the viscosity tensor. In the Itô scheme of the MSR path integral, integrating out
\pi_{i}
leads to a fluctuating diffusion equation for the velocity:

\displaystyle\rho\partial_{t}u_{i}=\eta_{ijkl}\partial_{j}\partial_{k}u_{l}+\xi_{i},

(280)

where the statistics of the white noise
\xi_{i}(\mathbf{x},t)
are such that

\displaystyle\langle\xi_{i}(\mathbf{x},t)\xi_{j}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},t^{\prime})\rangle=\beta_{0}^{-1}(\eta_{ikjl}+\eta_{jlik})\partial_{k}\partial_{l}\delta(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\delta(t-t^{\prime}).

(281)

Thus viscous hydrodynamics naturally emerges as the simplest
P
,
T
-invariant effective field theory for conserved momentum. We will discuss the pressure and stress, so far omitted, in the next subsection.

However, in a chiral fluid composed, for example, of spinning particles, P and Tmay be violated such that only PT is conserved. In that case, we may decompose

\displaystyle\eta_{ijkl}=\eta^{\mathrm{even}}_{ijkl}+\eta^{\mathrm{odd}}_{ijkl},

(282)

where
\eta^{\mathrm{even}}_{ijkl}=\eta^{\mathrm{even}}_{klij}
,
\eta^{\mathrm{odd}}_{ijkl}=-\eta^{\mathrm{odd}}_{klij}
. The latter is called the odd viscosity
Fruchart et al. 2022
. Note that
\eta^{\mathrm{even}}
, but not
\eta^{\mathrm{odd}}
, contribute to the variance of the statistical fluctuations; hence
\eta^{\mathrm{even}}
must a positive semidefinite tensor.

To find odd viscosity in our effective field theory, we simply need to enforce PT instead of T and P separately. The minimal Lagrangian becomes:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(u,\pi)=\pi_{i}\partial_{t}u_{i}+\beta_{0}^{-1}\rho^{-2}\eta^{\mathrm{odd}}_{ijkl}\partial_{i}\pi_{j}\partial_{k}\mu_{l}+\mathrm{i}\beta_{0}^{-1}\rho^{-2}\eta^{\mathrm{even}}_{ijkl}\partial_{i}\pi_{j}\partial_{k}(\pi_{l}-\mathrm{i}\mu_{l}),

(283)

where, as in the case of odd diffusivity, the antisymmetry of
\eta^{\mathrm{odd}}_{ijkl}
means this coefficient enters only at linear order in
\pi_{i}
. Accordingly,
\eta^{\mathrm{odd}}_{ijkl}
is not related to noise—the statistics of the noise remain (
281
). The resulting equation of motion is in agreement with (
280
).

Our approach differs from
Han et al. 2021
; in this work, the authors attributed the odd viscosity to an “antisymmetric” non-Markovian noise where
\langle\xi(t)\xi(t^{\prime})\sim f(t-t^{\prime})
with
f
an odd, sharply localized function. In our discussion, odd viscosity is allowed due to the gT invariance of the effective Lagrangian, and it is independent of noise as a dissipationless force in the effective theory. Odd viscosity, like odd diffusivity, therefore emerges naturally from the simplest PT-invariant effective field theory of a dissipative incompressible fluid. The fact that we obtain the same hydrodynamic universality class as
Han et al. 2021
therefore explains the emergence of an FDT and a second law of thermodynamics in more microscopically active models as well. Like in the discussion of odd elasticity, our approach does not give an unambiguous definition of the stress tensor, so certain global correlation functions of stress will not be universally captured by our EFT.

7.3.2
Fluid with spinning particles

Let us now work in a regime where both
n
and
\mathbf{u}
are nonzero. We also consider an additional spin density
s
, as slowly varying fields. The spin density
s
could correspond to a local angular momentum density of spinning particles, and we will first assume that
s
is a conserved quantity. The minimal steady-state distribution is given by

\displaystyle\Phi=\beta_{0}\int\mathrm{d}^{d}x~\left[\frac{\rho}{2}(u_{i})^{2}+\frac{1}{2\chi}(\delta n)^{2}+\frac{1}{2\chi_{s}}(\delta s)^{2}\right],

(284)

where
\chi_{s}
is the spin susceptibility. Spinning motions are P-odd and T-odd, but PT-even.

Under PT symmetry, the leading Lagrangian is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=

\displaystyle\pi_{i}\partial_{t}u_{i}+\pi^{n}\partial_{t}\delta n+\pi^{s}\partial_{t}\delta s-n_{0}u_{i}\partial_{i}\pi^{n}-\frac{n_{0}}{\chi\rho}\partial_{i}\pi_{i}\delta n-s_{0}u_{i}\partial_{i}\pi^{s}-\frac{s_{0}}{\chi_{s}\rho}\partial_{i}\pi_{i}\delta s

\displaystyle+\mathrm{i}\beta_{0}^{-1}\rho^{-2}\eta_{ijkl}^{\mathrm{even}}\partial_{i}\pi_{j}\partial_{k}(\pi_{l}-\mathrm{i}\mu_{l})+\sum_{\alpha,\beta=n,s}\mathrm{i}\beta_{0}^{-1}D^{\alpha\beta,\mathrm{even}}\partial_{i}\pi^{\alpha}\partial_{i}(\pi^{\beta}-\mathrm{i}\mu^{\beta})

\displaystyle+\beta_{0}^{-1}\rho^{-2}\eta^{\mathrm{odd}}_{ijkl}\partial_{i}\pi_{j}\partial_{k}\mu_{l}+\sum_{\alpha,\beta=n,s}\beta_{0}^{-1}D^{\alpha\beta,\mathrm{odd}}\epsilon_{ij}\partial_{i}\pi^{\alpha}\partial_{j}\mu^{\beta},

(285)

where
D^{ns}=D^{sn}
. Importantly, we do not include the following invariant term

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\sim\sum_{\alpha=n,s}\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\beta_{0}^{-1}K^{\alpha}\epsilon^{ij}(\partial_{i}\pi_{j}(\pi^{\alpha}-\mathrm{i}\mu^{\alpha})+\partial_{i}(\pi_{j}-\mathrm{i}\mu_{j})\pi^{\alpha}).

(286)

This is because to fulfill the positivity constraint of the noise invariance matrix, one has to also include terms with zero derivative order:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\sim\sum_{\alpha=n,s}\mathrm{i}\beta_{0}^{-1}\Gamma^{\alpha}\pi^{\alpha}(\pi^{\alpha}-\mathrm{i}\mu^{\alpha}).

(287)

Moreover, this zeroth order term is forbidden by the shift symmetry
\pi^{\alpha}\to\pi^{\alpha}+1
corresponding to the conservation of charge and spin. Indeed, (
287
) will lead to equations of motion:
\partial_{t}n\sim-\Gamma^{n}n
and
\partial_{t}s\sim-\Gamma^{s}s
that decay to zero. Without them, the stochastic equations of motion corresponding to (
7.3.2
) are given by

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\delta n+n_{0}\partial_{i}u_{i}-\chi^{-1}D^{n,\mathrm{even}}\partial_{i}^{2}\delta n-\chi_{s}^{-1}D^{ns,\mathrm{even}}\partial_{i}^{2}\delta s

\displaystyle=\xi^{n},

(288a)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\delta s+s_{0}\partial_{i}u_{i}-\chi_{s}^{-1}D^{s,\mathrm{even}}\partial_{i}^{2}\delta s-\chi^{-1}D^{ns,\mathrm{even}}\partial_{i}^{2}\delta n

\displaystyle=\xi^{s},

(288b)

\displaystyle\rho\partial_{t}u_{i}+\chi^{-1}n_{0}\partial_{i}\delta n+\chi_{s}^{-1}s_{0}\partial_{i}\delta s-\eta_{jikl}\partial_{j}\partial_{k}u_{l}

\displaystyle=\xi_{i},

(288c)

with the noise variance

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\xi^\alpha(\vb x, t)\xi^\beta(\vb x^\prime, t^\prime)}

\displaystyle=2\beta_{0}^{-1}D^{\alpha\beta,\mathrm{even}}\,\partial_{i}^{2}\delta(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\,\delta(t-t^{\prime}),

(289a)

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\xi_i(\vb x, t)\xi_j(\vb x^\prime, t^\prime)}

\displaystyle=2\beta_{0}^{-1}\eta_{ikjl}^{\mathrm{even}}\,\partial_{k}\partial_{l}\delta(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\,\delta(t-t^{\prime}),

(289b)

for
\alpha,\beta=n,s
. Therefore, we find that it describes the odd fluid dynamics, including the odd diffusivity and the odd viscosity that we discussed above, with two species of charge. In particular, to the leading order, the equation of state is given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}p\approx\chi^{-1}n_{0}\mathrm{d}n+\chi_{s}^{-1}s_{0}\mathrm{d}s+\cdots,

(290)

where
p
is the thermodynamic pressure, and there exist no odd stress.

However, if we were allowed to include (
286
), it will actually lead to the desirable odd stress. To see it, we vary (
286
) with respect to
\pi_{i}
, which gives

\displaystyle\sigma_{ij}^{\mathrm{P-odd}}=\sum_{\alpha=n,s}\frac{1}{2}\beta_{0}^{-1}\rho K^{\alpha}\epsilon^{ij}\mu^{\alpha},

(291)

where
\sigma_{ij}^{\mathrm{P-odd}}
is the odd stress that is defined by
\rho\partial_{t}u_{i}+\partial_{j}\sigma_{ij}=\cdots
and antisymmetric.
To avoid that
\mu^{\alpha}\to 0
as we argued above, we can introduce an active torque
\tau
into the equations of motion for
\delta s
:
Banerjee et al. 2017

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\delta s\sim\tau-\Gamma^{s}s,

(292)

such that a spinning steady state with
s_{0}\equiv\tau/\Gamma^{s}
can exist. Now, the odd stress is given by

\displaystyle\sigma_{ij}^{\mathrm{P-odd}}=\frac{1}{2}\rho\chi_{s}^{-1}K^{s}\epsilon^{ij}s,

(293)

in agreement with
Banerjee et al. 2017
. Several remarks follow. First, the odd stress strength
K^{s}
is constrained by the positivity of the noise invariance:
(K^{s})^{2}\leq\eta\Gamma^{s}
. Second, the torque
\tau
is not an ingredient that can be captured in the EFT –
\mathcal{L}\sim\pi^{s}\tau
is not invariant under PT – but the resulting steady state with
s_{0}\neq 0
is a valid starting point for our EFT and we have seen that it leads to the odd stress. Let us turn to the relevant changes of the equations of motion due to this odd stress. We have, to the leading order,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\delta n+n_{0}\partial_{i}u_{i}

\displaystyle=0,

(294a)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\delta s+s_{0}\partial_{i}u_{i}+\frac{1}{2}\rho K^{s}\epsilon^{ij}\partial_{i}u_{j}

\displaystyle=-\Gamma^{s}\delta s,

(294b)

\displaystyle\rho\partial_{t}u_{i}+\chi^{-1}n_{0}\partial_{i}\delta n+\chi_{s}^{-1}s_{0}\partial_{i}\delta s+\frac{1}{2}\rho\chi_{s}^{-1}K^{s}\epsilon^{ij}\partial_{j}\delta s

\displaystyle=0.

(294c)

Solving the above equations to the linear order in
O(k)
, we find, schematically, a longitudinal sound mode
\omega\approx\pm v_{0}k
with
v_{0}=n_{0}/\sqrt{\chi\rho}
, a decaying spin density mode
\omega\approx-\mathrm{i}\Gamma^{s}
, and a transverse diffusion mode
\omega\sim-\mathrm{i}k^{2}
.

There is an intrinsic spin density
\delta s=\epsilon^{ij}\partial_{i}u_{j}
corresponding to the angular momentum of the fluid. It also contributes to the odd stress if the rotational symmetry is broken explicitly. Specifically, there exists a rotational viscosity
Cook and Lucas 2019
;
Cook and Lucas 2021
;
Rao and Bradlyn 2023

\displaystyle\eta^{\mathrm{even}}_{ijkl}\sim\eta_{\circ}\epsilon^{ij}\epsilon^{kl},

(295)

and plugging it in (
288c
), we obtain

\displaystyle\sigma_{ij}^{\mathrm{odd}}=-\eta_{\circ}\epsilon^{ij}\epsilon^{kl}\partial_{k}u_{l}.

(296)

Notice that this is typically a higher derivative contribution to the odd stress, and that this contribution is P-even and T-even separately.
16
16

16

The “odd” stress refers to the fact that
\sigma_{ij}^{\mathrm{odd}}\sim\epsilon^{ij}
, but the tensor structure of (
295
) renders it even under P.
Recently, this relaxation of intrinsic angular momentum has been argued to relax the momentum along the helical axis of a fluid with helical symmetry in thermal equilibrium
Farrell et al. 2022
.

7.3.3
Odd stress and PT-odd charge

Lastly, suppose that we add to our fluid a P-odd but T-even charge, which we will denote
w
. A possible candidate for this P-odd and T-even charge is to consider a fluid consisting of two kinds of chiral particles (which cannot be created or destroyed), with
n_{\mathrm{L,R}}
the densities of left- and right-handed particles respectively. Since parity will exchange left/right-handed particles, we would then obtain both a P-even and P-odd charge:
n=n_{\mathrm{L}}+n_{\mathrm{R}}
and
w=n_{\mathrm{L}}-n_{\mathrm{R}}
. Both of them, however, remain T-even.

We take similarly a minimal steady-state distribution

\displaystyle\Phi=\beta_{0}\int\mathrm{d}^{d}x~\left[\frac{\rho}{2}(u_{i})^{2}+\frac{1}{2\chi}(\delta n)^{2}+\frac{1}{2\chi_{w}}(\delta w)^{2}\right],

(297)

where
\chi_{w}
is the susceptibility associated with
w
. Then, the leading order Lagrangian that is invariant under PT symmetry is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=

\displaystyle\pi_{i}\partial_{t}u_{i}+\pi^{n}\partial_{t}\delta n+\pi^{w}\partial_{t}\delta w-n_{0}u_{i}\partial_{i}\pi^{n}-\frac{n_{0}}{\chi\rho}\partial_{i}\pi_{i}\delta n-w_{0}\epsilon^{ij}u_{i}\partial_{j}\pi^{w}+\frac{w_{0}}{\chi_{w}\rho}\epsilon^{ij}\partial_{i}\pi_{j}\delta w

\displaystyle+\mathrm{i}\beta_{0}^{-1}\rho^{-2}\eta_{ijkl}^{\mathrm{even}}\partial_{i}\pi_{j}\partial_{k}(\pi_{l}-\mathrm{i}\mu_{l})+\sum_{\alpha=n,w}\mathrm{i}\beta_{0}^{-1}\chi_{\alpha}D^{\alpha,\mathrm{even}}\partial_{i}\pi^{\alpha}\partial_{i}(\pi^{\alpha}-\mathrm{i}\mu^{\alpha})

\displaystyle+\beta_{0}^{-1}\rho^{-2}\eta^{\mathrm{odd}}_{ijkl}\partial_{i}\pi_{j}\partial_{k}\mu_{l}+\sum_{\alpha=n,w}\beta_{0}^{-1}\chi_{\alpha}D^{\alpha,\mathrm{odd}}\epsilon_{ij}\partial_{i}\pi^{\alpha}\partial_{j}\mu^{\alpha}

\displaystyle+\beta_{0}^{-1}D_{\mathrm{mix},\mathrm{odd}}\left(\partial_{i}\pi^{n}\partial_{i}\mu^{w}-\partial_{i}\pi^{w}\partial_{i}\mu^{n}\right),

(298)

where the subscript “odd” indicates the term is T-odd.
In the above equation, we did not include

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\sim\mathrm{i}\frac{1}{2}\beta_{0}^{-1}D_{\mathrm{mix},\mathrm{even}}\epsilon^{ij}\left(\partial_{i}\pi^{n}\partial_{j}(\pi^{w}-\mathrm{i}\mu^{w})+\partial_{i}(\pi^{n}-\mathrm{i}\mu^{n})\partial_{j}\pi^{w}\right),

(299)

which is a total derivative,
and the invariant terms

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\sim\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\beta_{0}^{-1}K^{n}\epsilon^{ij}(\partial_{i}\pi_{j}(\pi^{n}-\mathrm{i}\mu^{n})+\partial_{i}(\pi_{j}-\mathrm{i}\mu_{j})\pi^{n})+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\beta_{0}^{-1}K^{w}(\partial_{i}\pi_{i}(\pi^{w}-\mathrm{i}\mu^{w})+\partial_{i}(\pi_{i}-\mathrm{i}\mu_{i})\pi^{w})

(300)

for the same reason as in Sec.
7.3.2
. Hence, the stochastic equations of motion is

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\delta n+n_{0}\partial_{i}u_{i}-D^{n,\mathrm{even}}\partial_{i}^{2}\delta n-\chi_{s}^{-1}D^{\mathrm{mix},\mathrm{odd}}\partial_{i}^{2}\delta w

\displaystyle=\xi^{n},

(301a)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\delta w-w_{0}\epsilon^{ij}\partial_{i}u_{j}-D^{w,\mathrm{even}}\partial_{i}^{2}\delta w+\chi^{-1}D^{\mathrm{mix},\mathrm{odd}}\partial_{i}^{2}\delta n

\displaystyle=\xi^{w},

(301b)

\displaystyle\rho\partial_{t}u_{i}+\chi^{-1}n_{0}\partial_{i}\delta n+\chi_{w}^{-1}w_{0}\epsilon^{ij}\partial_{j}\delta w-\eta_{jikl}\partial_{j}\partial_{k}u_{l}

\displaystyle=\xi_{i},

(301c)

with the noise variance

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\xi^\alpha(\vb x, t)\xi^\alpha(\vb x^\prime, t^\prime)}

\displaystyle=2\beta_{0}^{-1}\chi_{\alpha}D^{\alpha,\mathrm{even}}\,\partial_{i}^{2}\delta(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\,\delta(t-t^{\prime}),

(302a)

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\xi_i(\vb x, t)\xi_j(\vb x^\prime, t^\prime)}

\displaystyle=2\beta_{0}^{-1}\eta_{ikjl}^{\mathrm{even}}\,\partial_{k}\partial_{l}\delta(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\,\delta(t-t^{\prime}),

(302b)

for
\alpha=n,w
. A crucial difference from Sec.
7.3.2
is that there exists an odd stress (pressure) obeying

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}p_{\mathrm{P-odd}}\approx\chi_{w}^{-1}w_{0}\mathrm{d}w.

(303)

The odd stress results in a transversal sound mode, which is absent in the normal hydrodynamics. To see it, we compute the quasinormal modes corresponding to (
301
) to the leading (ideal) order. This leads to

\displaystyle\omega=\pm\frac{n_{0}}{\sqrt{\chi\rho}}k,\quad\omega=\pm\frac{w_{0}}{\sqrt{\chi_{w}\rho}}k,

(304)

where the former is the usual longitudinal sound mode, and the latter is the transversal sound mode due to the odd stress. It would be interesting if this effect can be discovered in active chiral matter.

7.4
Fracton hydrodynamics

“Fracton hydrodynamics” is the name for the universality classes of hydrodynamics associated to theories with multipolar conservation laws (e.g. charge and dipole conservation together)
Gromov et al. 2020
;
Feldmeier et al. 2020
;
Morningstar et al. 2020
;
Hart et al. 2022
;
Glorioso et al. 2022
;
Grosvenor et al. 2021
;
Burchards et al. 2022
;
Guo et al. 2022
. These exotic conservation laws require a careful formulation of hydrodynamic EFTs
Guo et al. 2022
, including in nonthermal systems. As mentioned in Sec.
1.2
for example, certian terms naively allowed in equations of motion can be forbidden by an EFT. Using the weak noise limit formalism described in Section
2.7
,
Guo et al. 2022
has studied theories of fracton hydrodynamics without time-reversal symmetry. To quickly re-cap why this formalism is very helpful, notice that if

0=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\int\mathrm{d}^{d}x\;(a+b_{i}x_{i})n

(305)

for any
a
and
b_{i}
, and
n(x)
is a conserved charge density, we must write down a theory invariant under
\pi\rightarrow\pi+a+b_{i}x_{i}
. Hence, the lowest derivative motif of
\pi
that can show up in
\mathcal{L}
, outside of
\pi\partial_{t}n
, becomes
\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\pi
. So long as the time-reversed theory remains local, one can show that in generic dimensions
17
17

17

In one dimension only, one can also add a third-derivative term which contributes a total derivative after applying time-reversal.
, the most general Lagrangian at lowest order in derivatives is

\mathcal{L}=\pi\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{t}n+\mathrm{i}D_{ijkl}(n)\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{i}\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{j}\pi\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{k}\partial^{\vphantom{\prime}}_{l}\left(\pi-\mathrm{i}\mu\right).

(306)

If we take

\Phi\,=\,\int\mathrm{d}^{d}x\,\frac{n^{2}}{2\chi}\,,~~

(307)

for constant
\chi
, then Eq. (
306
) predicts subdiffusive with
\partial^{\,}_{t}n\sim\partial^{4}_{x}n
, in agreement with Ref.
14
;
21
, and resolving the puzzle of Sec.
1.2
.

Note that the above choice of
\Phi
(
307
) is only reasonable if
n<0
makes sense—e.g., there are both positive and negative charges in the theory. If we wish to consider only positive charges, and we wish to consider nonlinear dynamics close to
n=0
, an appropriate, alternative choice of
\Phi
is

\displaystyle\Phi=\int\mathrm{d}x~n\log n,

(308)

which is inspired by the formula for Shannon entropy. Keeping the Lagrangian (
306
), and working in
d=1
spatial dimensions for simplicity, a short calculation reveals the equation of motion

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\delta n+\partial_{x}^{2}\left(D(n)\partial_{x}^{2}\mu\right)=0.

(309)

Using (
308
) we find

\displaystyle\mu=\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial\delta n}=\log n-1.

(310)

In order for the equation of motion to be regular, we demand

D(n)=D_{0}n^{2}+\cdots

(311)

as
n\rightarrow 0
. The resulting nonlinear equation of motion reproduces the predictions of a recent paper
Han et al. 2023
in the absence of noise. This effective theory approach conveniently streamlines what is otherwise a somewhat complicated microscopic calculation of the equations of motion. Dynamics conserving higher multipole moments can be similarly derived following
Guo et al. 2022
.

One further generalization considered in
Han et al. 2023
, which again immediately follows from our effective field theory, is to consider quantum particles (bosons or fermions) subject to the same dipole-conserving dynamics. In our EFT, we can take the
same
Lagrangian (
306
), but simply modify
\Phi
to

\displaystyle\Phi_{s}=\int\mathrm{d}x\left(n\log n-s(1+sn)\log(1+s n)\right)

(312)

where we take
s=1
for bosons and
s=-1
for fermions.
Calculating

\displaystyle\mu_{s}=\frac{\partial\Phi_{s}}{\partial\delta n}=\log\frac{n}{1+sn}

(313)

along with
D_{s}(n)=Dn^{2}(1+sn)^{2}
, which is the minimal scaling in which the equation of motion is not singular, we find the same equations of motion as
Han et al. 2023
.

Although this theory is nonlinear, it is straightforward to convert the discussion above into the operator formalism, where we maintain an exact implementation of time-reversal symmetry using regularizations analogous to App.
D
.

8
Lattice model for active phase separation

In this final section, we describe a lattice discretization of active phase separation between two interacting species. Such models have been of recent interest
Saha et al. 2020
;
You et al. 2020
. Here, we show how to readily build such a model consistently coupled to noise—as usual, in our construction, we will have explicit control over the thermodynamic (time-reversal even part of the) phase diagram of the model. While we leave an exhaustive analysis of this model to future work, we include the construction here to emphasize the versatility of our methods.

Let us begin by overviewing how an active Cahn-Hilliard model for phase separation has been derived in the literature
Saha et al. 2020
. Let
\phi_{\alpha,i}
denote the concentration of species
\alpha\in\{1,2\}
at site
i
. We assume that the net concentration of each species is conserved in the dynamics as a whole. A nonactive dynamical system can be obtained on a discretized lattice by considering the noisy stochastic equation

\dot{\phi}_{\alpha,i}=-\Gamma\sum_{j}\Lambda_{ij}\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial\phi_{\alpha,j}}+\text{noise}

(314)

where, as usual in this paper,
\Phi
denotes the logarithm of the stationary distribution:
18
18

18

Note that the
\lambda
term is different here vs. e.g. Ref.
Saha et al. 2020
; however our construction is not too sensitive to the choice of
\Phi
.

\Phi=\beta\left[\sum_{\alpha=1}^{2}\left[\sum_{i\sim j}\frac{1}{2}(\phi_{\alpha,i}-\phi_{\alpha,j})^{2}+\sum_{i}\left(\frac{\phi_{\alpha,i}^{4}}{4}-\frac{\phi_{\alpha,i}^{2}}{2}\right)\right]+\lambda\sum_{i}(\phi_{1,i}+\phi_{2,i})^{2}\right],

(315)

where
i\sim j
denotes nearest-neighbor sites on a two-dimensional square lattice; the parameter
\lambda
denotes an interaction between the two species. Here we have also defined
\Lambda_{ij}
to be the graph Laplacian:

\Lambda_{ij}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-1&\ i,j\text{ adjacent}\\
4&\ i=j\end{array}\right..

(316)

The noise in (
314
) conserves the net concentration of each species, and will be explicitly defined in the Fokker-Planck formalism shortly. Note that
(\nabla^{2})_{ij}\approx-\Lambda_{ij}
.
If
\beta
is very large, we expect to see
\phi_{\alpha,i}\approx\pm 1
, so that the first term in
\Phi
is small. When
\lambda\gg 1
, we would anticipate phase separation, where
\phi_{1,i}\approx-\phi_{2,i}
. To add activity to this passive model, Ref.
Saha et al. 2020
added a nonreciprocal diffusion term to the equation of motion:

\dot{\phi}_{\alpha,i}=-\Gamma\sum_{j}\Lambda_{ij}\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial\phi_{\alpha,j}}+\kappa\sum_{\beta,j}\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}\Lambda_{ij}\phi_{\beta j}+\text{noise}.

(317)

It is deliberately the point of Ref.
Saha et al. 2020
that the
\kappa
-dependent term above does not obviously follow from considering derivatives of
\Phi
with respect to
\phi_{\alpha i}
– this term will thus break detailed balance. The perspective of our paper is that a minimal model for active phase separation can instead demand that
\Phi
remains the stationary distribution
even out of equilibrium
; therefore, we elect to incorporate the nonreciprocal diffusion (
\kappa
term) consistent with stationarity. We can achieve this as follows. With Gaussian noise and detailed balance, the passive model we would write down becomes a lattice discretization of the passive model of
Saha et al. 2020
:

W=\sum_{\alpha,i,j}\Gamma\Lambda_{ij}\partial_{\alpha,i}\left(\partial_{\alpha,j}+\mu_{\alpha,j}\right).

(318)

where
\Gamma
denotes the noise strength. To write down active terms consistent with this stationary distribution, we can invoke (
67
) and look for an antisymmetric matrix
V_{\alpha i,\beta j}
that leads to the desired terms. Our strategy will be to look for
V
that give us a desired “active term”, up to presumably irrelevant nonlinear corrections (with the caveat that we will not perform an exhaustive renormalization group analysis in this paper). The key observation is that even in equilibrium configurations where
\mu_{\alpha i}=0
, we can still find active terms in the equations of motion of the form
[\partial_{\beta j}V_{\alpha i,\beta j}]
, and so we will choose a
V
so that this divergence gives us a discretization of the terms of interest. Consider the following choice of
V
:

V_{\alpha i,\beta j}=-\kappa\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}\Lambda_{ij}\left(\frac{\phi_{\alpha i}^{2}+\phi_{\beta j}^{2}}{2}-1\right)

(319)

where
\epsilon_{12}=-\epsilon_{21}=1
is the two-dimensional Levi-Civita tensor.
This choice of
V
is chosen to be small near the global minima of
\Phi
(where
\phi^{2}=1
); hence, it will make the “unwanted” terms added to the equations of motion to enforce stationarity as “small” as possible. Explicit calculation gives us

\displaystyle\sum_{\beta,j}\frac{\partial V_{\alpha i,\beta j}}{\partial\phi_{\beta j}}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\beta,j}\kappa\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}\Lambda_{ij}\phi_{\beta j}

(320a)

\displaystyle\sum_{\beta,j}V_{\alpha i,\beta j}\mu_{\beta j}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\beta,j}\kappa\beta\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}\left(\frac{\phi_{\alpha i}^{2}+\phi_{\beta j}^{2}}{2}-1\right)\Lambda_{ij}\left[\sum_{k}\Lambda_{jk}\phi_{\beta k}-\phi_{\beta j}+\phi_{\beta j}^{3}+2\lambda(\phi_{1,j}+\phi_{2,j})\right]

(320b)

Notice that the continuum limit of the first line is precisely the active term considered in
Saha et al. 2020
, and which appeared in (
317
) –
\kappa\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}\nabla^{2}\phi_{\beta}
– while the second line is an irrelevant correction to the standard passive dynamics, if we expand around a static solution
\phi_{1i}=-\phi_{2i}=1
: the
V\mu
term is
quadratic
in fluctuations while the
\partial V
term is
linear
in fluctuations, with each term having at least two spatial derivatives in the continuum limit. It is thus reasonable to speculate that this lattice model ought to lie in the same universality class as the active scalar mixture model, in which we simply do not include the
V\mu
term in the equations of motion.

In our construction, we know precisely that the stationary distribution of the dynamics is independent of
\kappa
. Thus while
\kappa
may lead to nonreciprocal phases, as we saw in the Kuramoto model, these nonreciprocal phases will provably obey all the laws of equilibrium statistical mechanics – in particular, in one dimension, this model cannot exhibit any long-range order.

This construction is curious in that it uses precisely the regularization subtlety which App.
D
aims to avoid, in order to construct equations of motion with desired active terms (at leading order in a long wavelength expansion). This means that the construction of this section cannot be reproduced by any MSR field theory in the continuum in which time-reversal takes the simple form (
97
). We leave a more detailed investigation of such methods to future work
Lew-Smith et al. to appear
.

9
Outlook

In this paper, we have proposed an effective theory formalism for nonequilibrium systems that extends the approaches recently pioneered for thermal systems
Harder et al. 2015
;
Crossley et al. 2017
;
Glorioso et al. 2017
;
Haehl et al. 2016
;
Jensen et al. 2018
. Numerous examples and applications demonstrate the broad applicability of our approach, along with its relative ease of use; see Tab.
3
for a summary. Many of the theories described in this manuscript were first derived and studied elsewhere, but our effective theory approach provides an instructive derivation of these theories, and can provide broader context into how one particular model fits into a broader paradigm of nonequilibrium matter.

Our methods can elucidate subtleties that are easily overlooked when only considering equations of motion, such as the subtle nature of parity and generalized time-reversal symmetries (and their breaking) in recently developed theories of active odd elastic solids. In other cases, our methods give a clear framework within which existing models can be generalized: e.g., Kuramoto models of chiral rotors on higher-dimensional spheres.

Perhaps most importantly, our conceptual starting point for building models of active matter is not to begin by breaking detailed balance or a second law of thermodynamics. Instead, we begin by understanding the typical states in the stationary distribution (i.e., we deduce
\Phi
), and in turn, understand how it is possible for the system to “flow” around phase space. From our perspective, a second law can often emerge, albeit with an entropy that may differ from equilibrium analogues (see App.
B
).
